Chapter 1 : The Description of Somakanta

Om, salutations to Ganesha, the bestower of the knowledge of Brahman, whose name acts like the sage Agastya in drying up the ocean of obstacles.

The sages said: O Suta, O wise one, expert in the Vedas and Shastras! You are a treasure of all knowledge, and there is no other speaker like you. We have accumulated great merit over many lifetimes, and because of that, we have been blessed with the sight of you, who are all-knowing. We are the most fortunate in this world; our lives are well-lived, and our ancestors, the study of scriptures, our penances, and our hermitages have all found fulfillment. You have already narrated the eighteen Puranas to us in detail, but O best of the twice-born, we still have a desire to hear more. In this great twelve-year sacrifice of Shaunaka, there is no other source of rest for us than drinking the nectar of your stories.

Suta said: O noble ones, you have asked well, for your actions are virtuous. The minds of saintly and balanced people are always inclined toward the welfare of the world. O twice-born, I too find great satisfaction in telling these stories, and therefore, I shall narrate them especially for those of virtuous conduct. There are eighteen Upapuranas, such as the Ganesha Purana, the Naradiya Purana, the Narasimha Purana, and others. I shall begin by narrating the Ganesha Purana, which is rare to hear in the mortal world. A person becomes fulfilled merely by remembering it, and even Shesha and the four-faced Brahma are unable to describe its full glory.

Nevertheless, I shall speak of it briefly at your request. One gets to hear this only through the merit earned over many lifetimes; it is not for the hypocritical, the atheists, or the sinful. Because of His eternal, attributeless, and beginningless nature, the true form of Ganesha cannot be described by anyone, yet for the sake of those devoted to worship, His attributeless form is defined. He is the Lord in the form of Om, established at the beginning of the Vedas, whom sages and gods like Indra always meditate upon in their hearts. He is the one whom Brahma, Shiva, Indra, and Vishnu constantly worship; He is the cause of all worlds and the cause of all causes. By His command, Brahma creates, Vishnu sustains, Shiva destroys, and the Sun moves. By His command, the wind blows, the waters flow in all directions, the stars fall to the earth, and fire burns in the three worlds.

I shall now narrate His secret story, which has not been revealed to anyone else; listen with reverence, O twice-born. This was told by Brahma to the brilliant Vyasa, then by him to Bhrigu, and then by him to Somakanta. Only those who have performed millions of vows, sacrifices, penances, charities, and pilgrimages possess the intellect to hear this Ganesha Purana. O best of the twice-born, those who are not attached to the illusions of the world, their wives, children, or land, listen to the story of Mayuresha with reverence. Hear its glory through the context of Somakanta.

In the city of Devanagara in Saurashtra, there lived a king named Somakanta, who was a knower of the essence of the Vedas and Shastras and devoted to the principles of Dharma. He was followed by ten thousand elephants, twenty thousand horses, and six thousand charioteers. He had countless infantrymen armed with fire-weapons, archers, and swordsmen. In intelligence, he surpassed Brihaspati; in wealth, Kubera; in patience, the Earth; and in profundity, the great ocean. In brilliance and beauty, he surpassed the Sun and the Moon; in valor, fire; and in handsomeness, the God of Love. He had five powerful and brave ministers who were experts in the science of politics and conquerors of enemy nations. They were named Rupavan, Vidyadhisha, Kshemankara, Jnanagamya, and Subala. Through their own valor, they conquered various lands. They were all very handsome and adorned with various ornaments and garments. They were always engaged in royal duties and were very dear to the king.

The king had a wife named Sudharma, who was full of virtues. Seeing her beauty, even Rati, Rambha, and Tilottama felt ashamed and could not find any joy or pride in their own beauty. She wore golden earrings set with various gems, and necklaces of gold coins and pearls. She wore a gem-studded girdle on her waist, anklets on her feet, and excellent rings on her fingers and toes. She wore thousands of precious garments of many colors. She was devoted to the worship of God, charity, and the service of guests. She was always engaged in serving her husband day and night and following his words. They had a virtuous son named Hemakantha, who was as strong as ten thousand elephants, wise, brave, and a tormentor of enemies.

Thus was Somakanta, the best of kings on earth. Having brought all other kings under his control, he ruled the earth. He was always devoted to Dharma, a performer of sacrifices, a donor, and a renunciant.

Thus ends the first chapter of the Upasana Khanda of the Shri Ganesha Purana, describing Somakanta.

Chapter 2 : The Origin of Somakanta's Leprosy and His Decision to Go to the Forest

The sages said: O Sage, please tell us quickly, out of your kindness, the glory of the Shri Ganesha Purana in the context of Somakanta.

Suta said: O sages, listen to the misfortune of Somakanta. Suddenly, he was afflicted with a very painful, festering leprosy. Because of the fruition of past deeds, neither auspicious nor inauspicious actions ever leave a person, regardless of their righteousness. Whatever action is performed in a certain state, the living being must surely experience its result in that same state. He was drowned in an ocean of sorrow, like one without a boat in the sea, and suffered intense pain as if bitten by a serpent. Covered in various wounds, bleeding from everywhere, and filled with pus and blood, the king was tormented by worms. Reduced to mere bones, he was as if consumed by tuberculosis. Distressed by anxiety and suffering in all his senses, the king, after composing his mind with effort, spoke to his ministers.

The King said: Woe to my kingdom, my beauty, my strength, my life, and my wealth. By what seed of action has this calamity befallen me? I was called Somakanta because I surpassed the moon in radiance. I have protected the virtuous, the poor, the learned, and the hermitages as if they were my own children. I have defeated fierce enemies with my arrows and brought the entire earth under my control. I have worshipped the Lord, the Supreme Soul Sadashiva, properly. I have lived a life free from evil company, controlling my mind, and enjoying pleasant fragrances. Now, my body emits a foul odor, and my life is in vain. Therefore, with the permission of all, I shall go to the forest. Let everyone anoint my son Hemakantha, who is endowed with intelligence and valor, for the sake of the kingdom, and let him rule with his prowess. I will not show my face in the world in any way now. I have no need for kingdoms, wives, life, or wealth. O great ministers, I will perform my own welfare in the forest.

Suta said: Having said this, he fell to the ground like a tree struck by the wind. O best of Brahmins, he was covered in streams of pus and blood. There was a great uproar among the ministers and the women. For a moment, there was a terrible cry of lamentation from the people. With cloths, fanning, quick-acting medicines, and the application of mantras by the ministers, they brought him back to consciousness. When the king was restored, the ministers said this:

The ministers said: By your grace, we have enjoyed happiness equal to that of the king of gods, which is unattainable for ordinary people. How can we live without you? How can we survive, feeling like those who kill animals? Let your son alone rule the kingdom, for he is wealthy, powerful, and a slayer of enemies. O King, we shall leave all this primary happiness and go to the forest with you.

Suta said: Then Sudharma, the foremost of the virtuous, spoke to the king, saying that she would serve him in the forest, and that the kingdom should be left to her son. No one else is the enjoyer of another's sorrow or happiness resulting from past deeds; whatever fruit of action is attached to a being, that very thing must be experienced by the person themselves. I have also enjoyed this kingdom happily with you, who possessed various types of enjoyments. The scriptures of the sages declare that a wife should follow her husband even to the next world.

Then the humble and grief-stricken son, Hemakantha, spoke these words to Somakanta at that time.

Hemakantha said: I have no use for the kingdom, wives, life, or wealth. Without you, O best of kings, there is nothing to be done anywhere. Just as a lamp is useless without oil, or a body without life, so is the kingdom useless without you, O protector of Dharma.

Suta said: Hearing the nectar-like words of his son, the ministers, and Sudharma, the king was pleased and spoke according to Dharma.

The King said: A son is called a true son if he is devoted to his father's words, performs Shraddha with faith, and offers Pinda at Gaya. Therefore, endowed with policy, rule the kingdom by my command. With the help of the ministers, govern all the subjects like your own children. A son is called a true son if he knows the essence of the scriptures, is wise in policy, removes all faults, and delivers his ancestors. I shall go to the forest, afflicted by this despicable leprosy, along with my wife Sudharma. Grant me your permission, O virtuous one.

Thus ends the second chapter of the Upasana Khanda in the Shri Ganesha Purana.

Chapter 3 : Description of Conduct and Other Matters

Suta said:
Then, rising from his seat and taking his son by the hand, the King entered the upper chamber where he always held his consultations. In that room stood a divine golden throne, adorned with many gems, pearls, and corals, shining like the seat of Indra. The father and son sat there, both appearing radiant, as if they were the embodiment of all virtues combined. Filled with compassion, the King began to instruct his son on proper conduct and various aspects of statecraft for the sake of his own glory and that of his lineage.

Somakanta said:
When only one watch of the night remains, a person should wake up, leave the bed, sit in a clean place, and remember the Guru. After contemplating one's chosen deity, one should offer prayers and praise. Then, touching the earth, one should ask for forgiveness for stepping upon her, as she is the manifestation of the world.

(Verses 7-11 contain morning prayers to Lord Ganesha, Lord Vishnu, Lord Shiva, the Sun God, and Goddess Parvati, seeking their blessings and protection.)

After remembering these and other gods and sages, one should worship them mentally and seek their forgiveness. Then, carrying a vessel of water, one should go to the southwest direction of the village. A Brahmana should take white earth, a Kshatriya red, and Vaishyas and Shudras should take dark-colored earth. One should not dig near a riverbank, in fertile land, in an anthill, or near a Brahmana’s house. Covering the ground with grass, one should relieve oneself while facing north or south, depending on the time of day.

First, one should cleanse the anus with grass or wood, then wash it five times with earth and water. One should wash the left hand ten times and both hands seven times. The private parts should be washed once, and the left hand three times. For urination, one should wash both hands twice, and the feet once; this is the rule prescribed for a householder. One who has taken a vow should do this twice as much, and one living in the forest three times as much. A renunciant (Yati) should do it four times as much, and at night, one who is observing silence should do half of that. For women and Shudras, the purification process is half of that prescribed for others.

Then, after performing Achamana (ritual sipping of water), one should take a twig from a tree that produces milk or has thorns, and after offering a prayer, cleanse the teeth and tongue, asking the tree to grant strength, vitality, fame, brilliance, animals, intelligence, wealth, and wisdom. Then, one should take a bath in cold water to remove impurities.

Following this, one should perform the Sandhya prayers as prescribed in the Grihya Sutras, along with Japa (chanting), Homa (fire sacrifice), study of scriptures, Tarpana (libations), and worship of the gods. One should also perform Vaishvadeva, hospitality, and eat in the presence of a Brahmana. One should listen to the Puranas, give charity, and avoid backbiting.

One should perform acts of kindness through wealth, life, and words, but one should never perform acts of kindness for the sake of self-praise. One must avoid betraying the Guru, criticizing the Vedas, atheism, sinful acts, eating forbidden food, and approaching another man's wife. One should only approach one's own wife during the appropriate season. One should always serve one's parents, teachers, and cows. One should give food and clothing to the poor, the blind, and the destitute. Even at the cost of one's life, one should never abandon the truth.

One should protect those who are virtuous and have the grace of God. A king, skilled in statecraft, should consult the scriptures and learned men to determine the appropriate punishment according to the nature of the crime. One should never trust someone who is untrustworthy. A person seeking growth should not place absolute trust in someone who has been trusted before, and one should never trust an enemy who has been reconciled.

A king should increase his kingdom through the application of the six policies (Sama, Dana, Danda, Bheda, etc.). He should give charity according to his capacity, otherwise, he will face decline. Attacking when the enemy is distressed is considered the path of the lowly. A king should be one who uses spies, speaks through messengers, and is ready to wield the rod of punishment. It is only through the fear of punishment that people remain established in their respective duties; otherwise, there would be no order, and people would claim others' property as their own.

If a lowly person criticizes or praises, one should neither be angry nor pleased; what is the use of either? If a former enemy seeks refuge, he should be protected, and one who was previously wealthy should always be supported. Secrets must always be guarded, as they are the root of a kingdom. Having conquered the six internal enemies (lust, anger, greed, etc.), one should then conquer others. A noble king should never destroy livelihoods, populations, deities, gardens, or sacred monuments.

One should give charity on auspicious occasions for the sake of fame and detachment. One should never deceive a friend and should not share secrets with three types of people. One should rescue a Brahmana from debt and a cow from mud. One should never speak a lie, nor ever abandon the truth. One should win the hearts of ministers, subjects, and servants. One should always bow to Brahmanas and deities.

Suta said:
Having thus instructed his son Hemakantha in conduct, righteousness, and the science of statecraft, the King, seeing that his son was capable, prosperous, and learned, consulted the ministers and chose an auspicious time. The King gathered various materials from different places and invited Brahmanas well-versed in the Vedas and skilled in sacrificial rites. He also invited other kings, the queen, friends, guild leaders, and citizens to witness the coronation of his son, the destroyer of enemies.

After worshipping Lord Ganesha and his chosen deity according to the rites, having the auspicious prayers recited, performing the worship of the mothers, conducting the Abhyudayika Shraddha, and feeding the Brahmanas, the King had his son anointed amidst the chanting of mantras. He then spoke these words to the ministers:

The King said:
"Consider this my son as your own. This son is now placed in your hands. Just as you have followed my instructions, which were guided by the principles of statecraft, you should now guide him, along with the guild leaders."

Thus ends the third chapter of the Upasana Khanda in the Shri Ganesha Purana, titled "Description of Conduct and Other Matters."

Chapter 4 : The King Enters the Forest

Suta said: After the coronation, the King performed worship of the Brahmins. He gave away jewels, pearls, corals, and cows along with their calves. He satisfied everyone by distributing elephants, horses, wealth, and garments. He gave various types of clothing from the Kashmir region, adorned with gold, to the kings, queens, village chiefs, servants, and men of merit, according to their worth. He also bestowed other villages and much wealth upon his ministers.

Then, the King, filled with grief and sorrow, departed for the forest, feeling extremely impure and tainted by the sins accumulated in his previous births. When he departed, a great cry of lamentation arose among the people. Abandoning their own duties, they followed the King. The ministers, the queens, and his son Hemakantha followed him; some stumbled, some ran, and some wept as they went.

Although the ministers and citizens were distressed, the King stopped them. Having traveled about four miles, the King, feeling exhausted, stood still. Seeing a pond with cool water surrounded by various trees, he spoke to all the citizens, ministers, and his own kin. He said, "O people, I fold my hands and bow to you; please forgive any wrong I may have committed while ruling the kingdom for so long. If, by divine providence, my son returns, I request you all not to diminish your affection for me. May all of you, including the women and the elderly, return to the city. May you live there, ruled by my son, free from anxiety. Grant me your permission; I shall go to the forest with a calm mind. If you return, my mind will become steady. Please do me this great favor out of your kindness. Being distressed, I am unable to speak harshly, even though I wish to die. It is my own great sin, accumulated over many births, that has caused this separation from the kingdom and the people who are so beneficial to me. But what can I do? I am suffering from leprosy. Everyone reaps the fruits of their own actions, whether good or evil."

Suta said: Hearing the King’s words, his friends fainted. Some, overcome with deep grief, struck their heads with their palms. Some, who were wise and knowledgeable, consoled one another by recalling the lives of kings from the past. Others observed his indescribable state, much like how yogis perceive the nature of the self when knowledge is attained. Seeing the distressed Somakanta eager to go to the forest, some wise men restrained their grief and spoke with courage.

The people said: "After having nurtured and protected us, you should not abandon us. Just as water does not abandon its coolness, fire its heat, the ocean its boundaries, or the sun its brilliance, how can we return to the city without you, O lover of your people? O King, just as the sky does not look beautiful without the moon, even if it is filled with stars, the city will not look beautiful without you, O destroyer of enemies. We shall accompany you to the holy places for a few days. Your form will become radiant again through the grace of the holy places. Then, we shall all return to the city together, filled with great joy, accompanied by musicians and bards."

Suta said: Hearing these words, the King, filled with both anger and sorrow, bowed to them and said, "Do not say this, do not say this again." Then, he spoke to his son Hemakantha and his ministers with affection, compassion, and humility.

The son said: "Without you, I have no desire to go, to rule the kingdom, or even to live. I have never seen such separation before; how can I endure it?"

The King said: "For this very reason, I previously taught you the righteous scriptures and good policy; you should not make them vain. It is heard that in ancient times, the wise and righteous Parashurama killed his mother in obedience to his father’s command. Rama abandoned his kingdom and went to the forest with his younger brother, and without asking for a reason, he even left Sita in the forest. Hemakantha, go to the city quickly with the three ministers. By my command, go and rule the kingdom I have entrusted to you. Just as a person engaged in work keeps his mind on the Supreme, and just as the mind of the people remains on the wealth placed in their care, so too, as I go to the forest, my mind remains with you. If by divine grace I am fortunate, I shall return home. Your duty will be fulfilled by following my words, which is not the same as accompanying me. Therefore, go, and I shall depart."

Suta said: The ministers, the citizens, and the son, though determined to follow him, were filled with great difficulty and bowed to the King. Then, permitted by him and blessed by his words, they circumambulated the King and returned toward the city. Hemakantha, proud and accompanied by a great army of elephants, horses, chariots, and infantry, with umbrellas and banners, went to the city.

Thus ends the fourth chapter of the Upasana Khanda in the Sri Ganesha Purana, titled "The Departure of Somakanta to the Forest."

Chapter 5 : The Dialogue between Sudharma and Chyavana

Suta said: Then, approaching her mother with a mind distressed by affection, he said, "Mother, why are you abandoning one who is innocent?"

The son said: "You must tell Father that this son should go along with us. If, following your words, he takes me with him, then I shall serve you both; I have no desire for the kingdom. What joy would that kingdom give me if I am separated from you?"

Sudharma said: "O mighty-armed one, the King is so filled with grief and sorrow that he will not heed my words. Therefore, go by my command."

"O child, I am bound by the duty of a devoted wife. For women, there is no god other than their husband, and he alone is worthy of honor."

Suta said: Hearing this, the son bowed to his mother and his friends. After circumambulating her and receiving her permission, he went to the city.

The city was decorated by the citizens with flags, banners, and garlands; the paths were sprinkled with water and filled with fragrance, just like the city of Indra.

Having distributed betel leaves and garments and taken leave of his kinsmen, the King entered his palace, filled with both joy and sorrow.

He ruled the kingdom righteously, protecting his subjects like his own children, and applied his mind to the pursuit of Dharma, Artha, Kama, and Moksha according to his training.

The sages asked: "How did King Somakanta come to the forest? Who accompanied him, and what did he do? Tell us in detail."

Suta said: "O sinless ones, listen with respect as I tell you how Somakanta went to the forest and what he did there."

Accompanied by his two ministers, Subala and Jnanagamya, and his virtuous wife Sudharma, he entered the impenetrable forest.

The subjects walked in front, the King in the middle, and behind him walked his virtuous wife Sudharma, like Sita following Rama.

The four of them, sharing the same food, the same thoughts, and the same resting places, and equal in their joys and sorrows, moved from forest to forest.

Exhausted by hunger, thirst, and the fatigue of walking over uneven terrain, they sat down in the shade to rest.

Moving further into the forest, they saw a great lake where crocodiles and turtles shone like elephants.

The area was adorned with Tala, Tamala, Sarala, Priyala, Bakula, Jackfruit, Jambu, Nimba, Ashvattha, and Banyan trees.

The trees, surrounded by various creepers, shone all around, creating a deep darkness like that in the hollow of a mountain cave.

The wind there was pleasant to the touch and fragrant with the scent of lotuses and Kadamba flowers, from where the sages gathered flowers and fruits.

There, swans, cranes, hawks, beautiful crows, cuckoos, parrots, and Chakravaka birds sang in chorus.

O best of twice-born, just as in the heaven of the virtuous, there was no fear of hunger, thirst, or death, nor the scorching heat of the sun, as it was sheltered by various creepers and flowers.

Having reached there, they all drank the cool, fatigue-relieving water. After bathing and performing their daily rituals, they ate fruits.

The King slept for a moment on the soft sand of the bank, while his virtuous wife Sudharma sat by his feet, massaging them.

Knowing his permission, the two ministers went away to gather tubers, roots, fruits, and lotus stalks.

There, Sudharma saw a wonderful and fierce-looking boy, glowing with radiance. Because of his exquisite form, she thought he must be Kama (the God of Love) born as a boy.

Seeing him, Sudharma rejoiced and considered him a helper. Observing his calm heart despite his appearance, she spoke to him, acknowledging his kindness.

She asked him, "Who are you, where are you going, whose son are you, and who is your mother? Speak to me with a stream of nectar-like words, and quickly satisfy my ears like a friend."

Suta said: Being asked thus, the boy spoke to the princess with nectar-like words: "O beautiful lady, my father is Bhrigu, and my mother is Puloma. I have come here from my home in search of water."

"I am Chyavana by name, an obedient son of my father. But who are you, who is this man, and how did you come to this forest?"

"Why are your limbs dripping like a mountain in the rainy season? And tell me, by what deed does he have such a foul odor?"

"Why do you serve him, who is covered with a mass of worms, while you yourself are so beautiful, delicate, and lovely-eyed?"

"You are beautiful, with a pleasant face and graceful limbs. Did your father, friends, brothers, or the twice-born not know that this man is a leper, covered in worms? Why did you marry him and come to this difficult forest?"

Suta said: Being asked thus by the wise son of the sage, Sudharma, filled with both sorrow and joy, explained everything to him.

Sudharma said: "In the Saurashtra country, there is a famous and great city called Devata. There, this Somakanta, my husband, ruled the kingdom."

"He was proud, generous, heroic, of firm valor, possessed of countless troops, and a destroyer of enemy kingdoms."

"He was a performer of sacrifices, very handsome, glorious, a source of joy to his friends, a judge of all matters, and skilled in the science of politics."

"The King enjoyed his kingdom for a long time, O best of twice-born. But due to the ripening of past deeds, he has reached this state."

"Accompanied by two ministers, he came to this forest. They wander behind him, as he has given the kingdom to his son."

"We came here with the ministers Subala and Jnanagamya. Having received the King's permission, they have gone into the forest in search of fruits."

"Here, demons, ghosts, spirits, and various wild animals frighten us. I do not know why they do not devour us."

"I do not know why they keep us alive to suffer. I see no end to this suffering or to the evil deeds of the past."

"He did not find as much pleasure in bitter, pungent, sour, salty, sweet, and oily foods served by the twice-born as he now finds in tubers, roots, astringent and sour fruits, and the food of the poor, even when cooked."

"The wealthy do not have the same strength in their food and cooking. He who used to sleep on a soft, divine, and charming bed..."

"Now, look at the reversal of time, he sleeps anywhere. He, who was surrounded by various auspicious fragrances that spread in all directions..."

"Now, he is covered in pus and blood and has a foul odor. He, who was immersed in an ocean of joy and surrounded by scholars..."

"Now, he is surrounded by worms in an ocean of sorrow. I do not know, O son of Bhrigu, how we shall cross this ocean of sorrow."

"Be like a boat to one who is drowning in the deep, like a ship in the ocean."

Thus ends the fifth chapter of the Upasana Khanda in the Shri Ganesha Purana, titled "The Dialogue between Sudharma and Chyavana."

Chapter 6 : The Journey to Bhrigu’s Ashram

Suta said: Having heard these words, Chyavana, the son of Bhrigu, quickly took his water-filled vessel. Distressed by the suffering of others, he returned home in silence. Bhrigu asked his son, who had been delayed, why he looked so troubled.

Bhrigu asked: What unusual thing have you seen, that you appear so startled? Tell me, my son, why were you delayed?

The son replied: In the famous city of Daivata in the land of Saurashtra, there lived a lotus-eyed king named Somakanta. He ruled for a long time, protecting his subjects righteously. Due to fate, he fell into misfortune and, having installed his son on the throne, he left. Accompanied by his wife Sudharma, who always obeyed her husband, and guided by his ministers Subala and Jnanagamya, he arrived here. He is now suffering from leprosy, his body covered in worms, wandering toward the difficult lake, having attained the state of a thousand marks, just as Indra did due to the curse of Sage Gautama. While I was asking about his story—wondering where that beautiful Sudharma was and where her leprous husband was—a moment passed. My heart became troubled by their words of sorrow, so I filled my vessel and returned quickly.

The son told him everything that had been said. Having heard this, Bhrigu said to his son Chyavana: Go quickly, my son, and bring them all here by my command. I shall witness this wonder or show them my own power.

Suta said: Thus instructed by his father, the compassionate Chyavana went to the land near the lake, eager to see Sudharma. At that very moment, the ministers Subala and Jnanagamya, carrying fruits and roots, arrived before the king. The sage then said to the beautiful-eyed Sudharma: My father calls you all to his ashram, O virtuous one. Hearing these words, Sudharma, who was overwhelmed with grief, became alert, as if life had returned to a body. The virtuous queen, having drunk the nectar of his words, went along with her husband and the two ministers, led by the sage’s son, just as Shiva is accompanied by Ganesha and Skanda.

She shone on the way, led by the sage, and reached Bhrigu’s ashram, which was filled with the sound of mantras. It was covered with various flowering vines and echoed with the sounds of many birds. Cats, mongooses, hawks, elephants, cows, peacocks, snakes, birds, lions, and tigers played there together. The wind did not blow too hard, nor did the sun scorch intensely. The clouds did not rain excessively, but rained only when desired. Filled with joy, they entered, led by the sage’s son.

There, they saw the wonderful Bhrigu, who sat on a tiger skin, radiant as the sun. The king, his wife, and the two ministers bowed with folded hands and spoke.

The king said: Today my blessings have borne fruit, O best of sages; the religious acts and penances performed by the twice-born have become fruitful. I am purified from birth, and the lives of my parents and myself have become meaningful. Seeing one like you destroys sins, even those accumulated from previous lives. O great sage, you make the three times of birth pure and bring future welfare. I ruled in the city of Daivata in Saurashtra, O seer of truth. Fearing sin, I performed worship of the twice-born and the gods, O sage. Why did this sudden, terrible, and endless misfortune befall me? I have been reduced to such a wretched state, and I know no remedy. All efforts made have failed; I seek the remedy that you provide. In your ashram, even natural enemies become free of enmity; I have come to you for refuge.

Suta said: Hearing his words, Bhrigu, filled with compassion, looked at Somakanta through meditation and spoke.

Bhrigu said: I will tell you the remedy, O king; you should not worry. Those who come to my ashram do not suffer. I will also tell you about the sin committed in a previous life that brought you to this state. Let everyone eat, for they have been hungry for a long time, having traveled from forest to forest, exhausted and weary.

Suta said: Having said this, he had them bathed with oil and fed them various six-flavored foods. They ate and rested, and by the command of the great sage, they were well-adorned. They slept on soft beds, leaving behind their insurmountable worries, as if they had returned to their own kingdom by the sage’s grace.

Thus ends the sixth chapter of the Upasana Khanda of the Shri Ganesha Purana, titled The Journey to Bhrigu’s Ashram.

Chapter 7 : The Story of Somakanta’s Previous Birth

The sages asked: O best of the twice-born, what did King Somakanta do after going there? What remedy did the all-knowing Bhrigu suggest to him? Please narrate this story to us, for we are listening attentively. We never tire of drinking the nectar of your words.

Suta said: You have asked well, O noble ones, who are oceans of wisdom. Indeed, those who do not ask questions, or those who do not answer when asked—whether they are listeners or speakers—are like the deaf and dumb in this world. Listen, I shall now narrate the story of Somakanta.

When that night passed and the sun rose, the great sage Bhrigu performed his morning ablutions, prayers, and fire rituals. Having completed his rituals and being joined by his wife, he began to tell the King the story of his previous birth.

Bhrigu said: In the beautiful city of Kolhara, near the Vindhya mountains, there lived a wealthy merchant named Chidrupa. His wife was a virtuous and fortunate woman named Sulochana, who was devoted to her husband and charitable. In your previous birth, O best of kings, you were born as their son and named Kamanda, as suggested by the Brahmins. Your parents, having had you in their old age, showered you with immense affection and indulgence day and night.

When you came of age, they arranged your marriage with great celebration. Your wife, known as Kutumbini, was a beautiful and virtuous woman who was always devoted to you and respected the gods and guests. She was the jewel among women. She truly lived up to her name, as she bore you seven sons and five daughters.

After a long time, your father passed away, and your mother, being a devoted wife, followed him into the funeral pyre and ascended to heaven. After their death, you squandered all the wealth you inherited with your companions until it was completely destroyed. Your wife, worried about the future, often rebuked you, but you did not listen to her advice and eventually sold the house. Taking the children with her, she left you—the thorn of the family—and went to her father’s house to provide for them.

Left alone, you became reckless, like a drunkard or a madman. You became unjust, like a wild elephant in the city. You became a thief, a womanizer, a gambler, and a tormentor of the people. You were a coward who acted brave, and you lived by violence. You extorted wealth from those who were happy, stole from friends, and cheated the citizens. You swore false oaths and gave false testimony. People feared you as if you were a venomous serpent.

Unable to bear your cruelty, the citizens complained to the King, who banished you from the city. You went to the forest and lived by killing animals, women, children, and the elderly. You fled from crowds like a deer from a lion. You killed fish, birds, wolves, monkeys, and hares to feed your body with sin. You drove lions and tigers from their caves and gathered a band of fierce thieves. Using wood, mud, and stones, you built a large, magnificent house in the forest, decorated with various stolen goods.

Your wife, who had been taken away by your father, eventually returned to your house with your children. With the wealth you had stolen, you lived there with your wife, children, and thieves, appearing like a king surrounded by his court. You would go out and kill innocent people on the road to bring back loot.

Once, a learned Brahmin named Gunavardhana was passing through the forest at midday. You caught him by the hand. Trembling with fear and knowing your cruel nature, he thought his end had come. Desperate to save his life, he spoke to you with compassionate and logical words.

Gunavardhana said: Being wealthy and fortunate, why do you wish to kill me? I am a Brahmin, a newly married man, peaceful and innocent. Abandon these evil tendencies and turn your mind toward righteousness. I have just married a new wife, who is virtuous, noble, and a treasure of good qualities. To fulfill my debt to my ancestors and to continue the lineage, I have strived to lead a householder’s life. Without me, or without her, our lives would be in vain.

Be a father to me, and I shall be like a son to you. In the scriptures, the one who saves a life and protects from fear is called a father. Even robbers protect a Brahmin who seeks refuge. You should not harm me, for I am a Brahmin worthy of protection. If you do, you will suffer in hell for thousands of kalpas. Your wife, children, and friends will enjoy the fruits of your wealth, but they will not share in your sins. They will be happy while you suffer, and I cannot even fathom how many births you will have to endure for these sins.

Chapter 8 : The Removal of Various Birds

Bhrigu said: Hearing those words of yours, spoken repeatedly while you were suffering, my heart did not melt, even though you were filled with pity. Has Brahma created you harder than a diamond? By killing thousands of animals and humans, your mind has become as cruel as that of an ungrateful person. You spoke then as cruelly as the God of Death.

The thief said: O Brahmin, why do you waste your words on me? Wisdom bestowed upon one who will not listen is like water poured into an overturned pot. O fool, where is my intellect, and where is this advice of yours? Just as a bee does not care for the philosophy of truth, I do not care for it now. I am obsessed with wealth; I have no regard for my father or relatives. Just as one blinded by lust has no fear or shame, so it is with me. Can you see purity in a crow, truthfulness in a gambler, patience in a eunuch, or peace in a woman or a serpent? You have been sent to me by destiny while I am idle, and I will never let you go.

Bhrigu said: Having said this, he took a sharp sword in his right hand and severed the thief's head, just as a cat kills a mouse. In this way, the number of your sins—specifically the killing of Brahmins, women, children, the elderly, and animals—cannot be calculated. Because the count of your various sins became fragmented, after a long time had passed, O Kamanda, old age came upon you. Then came phlegm, fatigue, sweating, hiccups, and trembling. You felt lethargy while sitting, though not while sleeping. Your sons, servants, friends, and grandsons showed you no respect.

There was only one person seen there, a trusted confidant who kept your secrets, a Brahmin of unstoppable movement, who was sent by you to summon all those sages living in the forest. Because of their fear of you and the Brahmin's words, they came. You bowed to them and said, "Please accept gifts from me." They replied, "We will not accept gifts from one as fallen as you. Sin spreads through sacrificing, teaching, sexual relations, social connections, and even by eating or traveling together." Saying this, they returned to their own ashrams, bathed with their clothes on, and recited the Pavamani mantras.

Then, O Kamanda, deep remorse arose in your heart due to your illness, the abandonment by your kin, and the rejection by the Brahmins. Your mind became focused on restoring dilapidated temples, seeing your vast wealth consisting of gold, gems, and other treasures. It was told to you by the Brahmins that a primeval and auspicious image of Ganesha was situated in a small, dilapidated temple in the forest. You then built a vast, long temple with four gateways, four doors, and four beautiful spires. It was adorned with various pillars, surrounded by different altars, inlaid with pearls, coral, and gems, and had a beautiful courtyard. It was filled with various flowering trees and fruit-bearing trees, and graced with beautiful water tanks in all four directions.

The wealth you spent on building this temple was exhausted; some was taken by your wife, sons, friends, and relatives. Not long after, you died. You were bound and severely beaten by the messengers of Yama with whips. Your whole body was pierced by thorns, you were crushed against rocks, and you were submerged in a terrible hell of pus and blood. In this way, you were led by those messengers before Chitragupta and Yama. Yama asked, "Will you experience the fruits of your merits or your sins first?" You said, "O Son of the Sun, I will experience my merits first." Consequently, you were born as a king in the Saurashtra country. I have told you about the sins of your previous life out of compassion for one who has sought refuge and by the power of my penance. Because you built a beautiful temple, you became King Somakanta, and you shine with beauty like the moon, accompanied by your beautiful queen.

Suta said: Hearing what Bhrigu said, King Somakanta, the lowest of kings, became filled with doubt and stood motionless like a stone. He harbored doubts about the words of Bhrigu, who knew the meaning of the Vedas and Shastras, who knew the past, present, and future, and who was a great ascetic. In an instant, many birds of various colors and shapes emerged from his body and began to devour the king. Flying repeatedly, they bit the king with their sharp beaks. They tore off his flesh and ate it in the presence of the sage.

Then, with a body full of extreme pain, he again sought refuge and spoke with a pitiful voice to Bhrigu, the treasure of knowledge and penance. The King said: In your forest, there is no mutual fear among those born as enemies. How is it that they are killing me, who am already dead, in your presence? I am devoted to your feet, miserable, suffering from leprosy, and have sought your refuge. O Sage, who gives fearlessness to all beings, please release me now.

Suta said: Being told this, Bhrigu, who is compassionate to the miserable, spoke to him again: "O King, you have experienced this because of your doubt in my words. I will tell you the remedy; be at ease for a moment, O King. By my mere utterance of 'Hum,' these birds will depart." Suta said: Hearing the Brahmin's sound, the birds vanished instantly. The King, along with his wife and ministers, was pleased.

Thus ends the eighth chapter of the Upasana Khanda of the Shri Ganesha Purana, titled "The Removal of Various Birds."

Chapter 9 : The King's Instruction

Suta said: Then, Bhrigu meditated for a moment and spoke again to the king, who was extremely distressed by the sight of the consequences of his past actions.

Bhrigu said: Where are those heaps of sins, and where are the remedies I have suggested? Still, I will tell you one remedy that destroys all sins. If you listen to the Ganesha Purana quickly, you will surely be liberated from this ocean of sorrow; there is no doubt about this. Having said this, he recited the excellent one hundred and eight names of Ganesha, chanted a mantra over some water, and sprinkled it upon the king.

As soon as the water was sprinkled, a small, black-faced figure emerged from the king's nostrils and grew instantly upon the ground. It became seven talas tall, with a gaping mouth, terrifying, with protruding fangs, red eyes, long arms, and matted hair. It vomited fire and blood from its mouth, blinding the eyes of those present like a second darkness. Seeing this, all the residents of the hermitage fled, while the strange being filled the ten directions with the sound of its gnashing teeth.

The great sage asked the being standing before him, "Who are you? Tell me your name, even though you are knowledgeable." The being, thus questioned by the sage, replied, "I am the 'Sin-Person' (Papa-Purusha), residing in the bodies of all living beings. By the power of your mantra-water, I have been expelled from the king's body. I am hungry and seek food; if you do not give me any, O sage Somakanta, I will devour the people before you. Tell me a beautiful place to dwell, now that I have been cast out."

Then, stepping outside, the sage said to him again, "Dwell in the hollow of this dry, withered mango tree. Eat the fallen leaves by my command; otherwise, I shall reduce you to ashes. Do not take my words as false, you wretch."

Suta said: Upon the sage's words, the being touched the dry tree, and it was reduced to ashes instantly. Fearing the sage's gaze, the being merged into those ashes. Once it had vanished, the sage spoke again to Somakanta.

Bhrigu said: O best of kings, the merit you have gained from listening to the Purana will remain as long as this burnt tree does not rise again. Every day, perform your duties in those ashes, and as the tree grows, you will become sinless, O King.

The King asked: O Brahmin, where can the Ganesha Purana be found, which I have neither seen nor heard, and who is its narrator?

The Sage replied: It was told long ago by Brahma to the wise Veda Vyasa, and from Vyasa, I learned this sin-destroying Purana. I will tell it to you; perform your ablutions in the holy river and make a vow to listen to the Purana, O virtuous one.

Suta said: Urged by Bhrigu, Somakanta bathed in the famous Bhrigu-tirtha and joyfully made the vow. "From today, I shall listen to the Ganesha Purana." As soon as the vow was made, the king became healthy. Freed from the worms and sores by Bhrigu's grace, the king, astonished and happy, followed the sage. Bhrigu offered him a seat, and the king, now radiating divine splendor, sat down and spoke.

The King said: By your grace, all my great suffering has vanished with just a vow. Please tell me the entire wonderful Purana of the Elephant-faced Lord.

Bhrigu said: Listen attentively, for I shall tell you that Purana, the hearing of which generates a store of infinite merit in the minds of men, and not otherwise for those who commit sins. By merely hearing it, even the great sins accumulated over seven births, whether small, dry, or massive, vanish instantly by the grace of Ganesha.

He is the imperishable, the immeasurable, the attributeless, the formless, the one who cannot be described by mind or speech, and the embodiment of pure bliss. His true nature is unknown even to Brahma, Shiva, and the other gods; even the thousand-headed Shesha is unable to describe his glory. O best of kings, I will tell you that highly meritorious Purana as I heard it long ago from the radiant Vyasa, who possessed transcendental knowledge. Daksha, distressed by the destruction of his sacrifice, heard it from Mudgala.

O King, one who has firm devotion to Ganesha, the giver of all success, should listen to this daily; it should not be told to others. If everyone were to serve the Lord of Obstacles, where would the heaps of obstacles go to roam freely? Who would then experience sorrows born of various separations? Vyasa, who knew the past, present, and future, composed this long ago. Seeing people in the Kali Yuga who were devoid of the knowledge of the Vedas, who neglected Vedic study, who lacked the conduct of their caste and stage of life, and who caused the mixing of castes, he created the eighteen Puranas for the protection of Dharma. There are as many Upapuranas, from which people understood the meaning, and through them, these people come to know the form of Ganesha.

Thus ends the ninth chapter of the Upasana Khanda in the glorious Ganesha Purana, titled "The Instruction to the King."

Chapter 10 : The Description of Vyasa’s Questions

Bhrigu said: The great sage, the son of Parashara (Vyasa), who was an incarnation of a portion of Narayana, possessed knowledge of the past and future and understood the essence of the Vedas and Shastras. In order to make the meaning of the Vedas accessible, he divided them into four parts. However, filled with the pride of his own learning, he began to compose the Puranas. He did not perform any auspicious rituals to ensure the completion of his work, nor did he offer salutations or praise to Ganesha at any point. Consequently, he became overwhelmed by obstacles and could not recall any of the meanings he intended to write. He fell into a state of confusion regarding both worldly and spiritual paths. Despite being omniscient, he found himself unable to explain the Vedic scriptures, or perform daily, occasional, or desire-driven rituals, as well as those prescribed by the Shruti and Smriti. Like a king of snakes whose power has been drained by herbs and mantras, he became completely paralyzed within himself and could not understand the cause.

Overwhelmed with wonder and shame, the sage, the son of Parashara, went to Satyaloka to ask the Creator. After bowing to the assembly of gods, the divine sages, and the lotus-seated Brahma, he was honored by Brahma and sat on a beautiful seat. The great sage touched Brahma’s feet with his hands, and bowing with devotion, he began to ask.

Vyasa said: O Brahma, this is truly wonderful. What has happened to me by fate? My intellect was set on composing the Puranas to explain the meaning of the Vedas. Seeing all the people of the Kali Yuga devoid of knowledge and conduct, dull in their duties, stubborn, atheistic, and critical of the Vedas, I intended that they would learn the rules and prohibitions through my words. Yet, my own knowledge has vanished, and I have become like one who is confused or intoxicated. I see no reason for this, and no inspiration arises within me. I have come to you to ask for the cause of this and the source of inspiration. To whom else can I turn for refuge, O Four-Faced One? You are the omniscient creator of all; please remove my confusion. Tell me the cause of my delusion, for even though I am devoted to daily rituals and am considered omniscient, I am in this state.

Suta said: Having heard these words and reflected upon them, the lotus-seated Brahma spoke to the bowing sage, smiling as if in wonder.

Brahma said: Alas, I will tell you the subtle nature of actions. One must carefully consider what is right and what is not. If a person acts otherwise, the result will be contrary to their intentions. A wise person achieves their goals through intelligence, logic, and simplicity, not through pride or jealousy. The lord of birds (Garuda) became a mere vehicle due to pride. Everything was destroyed by the son of Ambika (Kartikeya) due to jealousy, and in ancient times, the Kshatriya race was eradicated by Rama due to jealousy.

The One who is without beginning or end, the Lord, the Creator of the world, the essence of the world, the supporter of the world, the sustainer of the world, and the eternal, manifest, and unmanifest reality—He who is capable of creating, uncreating, and doing everything at all times, whose commands are always followed by the gods led by Indra, including myself, Vishnu, Rudra, the Sun, Fire, and Varuna—He is the remover of obstacles for His devotees and the creator of obstacles for others. You became proud due to the strength of your own learning, and in your pride of omniscience, you did not worship Him. You did not remember Ganesha at the beginning of your work or elsewhere, O sinless Vyasa, and that is why you are in this state of confusion.

He who is remembered at the beginning of all works, at the time of entering or leaving, in rituals of the Shruti, Smriti, or worldly life, removes all obstacles. He whom the seers of the Vedas and Shastras call the Supreme Bliss, the Supreme Goal, and the Supreme Brahman—go to Him for refuge with devotion, O child, the One with the elephant face. If that Lord is pleased, He will fulfill your desires; otherwise, even in a thousand years, you will not attain your goal.

Vyasa said: Who is this Ganesha? What is His form, and how is it to be known? To whom was He pleased in the past, O Four-Faced One? How many incarnations has He had, and what work has He performed? By whom was He worshipped in the past, and when was He remembered? O ocean of compassion, my mind is distracted, and as I ask you, please explain all of this to me in great detail.

Chapter 11 : The Recitation of the Mantra

Bhrigu said: Then, wishing to answer the questions asked, the four-faced Brahma spoke to him again. I shall now tell you everything in order, having considered the Ganesha mantras in many ways.

Brahma said: O Sage, the mantras of the great-souled Ganesha are indeed infinite. I will tell you about the worship that quickly grants success. There are seventy million great mantras of Ganesha existing in the Agamas. Lord Shiva knows their secret, and I know a little of them, O Sage. Among them, the six-syllable and the single-syllable mantras are the most excellent, by the mere remembrance of which all success comes into one's hand. Those who worship Him become liberated while still living, O Sage; they are blessed, worthy of worship, and revered even by the gods. Through His worship, the very powers (siddhis) serve the devotee; they become omniscient, capable of moving at will, and assume various forms. Those who perform devotion to Ganesha with a heart full of feeling are blessed; for those who lack even a trace of such devotion, their birth is meaningless. One should not even look at the faces of those who are averse to the Elephant-faced Lord; the mere sight of them causes obstacles at every step. Conversely, obstacles subside upon seeing His devotee; even the stationary and moving beings bow down to them. Therefore, I shall tell you the auspicious single-syllable mantra; by merely practicing it, you will attain your desired goal.

I will tell you the procedure of the practice as described to me by Shambhu. After bathing and wearing clean clothes, a pure man should sit on a seat made of grass or animal skin. Seated there, one should perform bhuta-shuddhi (purification of elements) and the installation of the vital breaths (prana-pratishtha). Having performed the nyasa of the matrikas (divine letters) internally and externally without lethargy, one should perform pranayama and then chant the root mantra in the heart. One should perform the mantra-sandhya as prescribed in the Agamas. Meditating on the Lord from head to toe with a steady mind, one should worship Him with mental offerings with great focus. Then, one should chant according to one's capacity following the path of purashcharana (preliminary ritual). One should remain devoted to the chant until the Elephant-faced Lord becomes favorable, grants a boon, and reveals His true form.

Bhrigu said: Having said this, Brahma looked at the auspicious day and taught the excellent single-syllable mantra, the king of mantras of Ganesha, to the best of sages, who was then bewildered.

Brahma said: When you see that Lord who shines like ten million suns and has come to grant a boon, steady your mind. Say, "O Elephant-faced Lord, remain forever steady in my heart." Ask for a boon, and He will undoubtedly grant it to you. With the Lord residing in your heart, you will attain divine knowledge. You will know everything—past, present, and future. Abandoning all confusion, my son, you will compose various scriptures.

Vyasa said: My confusion has vanished due to your instruction, O Father. I will perform the practice according to your command, O Grandfather.

Brahma said: Perform the practice in a secluded, lonely place, free from causes of distraction, while remembering the Elephant-faced Lord. This king of mantras should not be spoken to an atheist, a critic, a cruel person, one who lacks conduct, a wicked person, or a rogue, even if they seek refuge. Reveal it only to one who has firm devotion, faith, humility, knowledge of the Vedas, desire for truth, compassion, and knowledge of the scriptures. If this king of mantras is revealed to those who should not hear it, it leads the speaker to hell, along with ten generations of ancestors and ten generations of descendants. Whoever chants it with devotion attains the desired fruit, blessed with sons, grandsons, wealth, and grain. By the power of the Single-Tusked Lord, having attained pure knowledge and enjoyed all pleasures here, one shall attain liberation in the end.

Thus ends the eleventh chapter named "The Recitation of the Mantra" in the Upasana Khanda of the Shri Ganesha Purana.

Chapter 12 : The Vision of Gajanana

Suta said: Having heard these words emanating from the mouth of Brahma, the sage, filled with great joy, asked him again.

Vyasa said: O Father, having drunk the nectar of your speech, I have attained full attention. Now, I wish to hear about this king of mantras. Who chanted it, and how did he attain perfection from Gajanana? Remove this doubt of mine, for there is no guru for me other than you.

Bhrigu said: Asked thus by the sage, Brahma, the best of speakers, spoke with compassion to Vyasa, the best of kings.

Brahma said: Well done, well done! You have asked well, and you are truly fortunate. Indeed, those who lack merit have no interest in hearing such stories. I shall teach you this path of worship properly, for there is nothing to be hidden from a devoted and intelligent disciple. The Lord, the leader of the ganas, is the embodiment of Om. Just as this Vinayaka is worshipped in all tasks, if he is not, he creates obstacles. Therefore, mantras containing the seed of Om and adorned with the sprout of Om are proclaimed in all scriptures; others are fruitless. The leader of the ganas is everything—the manifest and the unmanifest, the existent and the non-existent.

Thus, all gods, perfected beings, sages, demons, kinnaras, gandharvas, charanas, nagas, yakshas, guhyakas, and humans, as well as all beings, moving and stationary, are worshippers of Ganesha. Therefore, there is nothing higher than Ganesha. Now, I will tell you an ancient story of how Gajanana was pleased by the chanting of this king of mantras.

Once, by the decree of fate, when the dissolution arrived, mountains were shattered by winds and fell in all directions. The twelve suns scorched the earth, drying up the great waters. A massive fire, garlanded with flames, burned everything. The great clouds of dissolution rained water all around. O best of twice-born, with streams like the trunks of elephants, the oceans and rivers crossed their boundaries. Thus, everything from Brahma to the stationary objects perished. When the world was destroyed in this illusory state, Gajanana, having assumed a form smaller than the smallest, remained somewhere.

After a long time had passed, shrouded in deep darkness, the single-syllable Brahman, endowed with the primordial sound (nada), was born. When that form, consisting of sound, remained, it attained the state of Ganesha through the influence of Maya. From that, the three qualities—Sattva, Rajas, and Tamas—arose. From them, the three—Vishnu, Brahma, and Hara—were born. The entire universe, moving and stationary, was created by my Maya.

Then, bewildered by that Maya, the three gods wandered, eager to see and ask their creator. O sage, curious about what work they were to perform, they looked upwards through the twenty-one heavens. They went through the atmosphere and then to the netherworlds. Not seeing the Supreme Soul, they performed intense penance. Fasting and devoted to chanting for a thousand divine years, they became weary and distressed. Then, they returned to the earth. Searching, they went to forests, groves, rivers, oceans, mountains, peaks, and caves.

Then, the gods saw a great reservoir of water, filled with various aquatic creatures, trees, and birds. It was resonant with the sounds of cranes, ruddy geese, swans, and ducks, all busy eating lotus stalks. Having bathed and rested on its bank, they crossed it and moved forward. They saw that great reservoir, filled with various waves, difficult for men to cross, and teeming with fish and crocodiles. They gazed upon it, seeing it as difficult to behold, resembling the fire of dissolution.

O sage, they saw a mass of light before them, shining like a crore of suns. At the end, their vision was stolen by the brilliance, and they were filled with anxiety. Then, they emerged from the middle of that light through the path of the sky. Exhausted by hunger and thirst, sighing repeatedly, they blamed and cursed themselves, filled with fear. Then, the Lord of the worlds, the knower of all, filled with great compassion, showed them his form, which delights the mind and eyes.

His lotus-like feet were adorned with nails that surpassed the filaments of a red lotus. The radiance of his red garments surpassed the circle of the evening sun. The brilliance of his waist-belt surpassed the peaks of the golden mountain. He was adorned with a sword, a shield, a bow, and a mace, and possessed four beautiful arms. His lotus-face, with a beautiful nose, surpassed the beauty of the full moon. He was radiant day and night, with beautiful lotus-like eyes. His head shone with a crown that surpassed the brilliance of many suns. His upper garment surpassed the beauty of the sky adorned with various stars. He was resplendent with a single tusk that surpassed the beauty of a boar's tusk. He was the great lotus, terrifying to the guardians of the directions like Airavata. Seeing the Lord suddenly, the gods bowed down with joy, O sage. Touching his lotus feet, they began to praise him.

Chapter 13 : The Description of the Hymn to Brahma

Vyasa said: How did the five-faced (Shiva), the four-faced (Brahma), and the thousand-headed (Shesha) praise the elephant-faced Lord who grants boons?

Brahma said: By the grace of the sidelong glance of the Lord of Obstacles, who is ever ready to bestow favor, those who had attained the grace of wisdom then praised Him.

Brahma, Vishnu, and Maheshwara said: We worship Ganesha, the form of the Supreme Brahman, who is birthless, without attributes, without form, the One, without duality, full of bliss, supreme, devoid of qualities, without distinction, and without desire. We worship Ganesha, the form of the Supreme Brahman, who is beyond qualities, the primordial one, the embodiment of consciousness and bliss, the reflection of consciousness, all-pervading, attainable through knowledge, meditated upon by sages, formless like the sky, and the Supreme Lord. We worship Ganesha, the form of the Supreme Brahman, who is the cause of the universe, the form of causal knowledge, the beginning of the gods, the beginning of happiness, the beginning of the ages, the all-pervading, the one worshipped by the world, and the Lord of gods. We bow to Ganesha, the form of the Supreme Brahman, who, through the connection with Rajas, is the form of Brahma, the knower of the Vedas, always engaged in creation, and the object of contemplation in the heart, the creator of the universe, and the repository of all knowledge. We bow to Ganesha, who is always the form of Vishnu, who is ever connected with Sattva, playing with joy, destroying the enemies of the gods, protecting the universe, taking many incarnations, and removing the ignorance of His devotees. We bow to Ganesha, who is always the form of Shiva, who is connected with Tamas, the form of Rudra, the three-eyed one, the supporter of the universe, the savior, the cause of knowledge, and the one who instructs His people through many scriptures. We bow to Ganesha, who is always the form of Brahman, the destroyer of the mass of darkness, the remover of the ignorance of people, the essence of the three Vedas, the beyond of the Supreme Brahman, the cause of the knowledge of sages, and far removed from change. We bow to Ganesha, who is the form of the Moon, who satisfies the medicinal herbs with His rays, the hosts of gods with His nectar-dripping digits, the remover of the heat of the sun’s rays, and the Lord of the twice-born (Brahmins/Moon). We bow to Ganesha, who is always the form of Shakti, the form of light, the form of sky and air, the cause of change, the one who becomes time and arts, and the form of many actions and many powers. We bow to Ganesha, who is always the form of the universe, the primordial nature, the form of the great principle (Mahat), the form of earth and water, the form of the lords of the directions, the form of both the unreal and the real, and the cause of the universe. If a person places their mind on Your pair of feet, they will not suffer from the multitude of obstacles; just as one who stands in the vast, shining solar disc, how could they suffer from the pain of darkness? We, who were completely deluded by the connection with ignorance, have now attained Your feet after many years; O Lord of the earth, protect us who have surrendered to You, now that we have attained Your grace.

Brahma said: O great sage, Ganesha, being thus praised, was satisfied and, filled with supreme grace, began to speak to them.

Shri Ganesha said: I am pleased with this hymn for which you were distressed and for which you have come here; ask for a boon from Me. This hymn, which has been composed by you with focused minds, shall be known as the "King of Hymns" by My command. Whoever, being wise and pure, recites this in the morning, at the three junctures of the day, with devotion, shall attain sons, wealth, and all desires, and at the time of death, shall become the form of the Supreme Brahman.

Brahma said: Hearing these words, they were satisfied and spoke to Him. By His gaze, the three qualities of Rajas, Sattva, and Tamas arose.

The three said: O Lord of gods, creator and destroyer, if You are pleased, may we have unwavering devotion to Your lotus feet. Furthermore, command us as to what we should do; this is the only boon we desire, O Elephant-faced One.

Hearing their words, the Elephant-faced One spoke again: Your devotion to Me shall indeed be firm, O fortunate ones. So that you may overcome even great difficulties, I will tell you your tasks separately for your fame. O Brahma, you, born of the Rajas quality, be the creator. O Vishnu, you, being all-pervading and relying on Sattva, perform the protection. O Shiva, you, born of the Tamas quality, destroy everything.

Brahma said: The Lord Ganesha then respectfully gave the Vedas, scriptures, Puranas, and the power of creation to Brahma. To Vishnu, the Lord gave the ability to take any form at will through Yoga. To Shiva, the Lord gave the single-syllable and six-syllable mantras, all the scriptures, and the power of destruction. Then, I, with a humble mind, spoke with folded hands to the Lord of the three worlds, the teacher of the world, the Elephant-faced One who grants boons.

Brahma said: Just as one who has received power does not distinguish between what should be said and what should not, similarly, seeing the many kinds of creation, how could I dare to differentiate the command of the Lord? How did I come to think, "This is a well, that is a pond"?

Then the Elephant-faced One, having given divine vision to Brahma, who was thus confused and knew the Vedas and scriptures, spoke.

Ganesha said: O Lotus-born, look at the countless universes revolving within and outside My body even now.

Brahma said: Then, I was taken by the breath of Ganesha into His stomach. There, the creator saw countless universes, like figs on a tree, and within them, beings like mosquitoes. I pierced one of them with My supreme radiance and saw the entire creation within it again and again. I saw another Brahma, Vishnu, Indra, Prajapati, Shankara, the Sun, the Wind, forests, rivers, and oceans. I saw Yakshas, Gandharvas, Apsaras, Kinnaras, serpents, sages, virtuous people, Sadhyas, humans, mountains, and trees. I saw beings born from the earth, from the womb, from sweat, and from eggs. I saw the earth, the seven netherworlds, and the twenty-one heavens. I saw the entire universe, both manifest and unmanifest, moving and stationary. Whatever universe I pierced, I saw all this within each one. Seeing this illusion, I reached the same state as before and could not find their end in any way. I, the lotus-seated one, could neither stand nor move. Then, O Brahma, I sat down and praised the Elephant-faced One.

Brahma said: I bow to the Lord, the God of gods, Ganesha, in whose body there is no counting of the universes. Who can count the clouds in the sky, the fish in the ocean, or the grains of sand on the shore? O Lord worshipped by the king of gods, I have no shame in seeing Your lotus feet; I became deluded when You, the treasure of knowledge, were pleased; even liberation is trivial, what to speak of other matters? O Lord of gods, I have seen the heap of universes, filled with various things, in Your belly. I am unable to stand or go outside; I do not seek any other refuge but You.

Then the infinite Lord Ganesha, being pleased, brought me out, as I was distressed in mind. The Lord then brought out Hari, who followed me, and the Tamasic Shiva, along with Vishnu, through the path of the nostrils. Both Hari and Hara lay happily on His body.

Thus ends the thirteenth chapter of the Upasana Khanda of the Shri Ganesha Purana, titled "The Description of the Hymn to Brahma."

Chapter 14 : The Description of Brahma’s Contemplation

The King said: What did Brahma do after seeing thousands of universes? And how did he perform creation after receiving the command from the Elephant-faced Lord (Ganesha)?

Bhrigu said: Brahma became filled with pride and thought to himself, "I know the scriptures, the Vedas, the Puranas, and the Agamas. I am endowed with knowledge and wisdom, and I possess the power to curse and to bless. I have seen the universes and the structures of creation. Now, there is nothing impossible for me in the act of creation." As the lotus-born Brahma, thus filled with pride, prepared for creation, thousands of various obstacles appeared, O King. These terrifying beings surrounded Brahma. They were like bees intoxicated by flower nectar, swarming around a honeycomb. They had three eyes, five hands, well-like mouths, and burning palms. They had three feet, five or seven mouths, six feet, ten faces, five legs, teeth like palm trees, and bellies like wolves. These mighty beings of various forms were countless. Brahma trembled upon hearing their diverse cries. Some struck him with their fists, while others bowed and praised him. Some grabbed him by his four heads and swung him around with enthusiasm. Others laughed at his four faces, while some insulted him, praised him, or served him. Some tried to release him, while others bound him again. Others pulled him here and there to set him free. Some embraced him, while others kissed him like a child. One held him by his eight mustaches and danced with hands on his sides.

Being thus helpless, Brahma became filled with anxiety and sorrow. He abandoned the immense pride of creation that had resided in his heart. He fell into a deep faint, hopeless about his own life. After a moment passed, he remembered the Lord in his mind. Weeping, he prayed to the Elephant-faced Lord for mercy.

Brahma said: "I have no longevity, as my mind is attached to diverse births, nor do I possess the pure knowledge of truth that crosses the ocean of existence. Having obtained birth on earth, when shall I attain supreme enjoyment or liberation—the incomparable happiness—by worshipping You, O Teacher of all? Your devotee suffers, being touched by the nectar of Your sidelong glance, O Lord. Let this shame be Yours alone, for I am not one who dies, but one who is long-lived."

Bhrigu said: While he was praying thus, he heard a voice from the sky saying, "Perform penance." Then he prayed to that voice again. Upon hearing the voice from the sky, those obstacles of various forms and great power vanished, releasing the lotus-seated Brahma. Once freed, the glorious Brahma thought, "Without a mantra and without a place, how shall I perform great penance?" Distressed in mind, he wandered in the midst of the water, meditating with a focused mind on the sinless Elephant-faced Lord. He meditated on Him as adorned with a crown shining with pearls and gems, his body smeared with red sandalwood, and his forehead crimson with vermilion. He meditated on Him with a neck shining with pearl necklaces, a serpent as his sacred thread, and arms decorated with priceless jeweled ornaments. He meditated on Him with fingers shining with sparkling emerald rings, a great belly adorned with a large navel encircled by a great serpent, and a waist shining with a belt inlaid with various gems. He meditated on Him dressed in red garments shining with golden threads, with the moon on his forehead, and hands shining with the beauty of his tusks. As he was meditating thus, a voice from the sky arose again, saying, "Look at the banyan tree, look at the banyan tree." Hearing these words, Brahma became filled with contemplation once again.

Thus ends the fourteenth chapter of the Upasana Khanda of the Shri Ganesha Purana, titled "The Description of Brahma’s Contemplation."

Chapter 15 : Description of the Worship of Gajanan

Bhrigu said: O Somakant, listen with respect to the story that Brahma, the grandfather of the world, narrated to Vyasa earlier.

Brahma said: O best of sages, I once saw a magnificent dream. While wandering through the sky, I saw a great banyan tree in the water. When all moving and non-moving beings were destroyed by great winds and heat, only this one great banyan tree remained. Being filled with doubt, I saw on its leaf a small boy with four arms, wearing a beautiful crown and adorned with earrings. He wore a necklace of pearls and gems that looked beautiful on his neck. He had a crescent moon on his forehead, wore red garments, and a waistband. He had one tusk, a human body with an elephant face, and was glowing with brilliance. Seeing this, I wondered how this boy could be here. The boy then sprinkled water on my head with a lotus. I laughed loudly, filled with wonder and joy. As I laughed, the boy rose from the banyan tree, came to my lap, and spoke sweet words.

The boy said: You are the smallest of the small, and though you are old, your intellect is foolish. You are troubled by obstacles and consumed by the anxiety of creation. You are always engaged in meditation and penance, wandering in the midst of the waters. O four-faced one, I will give you a remedy to remove all anxieties. I instruct you in this one-syllable mantra of mine. Chant it ten lakh times through the process of Purascharana. Then I will manifest before you and grant you supreme power.

Brahma said: Having seen this wondrous dream, I woke up. I felt sorrow, wondering when I would have the vision of the Supreme Lord. Having seen the dream, I remained immersed in an ocean of bliss. Then, after bathing, I chanted the supreme mantra for many days, standing on one leg on a lotus, meditating on Gajanan. I remained steady like wood or stone, having conquered my senses and appetite, and performed great penance for a thousand divine years. Then, flames emerged from my mouth, which were difficult to endure. All beings were severely troubled by them. Seeing my firm resolve, Gajanan was pleased and appeared before me. He was as brilliant as a crore of suns, like a fire surrounded by flames, as if burning the three worlds and destroying the universe. He held an axe and a lotus, was adorned with divine ornaments, was the remover of all sins, a treasury of all beauty, beautiful with an elephant face, the fulfiller of devotees' desires, and the destroyer of all obstacles for gods, humans, and sages.

Seeing that mass of brilliance, I trembled, O Vyasa. I became deeply anxious and distracted from my chanting. My eyes were covered, and my memory faded. Seeing this state, the Lord of Obstacles spoke quickly.

Ganesh said: Do not fear, O Lord of the worlds! I am the same one who has come, by whom the auspicious one-syllable mantra was ordered to you in your dream. By that, you have attained perfection, and I have come to grant you a boon. I have become gentle; ask for a boon, O virtuous one. I am pleased and will grant you whatever is in your heart. With me pleased, everything will happen; there is no doubt about it.

The sage said: Hearing these pure words of the Lord of Ganas, Brahma rejoiced greatly. Bowing to the Guru of the moving and non-moving with all his heads, he said with a pleased heart, "My life is successful."

Brahma said: You, who are never within the reach of the Vedas, scriptures, the wise, yogis, or even all the Upanishads, have today become manifest to me through my accumulated merits. You are without beginning or end, infinite, immeasurable, and even without attributes. If you are pleased, O Lord of Gods, Lord of Obstacles, and ocean of compassion, grant me firm devotion so that sorrow may not touch me. Now, grant me the power for various creations, and if you are pleased with me, O Lord, may all obstacles be pacified. May you always be present to me upon being remembered, and may you complete all my tasks. At the end, grant me eternal liberation and pure knowledge.

Gajanan said: So be it. You shall perform the creation of many kinds. By remembering me, all obstacles will be destroyed from everywhere. Firm devotion and auspicious knowledge will come through my grace. Perform all your tasks without fear, O four-faced one.

The sage said: Having received the boon, Brahma worshipped that Lord. Whatever he thought of in his mind, that very thing appeared. For the worship of the Lord of Gods, Ganesh, by his grace, two maidens appeared at the time of the offering. They were beautiful, with pleasant eyes and faces, adorned with various ornaments inlaid with many gems. They were endowed with divine fragrance and adorned with divine garments and garlands. Brahma offered them as a dakshina (offering). He performed the waving of lights with a banana leaf, offered a handful of divine flowers, praised him with a thousand names, and circumambulated him. Bowing down, he prayed, "Be the savior of the helpless." Having been worshipped thus by Brahma, the Supreme Lord, the remover of obstacles, Gajanan, became pleased. Taking Siddhi and Buddhi with him, the Lord disappeared. Brahma then created the world as it was before, by the command of the Supreme Lord and by his grace, with a pleased mind.

Thus ends the fifteenth chapter of the Upasana Khanda of the Shri Ganesh Purana, titled "Description of the Worship of Gajanan."

Chapter 16 : The Prayer to the Goddess

The King said: Hearing the story of Ganesha fills my heart with joy. O sage, tell me more, for I am not satisfied with this nectar of stories. When the Lord Ganesha, the Supreme Soul, disappeared, how did Brahma create the universe? Please describe that to me, O Lord. I wish to hear in detail the account that the Creator once told Vyasa, O noble sage.

Bhrigu said: The Lord told Vyasa the greatness of the holy land and the sequence of how He created the world. I shall tell you the same. Brahma first created seven sons from his mind and asked them to assist in creation according to their own wisdom. Hearing this, they decided to perform penance. After performing great penance, they attained the Supreme Brahman. Then, the Creator fashioned seven other sons, but they were so filled with knowledge that they did not engage in the act of creation.

Then, seeing the sages like Sanaka, Brahma himself began the work. From his mouth, the Lotus-seated Lord created fire. From his arms and feet, he created the other three varnas; from his heart, the moon; from his eyes, the sun; from his ears, the wind and the life-breath. From his navel, he created the atmosphere; from his head, the heavens; from his feet, the earth; and from his ears, the directions and other worlds.
