He created the entire universe, both stationary and moving, including the oceans, rivers, mountains, grasses, shrubs, and trees.

Then, after some days had passed, two great demons were born from the earwax of the sleeping Lord Vishnu. They were known as Madhu and Kaitabha, famous in the three worlds. They had terrifying tusks, tawny eyes, and long noses. They were huge, loud, and as tall as mountains. They roared like rain clouds, filled with great arrogance. With many wicked words, they insulted the Lord. They reviled the Brahmins, gods, sages, saints, and the scriptures. At their sound, the earth and the serpent Shesha trembled. The entire universe became distressed by their noise.

Then, with eyes red with anger, they prepared to devour Brahma. Seeing this, the Lotus-seated Lord prayed to the Goddess, the bestower of boons, who causes the delusion of Vishnu, and who resides in the eyes of Vishnu as sleep, in order to destroy Madhu and Kaitabha and to awaken Hari. Knowing through the grace of Ganesha that they would be killed by the hands of Vishnu, and having considered it well, Brahma was filled with both anxiety and joy.

Brahma said: You are the embodiment of Svaha and Svadha, the nectar, the half-syllable, and the form of Maya. You are the creator, the destroyer, and the mother of all beings, both real and unreal; You are the Power itself. You are the Vedas, the musical notes, the dark night of time, beginningless, endless, and imperishable. You are the Mother of the world, the supporter of the world, and the cause of creation, sustenance, and dissolution. You are Savitri, Sandhya, Mahamaya, thirst, and hunger. You are the power behind all existing things, O Parvati.

The Lord of the three worlds, the destroyer of demons and giants, Hari, who possesses all knowledge and wisdom, is now overcome by sleep. He, by whom the world is created, sustained, and destroyed, is being restrained by You in this crisis of incarnations. Delude these wicked demons, Madhu and Kaitabha. Grant knowledge to Hari so that He may slay these invincible ones. In a previous birth, they worshipped me tirelessly, and I granted them many boons, making them both unkillable by me. Because of their loud and varied noises, I am suffering greatly. They even wish to kill me, despite having praised me with various hymns. Even so, they have not turned away from their wicked nature despite my pleas. Therefore, I pray to You, O Goddess, to awaken Vishnu.

Thus ends the sixteenth chapter named The Prayer to the Goddess in the Upasana Khanda of the Sri Ganesha Purana.

Chapter 17 : The Instruction of the Mantra

As long as Vishnu remained asleep, the two demons (Madhu and Kaitabha) conquered the heavens, the abode of Indra, the realm of Yama, and the kingdom of Kubera. Seeing them, the gods fled in all directions; some fell, some wandered aimlessly, some fainted, and others stumbled. Then, Hari, the Lord, was awakened from sleep by the Goddess. After consoling all the gods, He engaged in battle with the two demons. He prevented them from further attacking the gods, the serpents, the sages, the Yakshas, and the Rakshasas.

Hari appeared there, radiant, holding the conch, discus, and mace, adorned with a crown and earrings, and possessing the deep complexion of a dark cloud. The Lord blew His conch with great force, and by that mighty sound, He shook the entire universe. Hearing the sound of Panchajanya, the hearts of the two demons trembled. Terrified, they said to each other, "We have conquered the entire earth, the netherworld, and the twenty-one heavens, yet we never heard this sound before. This sound has shaken our hearts, which are as hard as a thunderbolt. Therefore, we must fight this powerful being. To satisfy our urge for battle and to achieve victory, we shall either kill this enemy or go to the next world."

Having decided this, they challenged Hari, who was ready for battle, saying, "O Supreme Being, we have seen you to satisfy our urge for combat. How have you attained greatness by coming into our sight?"

The Sage said: Hearing these words, the Lord, whose fame is widespread, replied.

Hari said: "You have spoken well, O great demons. Fight with me as you wish. No one desires their own death."

They replied: "You are four-armed, O Lord of gods; grant us a duel."

The Sage said: Hearing this, Hari, filled with joy, said "So be it." Abandoning His weapons, the four-armed Lord fought the two. They struck head against head, thigh against thigh, elbow against elbow, and arm against arm. They struck ankle against ankle, waist against waist, nose against nose, fist against fist, and back against back. They grappled, wrestled, and circled each other. Thus, the battle continued for a long time.

Five thousand years passed, O great sage, but the Lord Hari could not defeat them. Then, He assumed the form of a Gandharva, skilled in music. Going to a beautiful forest, He played the Veena and sang. The deer, the animals, the people, the gods, the Gandharvas, and the demons all became motionless, absorbed in His music. Lord Shiva, on Mount Kailasa, heard the melody repeatedly. He said to Nikumbha and Pushpadanta, "Bring here quickly the one who is singing in the forest."

They went, saw him, and said, "Hearing your song, Shankara is filled with joy. O sinless one, the Lord calls you to hear your music. Come with us; we shall go to him quickly." Hearing their words, the Gandharva, who was a devotee of Hari, went with them to where the great Lord Maheshwara resided. He saw the beloved of Parvati, adorned with the crescent moon, wearing an elephant skin, and decorated with a garland of skulls. He saw the Lord with matted hair, wearing a serpent as a sacred thread, and bowed before the Lord of the world, the destroyer of the suffering of those who surrender.

Shiva lifted him up with his own hand, offered him a seat, and worshipped him. Then Hari said, "Today my life is fulfilled, for today I have had your vision, which grants righteousness, desire, wealth, and liberation." The Gandharva, intent on music, pleased the Lord with the sounds of the Veena, sweet notes, and various melodies. Skanda, the leader of the Ganas, the Goddess Parvati, and the gods and sages were all delighted. Then Maheshwara affectionately embraced Hari, who was adorned with the conch, discus, mace, lotus, and yellow garments. He said, "O Hari, ask of me whatever you desire. I will grant it, for I am filled with supreme joy by your music."

Bhrigu said: Then the Lord told him the story of the two demons.

Hari said: "While I was sleeping on the ocean of milk, Madhu and Kaitabha were born from the earwax and went to devour Brahma. Brahma praised the Goddess of Sleep, and by her, I was awakened. I fought them in a duel, but I have not been able to defeat them. Therefore, O ocean of compassion, tell me the way to kill them."

Shiva said: "You went to the battlefield without worshipping Ganesha. That is why you are devoid of power and suffering greatly. Go and worship Ganesha, and then proceed to battle. He will delude them with His Maya and bring them under your control. By my grace, you will undoubtedly kill those wicked ones."

Hari said: "O Shiva, tell me how to worship the Lord Vinayaka."

Ishwara said: "There are seven crores of mantras for the Lord of the Ganas. Among them are great mantras, and among those, the single-syllable mantra is supreme. I will tell you the six-syllable mantra. By removing the single-syllable mantra and using the method of the 'Siddhari' cycle, and after balancing the debt and wealth, the six-syllable mantra is revealed—the great mantra of Ganesha, which grants all success and is auspicious. By merely practicing this, your task will be accomplished." Then Hari went quickly to perform the practice.

Thus ends the seventeenth chapter, titled "The Instruction of the Mantra," in the Upasana Khanda of the Shri Ganesha Purana.

Chapter 18 : The Story of the Origin of the Siddha Kshetra

Somakanta asked: How did Hari chant the supreme mantra, and where? How did he attain perfection? Tell me this in detail.

Bhrigu replied: There is a place on earth known as Siddha Kshetra, which is supreme in granting perfection. Having gone there, Lord Vishnu performed great penance. Meditating on Lord Vinayaka according to the six-syllable method, he worshipped him after subduing his senses with effort. He performed the protection of the directions using the arrow mantra, purified the elements, established the vital breaths, performed the internal and external Matrika Nyasa, and controlled his breath. Meditating on the elephant-faced Lord, he worshipped him with mental offerings, various substances, and the sixteen modes of service, chanting the supreme mantra, for he is the Lord of all Yogis.

After a hundred years had passed, the Supreme Lord, the master of the Ganas, appeared before him, shining like ten million suns and fires. With a very pleased heart, he spoke to the Lord whose emblem is Garuda: "O Hari, ask for the boons you desire from me. Pleased by this penance, I shall grant them all. Had you worshipped me earlier, your victory would have been certain."

Hari said: O Ganesha, you are the one whom Brahma, Shiva, Indra, and other gods are unable to see even through penance. I behold you, the one who possesses many forms yet is one, the manifest and the unmanifest. You are smaller than the smallest and greater than the greatest, such as the sky. By divine will, you repeatedly create, sustain, and destroy the beings. You are the soul of all, all-pervading, all-powerful, the creator of all, the supreme Lord, the witness of all, the destroyer, the sustainer, the supporter, the leader of the universe, and the father. By your vision, all my perfections will be fulfilled. Yet, I ask one thing of you. At the end of my Yoga Nidra, the two mighty demons Madhu and Kaitabha, born from the impurities of the Vedas, are ready to devour me. I fought them for many years, but then, exhausted, I sought your refuge. Therefore, consider how they may be killed by me. Grant me victory over other demons as well, and give me your unfailing devotion, by which my unparalleled fame will purify the three worlds.

Ganesha said: O Vishnu, whatever you have requested will certainly come to pass. Your fame, strength, and glory will be supreme, and you will be free from obstacles. Having said this, the Lord vanished right there.

Filled with joy, Vishnu considered the two demons already defeated. He built a palace there made of crystal and precious gems, with shining golden spires and four beautiful gates. He installed an image made of Gandaki stone. The gods and sages named it Siddha Vinayaka, as Hari had attained auspicious perfection there. Thus, that place became known as Siddha Kshetra throughout the world.

Then Hari went to where Madhu and Kaitabha were. Seeing him approach, they laughed and mocked him, saying, "Why do you show us your cloud-dark face again? We shall grant you great liberation; why have you become so lowly and come here to fight?"

Hari replied: "A small fire suddenly burns everything, just as a small lamp destroys darkness in a great night. Similarly, I am capable of destroying you both today, despite your arrogance."

Hearing his words, the furious Madhu and Kaitabha suddenly struck Vishnu's heart with their fists. A wrestling match ensued between them. After fighting for many days, Hari, eager to grant them a boon, spoke to them in a gentle voice: "I am pleased by your strength, O great demons, for you have withstood my blows for many years. None like you have been born or will ever be born."

They replied: "Ask for boons from us, O Hari; we shall grant you many. We are satisfied with your fighting and with you."

Hearing their words, Hari, deluded by Maya, said: "If you are ready to grant me boons, then I choose this boon: that you be killed by my hands."

Seeing that everything was water, the two demons, highly pleased, said: "Death by your hands is auspicious. At the end, by thinking of you, one immediately attains eternal liberation. O Madhava, kill us where there is no land, only water. We both renounce everything; truth is established in truth."

Hearing their words, Hari held them and cut off their heads with his razor-sharp discus. The gods rejoiced and showered flowers. The Gandharvas danced, and the Apsaras sang. Then Vishnu, filled with joy, went to Brahma and told him the whole story.

Hari said: "When I was unable to defeat them, I went to Shiva. I was instructed by Shankara in the supreme six-syllable mantra. Through it, I worshipped the Lord, the remover of obstacles. He granted me boons that fulfill all desires. By that power, I killed the wicked Madhu and Kaitabha."

Having been praised and worshipped, the Lord then vanished. The greatness of the noble Ganesha was thus known. "By the grace of Shankara, I shall destroy the demons." The gods and sages, having praised the elephant-faced Lord, Brahma, Shiva, and me, returned to their respective abodes. Whoever listens to this greatness daily, which destroys all sins, will have no fear from anywhere and will attain all desired objects.

Thus ends the eighteenth chapter of the Upasana Khanda in the Shri Ganesha Purana, titled "The Story of the Origin of the Siddha Kshetra."

Chapter 19 : Description of Kamala’s Son

Bhrigu said: Having heard this narrative and the six-syllable mantra along with the prescribed rituals, Vyasa spoke again to the Creator, his heart not yet fully satisfied.

Vyasa said: I have heard the greatness of the holy site and the Lord of the Ganas, which destroys all sins and increases merit and desires. O Brahman, please tell me more stories of Vinayaka; one never attains complete satisfaction while drinking the nectar of his stories.

Brahma said: O son of Parashara, I shall narrate to you the auspicious story of Ganesha, the God of all gods, which is before you. In the land of Vidarbha, there was a king named Bhima, who was heroic, generous, and possessed immense strength. He resided in the city of Kaundinya. He was served by many feudatory kings, and he commanded ten crores of horses, elephants, and infantry. Thousands of Brahmins lived under his protection and found happiness. He had a wife named Charuhasini, who was highly virtuous, with a face like a blooming lotus and eyes like those of a young deer. She was devoted to the gods, righteous, chaste, and always obedient to her husband.

By the decree of fate, she remained childless. Seeing her, the king, who was otherwise perfect, felt great sorrow. He said, "Without a son, there is no path to heaven or happiness. The gods do not accept the offerings of one without a son, nor do the ancestors. My birth, my father, my home, my wealth, and my lineage are all in vain." Having decided this, he called his two ministers, Manoranjana and Sumantu. They were learned in all sciences and arts. They came and bowed to the king. Bhima told them, "Govern my kingdom. Because of some sin from a past life, either mine or my wife’s, we have no children. If I return, give the kingdom back to me; otherwise, divide it between yourselves."

Having decided this, the king, after performing auspicious rites and giving many gifts to Brahmins, left the city with his wife. After traveling a short distance, he dismissed his attendants. The ministers said, "O King, we shall accompany you." The citizens and friends wept in sorrow. The king consoled them, saying, "Do not fear; I have made these two your lords. They will protect you just as I have." After entrusting the kingdom to them, he left the city with his wife.

While wandering, he saw a lake filled with lotuses and surrounded by flowering trees and aquatic life. Near it, he saw a beautiful hermitage that brought joy to all. There, animals that were natural enemies—lions and deer, snakes and mongooses, cats and mice—lived without enmity. He saw the sage Vishwamitra sitting on a kusha-grass mat, surrounded by disciples intent on studying the Vedas. Both the king and his wife bowed down, folded their hands, and touched the sage's feet repeatedly.

The great sage Vishwamitra, knowing the purpose of their visit, raised them and spoke in a gentle voice. Vishwamitra said, "O King, you will have a virtuous and famous son. Where have you come from, and what is the name of your city? I shall strive to remove your sins."

Bhima said, "My kingdom is Vidarbha, and my city is Kaundinya. My name is Bhima, and this is my wife, Charuhasini. We have tried to have a son through penance, charity, and vows, but due to sins from a past life, we have not succeeded. We have left our kingdom and come to the forest, and now we have found your feet. The company of the righteous immediately bears fruit. I have no doubt that through your blessings, a son will be born to me. O Sage, tell me what sin I committed in my past life so that I may perform the necessary atonement, for you are all-knowing."

Brahma said: Hearing his words, the great sage Vishwamitra kindly told the king the story of his past. Vishwamitra said, "O foolish king, you have abandoned the family traditions established in the Vedas, Shastras, and Puranas. Your ancestors always worshipped the Lord of the Ganas (Ganesha). It is because of his anger that you have no progeny. He is the family deity of your lineage. Listen to the story from the beginning. Seven generations ago, there was a king named Bhima, who was handsome and wealthy. After a long time, a child was born to him. However, the child was born mute, deaf, hunchbacked, and suffering from a foul-smelling disease. His mother, Kamala, was deeply saddened. She thought, 'Is this what it means to have a son? It is better to die.' She wept bitterly. Hearing her cries, the king came to the maternity room. Seeing the child and his grieving wife, he spoke gently, 'Do not grieve, O auspicious one. Such is the path of karma. A person suffers due to sins of a past life. Do not lament; this child will become well. We shall strive with gems, mantras, medicines, penance, and prayers.' Consoling her, the king had her perform all the rites appropriate for the birth of a son, and she worshipped the Brahmins many times."

Thus ends the nineteenth chapter of the Upasana Khanda of the Shri Ganesha Purana, describing the son of Kamala.

Chapter 20 : The Hymn of Daksha

Brahma said: Then, Daksha invited learned Brahmins who were well-versed in the Vedas and astrology, and honored them with gems, garments, and wealth. After consulting them, he named his son Daksha. He performed various rituals, chanted mantras, and prepared medicines for his son. He himself performed supreme penance for twelve years for the sake of his son’s recovery and the prosperity of his lineage. However, the King did not see his son cured of his ailments. Filled with despair and overcome by anger, he said to his wife, "O Queen Kamala, leave my palace with your son. I am no longer able to look at this son or you."

Thus rebuked by her husband, Kamala took her son and departed from the city toward the forest. Weeping, distressed, and wiping away her tears, she carried him on her back, exhausted by hunger, thirst, and fatigue. She wandered from village to village, begging for alms, deeply miserable. Thieves stole her garments and ornaments. Eventually, she reached another village, left her son in a temple of Shiva, and wandered into the town to beg.

Once, while she went to beg with her son, the child was touched by the breeze near a great Brahmin. Due to the intensity of his devotion, the child was blessed by Lord Lambodara (Ganesha). Suddenly, he gained his sight, his hearing was restored, and he attained a divine body. Kamala was overjoyed, her sorrow vanishing as she saw him speaking beautiful, pleasant words repeatedly. She wondered if it was the result of her gems, mantras, medicines, rituals, or great fire sacrifices that had not worked before. "How did my son become healthy just by the touch of the breeze? Where will I find that person who destroyed my son's misfortune?" she pondered. Embracing her son, she stood there, relieved of her fever, and took him back to the city to beg again.

The townspeople, having invited them with respect, fed them various cooked foods, vegetables, sweets, ghee, and milk-rice. Every day, they ate with joy and received new clothes and precious gifts. A citizen asked him, "Tell me the name of your father, your country, your city, your lineage, and your story." Hearing this, he said his name was Daksha. Then, having approached his mother, he asked about his father, city, and lineage. She told him, "In the city of Bhanunagara in Karnataka, your grandfather was a powerful Kshatriya, famous for crushing enemy forces. I am Kamala, his wife, and you are my son, Daksha."

He replied, "When I was born, O Brahmin, I was blind, deaf, and afflicted with many ailments, so my mother was ready to abandon me. My father forbade her and made many efforts. He performed penance for twelve years and offered his merit. When my father saw that I had no physical strength, he sent my mother and me away, O citizen." Daksha told him everything he had heard from his mother. "Here, I became fortunate by the touch of the breeze of someone."

Hearing this, the man left, and Daksha quickly went to his mother, rejoicing. Then, in that city, both were instructed by a Brahmin in the worship of Ganesha with a compassionate heart. Kamala and Daksha attained the supreme state of liberation, devoted to the worship of Ganesha while performing penance on one toe. Chanting the eight-syllable mantra—the name of the Lord in the dative case preceded by Om—they became absorbed in devotion.

Seeing them thin and surviving only on air, the Lord of Compassion, Vinayaka, appeared before them. He had four arms, a massive body, an elephant face, and was extremely beautiful, shining like many suns, as if the sun had risen at night. His head was adorned with a crown of gems, gold, and pearls; he wore yellow silk garments and golden armlets. He sat on a great seat with one knee bent, wearing a golden waist-string and gem-studded rings. Bearing a great serpent on his belly, he had one tusk and the face of an elephant. Seeing him in the form of a Brahmin, they bowed.

The Brahmin, pleased by their liberation, said, "I have come to grant you a boon; ask for what your heart desires."

Vishvamitra said: Thus, when the Lord of Obstacles appeared in the form of a Brahmin, Daksha bowed with supreme devotion and, with folded hands, spoke to him.

Daksha said: The merit of my previous birth has borne fruit, O best of Brahmins, as I have seen your supreme and magnificent form. My birth has become meaningful through both your Ganesha form and your Brahmin form. You are the cause of all causes and the source of the Vedas. You are the supreme knowledge, the supreme Brahman, the eternal one sought by the Shruti. You are the witness of everything, existing both inside and outside all. You are the creator of all actions, whether of small or large beings. You are the one in many forms, yet formless and without attributes. You are Shankara, Vishnu, Indra, Agni, and Aryama. You are the form of earth, air, space, water, the moon, and the stars. You are the creator, protector, and destroyer of the universe. You are the teacher of the moving and non-moving, the knower of knowledge and science. You are the past, the future, and the present; you are the deities of the senses. You are the divisions of time, prosperity, patience, and radiance. You are Samkhya, Yoga, the scriptures, the Vedas, the Puranas, the sixty-four arts, and the Upanishads. You are the Brahmin, Vaishya, Kshatriya, and Shudra. You are the country, the foreign land, and the sacred places. You are the measurable and the immeasurable, the object of knowledge for yogis. You are heaven, the netherworld, the forests, and the gardens. You are the herbs, creepers, trees, roots, and fruits. You are the beings born from eggs, the womb, sweat, and the earth. You are desire, anger, hunger, greed, vanity, pride, compassion, forgiveness, sleep, lethargy, pleasure, and sorrow.

Vishvamitra said: Hearing these words of Daksha, Vinayaka was very pleased and spoke to him with a voice as deep as a cloud, as if smiling.

Gajanana said: I am pleased with you, O fortunate one, by this profound hymn. I am eager to grant you a boon, yet I will not give it to you myself. If I were to grant you the boon, you might become angry with me. He who restored your body, through whose breeze you attained a divine body and gained sight and hearing, he will grant you the boon. His name is Mudgala. If you merely meditate on that great Brahmin, he will show himself to you and grant you all the desires you seek.

Having said this, the Supreme Lord disappeared right there. When he vanished, Daksha wept, deeply distressed. Just as a poor man laments when a treasure he has found is lost, or as a calf cries when the cow is gone, he shed tears from his eyes and fell to the ground, repeatedly calling out, "Where has he gone? Where has he gone?"

Chapter 21 : The Description of the Instruction of the Mantra

Vishvamitra said:
Wandering here and there, the son of Vallabha, being extremely distressed, did not even realize that his clothes and ornaments had fallen off. He asked the Brahmins on the road and even the trees, "Where has Vinayaka gone? Have you seen him anywhere? Tell me." With a bewildered heart and wandering eyes, the best of princes fell to the ground, losing consciousness for a moment. In the meantime, in a dream, he saw a Brahmin before him who said, "O child, that which you had previously requested from the Lord of Obstacles (Ganesha) has been granted to you by me, Mudgala." Having said this, the Brahmin departed.

Upon waking, the prince felt as if he had just risen from sleep, and his heart became joyful. He asked a passing Brahmin for directions. The Brahmin pointed him toward an ashram, adorned with many disciples, belonging to Mudgala, who was a great devotee of the Elephant-faced Lord. It was a supreme, divine hermitage that granted fearlessness to all beings. Meditating on Mudgala in his mind, the prince wandered until he reached the ashram, which was filled with many wonders and surpassed even Alakananda in beauty.

There, he saw Mudgala, a Brahmin seated on a precious throne, a knower of the Vedas and Vedangas, and an expert in all scriptures. Through the power of his yoga practice, he appeared in various forms, radiant as the sun. He was worshipping a great idol of Vinayaka, made of gold and gems, which had four arms, three eyes, and was adorned with various ornaments, following the sixteen-fold ritual. Seeing him, Daksha bowed down to the ground like a staff, shedding tears from his eyes and sighing repeatedly.

Vishvamitra said:
Mudgala asked him, "Who are you and why have you come here? Tell me everything completely, so that I may remove your sorrow." Hearing the words of the Brahmin, the son of Kamala became attentive and spoke to the best of the twice-born.

Daksha said:
O Brahmin, I shall tell you the truth. In the Karnataka country, there was a king in a city named Bhanu. He had a minister named Vallabha, who was wise, generous, and compassionate. When his wife, Kamala, gave birth to me, I was born with a foul-smelling body, bleeding nose, blind, hunchbacked, deaf, and mute, breathing with great difficulty. Seeing this, the citizens told my father to abandon me. For twelve years, my father tried various efforts to improve my body, but he could not find a remedy from the great lords. Consequently, he heartlessly cast me and my mother, Kamala, out of the house.

My mother, wandering from city to city in grief, arrived at Kaundinyapura, hungry and exhausted. While begging for alms, due to the influence of my past merits, I had a vision of you, just as a blind man regains his sight. By the touch of the breeze coming from your body, my defects vanished. Just as Ahalya attained a divine body by the touch of Raghunatha’s feet in the past, I too attained a divine body through your grace, O virtuous one. I did not know anything, but my mother told me everything. I was amazed and made a firm resolve in my heart: "If the touch of the breeze from his body can grant a divine form, then I shall only accept this body when I see him again."

Wandering for many days, that ocean of compassion (Ganesha) appeared before me, radiant as a crore of suns. Pleased by the penance of both of us, the Lord of Lords, the Elephant-faced one, appeared. Seeing him, Kamala attained all the desires of her heart. Then the Lord, pleased, spoke to me, "O you with beautiful limbs, for the sake of which you performed penance and wandered in sorrow, I have come to give you my vision." Hearing those words, my heart became joyful.

I praised the Elephant-faced Lord with various hymns. He, being very pleased, said, "Ask for a boon, O great-minded one." I told him everything that was in my heart. Then, leaving the Brahmin form, he assumed another form—four-armed, massive, with a crown, holding an axe, a lotus, a garland, and modakas in his hands. He wore divine garments, his tusks were shining, and he wore earrings that looked like two suns. He was adorned with divine ornaments, with a snake as his belt, and was surrounded by gods, sages, Gandharvas, and Kinnaras. Seeing that form, I was filled with joy, just as the ocean becomes full upon seeing the full moon. He then said, "Mudgala will fulfill all your desires."

As soon as that form disappeared, I looked for it with longing, but just as everything seen in a dream is not visible in the waking state, I could not see him. I became extremely distressed and fell to the ground unconscious. Regaining consciousness, I remembered his words to ask for a boon. I asked the Lord of Lords, the all-pervading God, "May Lakshmi be stable in my home, and may I have such devotion for you. If I have seen you due to past merits, please grant me these two." I then heard a voice from the sky saying, "It is granted." O Brahmin, with a joyful heart, I have come to your presence. You are Mudgala, and he is the Elephant-faced one; you are the Elephant-faced one, and he is Mudgala. This is clearly evident to me.

Bhrigu said:
Hearing these words, Mudgala said: "O son of Kamala, you are fortunate and accomplished, and you are a devotee. The greatness of your devotion cannot be described by anyone. I have performed intense penance for a thousand years, yet I have never seen such a God. He who is the Lord of all worlds, the Guru of the moving and non-moving, the leader of the Gunas (Sattva, Rajas, Tamas), who creates the bodies of Brahma, Shiva, and Vishnu, whom even the gods, Vedas, and sages do not fully know—you have seen that Elephant-faced Lord clearly. I bow to your feet because you are the supreme devotee."

Then they bowed to each other and embraced. As spiritual brothers, they remained together with one mind for a long time. Then, Mudgala instructed the humble prince in the single-syllable mantra, along with the method of japa and meditation. He said, "Practice this mantra every day. The Elephant-faced Lord will be pleased and will grant you all the desires of your heart. If you abandon this mantra, you will face total destruction. If you practice this devotion for a long time while wandering in this world, the hosts of Indra and other guardians will come under your control. Having enjoyed all pleasures here, you will attain liberation in the end."

Thus ends the twenty-first chapter of the Upasana Khanda in the Shri Ganesha Purana, titled "The Description of the Instruction of the Mantra."

Chapter 22 : The Story of Ballala Vinayaka

The King said: O best of sages, you have narrated the astonishing deeds of Daksha’s son, and I am filled with great wonder. How did he become blind, hunchbacked, bleeding, foul-smelling, speechless, impure, and left with only his breath? By what merit did he attain a divine body through the breath of Mudgala, and why was he freed from his sins? He performed supreme penance for a thousand divine years; why did he not receive the vision he desired? How did the Lord of Lords, the Elephant-faced God, appear before the son of Vallabha without any struggle, and who was he in his previous life? O omniscient one, please remove these doubts of mine, for I am never satisfied while drinking the nectar of the stories of the Elephant-faced God.

Vishvamitra said: O King, you have asked well. To remove your doubts, I shall tell you everything correctly; listen with a focused mind. In the Sindh region, there was a very famous city named Palli. In that city lived a wealthy merchant named Kalyana. He was generous, skilled, wise, and devoted to Brahmins and gods. His wife, Indumati, was famous, beautiful, chaste, devoted to her husband, and obedient to his words. In time, they had a virtuous and excellent son. Kalyana gave cows, clothes, ornaments, gold, and abundant wealth to the Brahmins. Following the advice of astrologers, he named his son Ballala, as he was auspicious and strong.

As time passed, Ballala, along with his friends, would happily go outside the village daily to worship the gods. Once, Ballala and the other boys went to the forest, where they played various games. After bathing, they set up a beautiful stone, worshipped it as Ganesha with sprouts of Durva grass, and some, absorbed in meditation, chanted His name. Some danced out of devotion, while others, skilled in singing, sang to please the Lord. Some built a pavilion with wood and leaves, while others constructed walls and an excellent temple. Some performed mental worship, while others offered flowers, creepers, incense, lamps, food, fruits, betel leaves, and gifts. They worshipped Him with supreme joy. Some, acting as scholars, recited Puranas, while others explained the scriptures and other texts. In this way, all of them, devoted to the Lord, spent many days. They did not even feel hunger or thirst due to their devotion to the Lord.

One day, their fathers approached the merchant Kalyana, angry, and said, "Stop your Ballala! He takes all the children to the forest every day. They do not return for breakfast, lunch, or at dusk. Our children have become emaciated; discipline your son now. Otherwise, we will bind him and beat him, or approach the city ruler and have you exiled." Hearing these words, which he had never heard before, Kalyana became enraged, his eyes turning red like Japa flowers. He took a large stick and went to beat his son. With a strike of the stick, he broke the pavilion. The boys fled in all directions, but Ballala remained firm in his devotion.

Kalyana grabbed him firmly and beat him severely with the stick until blood flowed from his entire body like streams of water from a mountain during the rainy season. He then threw the beautiful, vermilion-colored idol far away. He tied his son to a tree with hundreds of creeper-ropes, abandoning his paternal love like a servant of Yama. He said, "Let your God free you! If He is a giver, let Him provide you with food, drink, and shelter. If you come home, you will surely die."

The sage said: Having destroyed the temple, the angry Kalyana went to his house, having bound his son in the forest, and became very wicked due to fate. After he left, the merchant’s son lamented, thinking of the Lord in his mind. "How is it that Your name, 'Vighnarari' (Destroyer of Obstacles), is sung by all people? If You do not destroy wicked obstacles, how are You famous as the destroyer of the wicked? The serpent holds the earth, the sun gives light, the moon gives nectar, and fire gives heat. If they abandon their devotees, how would they be famous in the Vedas and scriptures? Why do You abandon me?"

Lamenting thus, he cursed his father, Kalyana, who had become very wicked. "Because he destroyed my excellent temple, threw away the Ganesha idol, and beat me, he shall surely become blind, deaf, hunchbacked, and mute. If my devotion to the Elephant-faced God is firm, then my words shall be true. He is not capable of binding me in this body when my heart is bound to You. With a focused mind, I shall abandon this body in this lonely forest, as I have not fled; this body is offered to the Lord."

Knowing his resolve, the Elephant-faced God appeared in the form of a Brahmin due to Ballala’s influence. Just as the sun rises on the eastern mountain when the darkness of night vanishes, so too did the bonds loosen by His radiance. Ballala then bowed down to Him like a stick. His body became beautiful, with no wounds or blood, and his knowledge became pure from the vision of the Lord of Lords. He praised the Elephant-faced God according to his ability.

Ballala said: You are my mother, father, and friend; You are the creator of the moving and the non-moving. You create the wicked, the villains, and the saints, and You appoint them to various births. You are the form of the directions, the sky, the earth, the ocean, the mountains, the fire of time, and the wind. You are the sun, the moon, the stars, the planets, the guardians of the world, the senses, the objects of senses, the herbs, and the elements.

The sage said: Hearing this praise, the Elephant-faced God was pleased. Embracing His devotee, He spoke with a deep voice.

Ganesha said: He who destroyed my temple will fall into hell. By my command, your curse will also come true for him. He will become blind, deaf, hunchbacked, mute, and afflicted with bleeding, without a doubt. His father and mother will cast him out of the house. Ask for another boon, and I will grant it to you, even if it is difficult to obtain.

The sage said: Ballala then said to the Lord, "May my devotion to You be firm. Remain here in this region and protect the people from obstacles."

Ganesha said: Your name will precede mine and become auspicious among people; it will be well-established in the city as Ballala Vinayaka. Your mind will remain fixed on me with unwavering devotion. People will make pilgrimages to the city named Palli. To those who come on the fourth day of the bright fortnight of Bhadrapada, I will grant their desires.

Bhrigu said: Having granted this boon, the Lord disappeared right there. Then, Ballala, along with the Brahmins, installed Vinayaka and had a temple built, adorned with various beauties.

Vishvamitra said: I have told you the story of Ballala Vinayaka. Hearing this, one is freed from all sins and attains their desires.

Chapter 23 : The Prediction of the Future

Bhrigu said: Having heard the words of Vishvamitra, Bhima asked him again, being extremely eager to hear the story of the merchant's conduct.

Bhima said: I have heard the story of Daksha, and my mind is at rest. Tell me, what was the fate of the virtuous merchant?

Vishvamitra said: Listen with a focused mind, Bhima, as I tell you this story. Due to the curse of Ballala, blood flowed from the body of Kalyana. The wicked man became afflicted with countless wounds, deafness, blindness, dumbness, and a foul odor. Indumati saw his sudden condition and lamented, asking why this had happened to such a wise, generous, and pious man who was devoted to gods and Brahmins, firm in the principles of Dharma, and satisfied with his own wife. How did my innocent husband fall into such a state?

The sage said: After lamenting thus and sighing repeatedly, hearing that her son had been bound in the forest, she wept and went with the townspeople to the place where he was bound. There, she saw the temple of the Lord, the leader of the Ganas. He was four-armed, three-eyed, and had a body as red as vermilion. She saw her son, Ballala, worshipping the elephant-faced Lord. Seeing him free, uninjured, and whole, Indumati, in her anger, rebuked him repeatedly, asking how she had been deceived by liars in the presence of her husband, and how she had come there out of affection for her son, leaving her husband in such a state. She told him to look at her devotion to the Lord, which had made her son like this.

The sage said: All those present were astonished and said nothing. Some said, who can fathom the greatness of such devotion? Seeing her son with his body smeared with vermilion and red sandalwood, adorned with red garments and garlands, selfless, ego-less, and looking like the remover of obstacles himself, she shed her grief and rejoiced. She embraced him, her breasts flowing with affection, and told him to go home, saying that his father was in great distress and had become deformed. She said, we are the most blessed in the world to have a son like you, but your father is now blind, deaf, wounded, and suffering. I have come to tell you this. If he punished you out of paternal duty, there is no sin in it according to the scriptures. Look at your duty as a son and seek a cure. Your father is most praiseworthy. A noble son must serve, nourish, and protect his parents. Seek a remedy through medicine, mantras, or prayer to the gods, and look at me. You will gain fame and fortune.

Hearing her words, Ballala said: Who is a mother, who is a father, who is a son or a friend? Everything is caused by the Lord of Obstacles. Therefore, O lady, my father and mother are the Lord Vinayaka. One reaps the fruits of one's actions. I have surrendered my life to the elephant-faced Lord, and He has given me life and knowledge. The suffering of my father is the result of his own actions—breaking the temple and attacking the devotee of Vinayaka. In truth, you are not my mother, nor is he my father. The Lord is the father and mother of all. He is the savior, the destroyer, the essence of all, and the embodiment of Brahma, Vishnu, and Shiva. The sight of that wicked man who attacked the Lord and broke the temple is a great sin. Leave your affection for me and go serve your husband.

Vishvamitra said: Hearing the words of her son, she asked him again to grant a counter-curse out of compassion and grace. The son replied: In another birth, you will be his mother, and you will be a virtuous woman. You will be known as Kamala, and he will be a noble Kshatriya named Vallabha. Your son will be known as Daksha. For twelve years, Vallabha will perform penance to remove the blindness, deafness, and wounds of Daksha. Failing to get results, he will cast you out of the house, and you will live in a foreign land. By the grace of a noble Brahmin who is devoted to the elephant-faced Lord, you will have a son. There, you will have a vision of the Lord of the Ganas, and he will attain a divine body. This is the reason for the counter-curse. Now that the future has been told, you may go as you please.

Vishvamitra said: Thus rejected by him, the mother departed, filled with both sorrow and a little joy. Ballala ascended the divine chariot provided by the elephant-faced Lord and went to heaven. I have told you everything you asked, the fate of that merchant in both births. Everything happened just as Ballala had said. She became Kamala, and he became the noble Kshatriya.

Chapter 24 : The Narration of the Dream

Bhima said: O best of sages, where, how, and for whom did the wise prince Daksha perform his rituals? Please explain this to me in detail, for I am not satisfied with hearing just a little.

Vishvamitra said: Not far from that city was the great forest of Kaundinya, which was beautiful, filled with various trees, teeming with diverse wild animals, inhabited by flocks of many birds, and adorned with networks of creepers. It was graced by lakes and ponds with water as pure as a noble mind, and in the center of an old palace stood an image of the elephant-faced deity. Staying there, he performed penance to please the elephant-faced Lord, using the single-syllable mantra instructed by Mudgala. He pleased that deity for twelve years with baths, garments, fragrances, garlands, incense, and lamps. The best of Kshatriyas offered food, roots, and fruits, and mentally offered his own devotion. O King, twenty-one days passed in this manner. Then, at the time of dawn, he saw this himself: a great elephant, smeared with vermilion, beautiful, with temples dripping with ichor, resembling a mountain, with a pleasant face, shining tusks, and a great trunk, surrounded by bees, appearing like another Ganesha. That elephant placed a garland of gems around his neck. Then, lifting him up, the king of elephants placed him on his shoulders. The elephant then went to the city, which was adorned with flags and banners.

Upon waking, he asked his mother, "O Kamala, tell me today the meaning of this. Is riding on the shoulders of an elephant auspicious or inauspicious?"

Kamala said: You are blessed, for the one you saw in the form of an elephant is Vinayaka himself. The result of riding him is undoubtedly the attainment of a kingdom.

Daksha said: If I attain the kingdom, then I will give you a palanquin, villages, lamps, and necklaces of pearls. I will also perform righteous deeds by gifting cows and gold, and I will observe many vows, disciplines, and charities.

The sage said: Hearing this, Kamala was delighted and said to her son: My son, when you are established in the kingdom, I will experience supreme joy. May your mind delight in true righteousness, may you be a protector of the virtuous, may your lifespan be long, and may you find joy in the worship of the twice-born and the gods.

Thus ends the twenty-fourth chapter named The Narration of the Dream in the Upasana Khanda of the Shri Ganesha Purana.

Chapter 25 : The Death of King Chandrasena and Queen Sulabha's Grief

Vishvamitra said: Listen, O King, to a great and auspicious wonder brought about by destiny and time. In the city of Kaundinya, there lived a wise king named Chandrasena. Due to the ripening of his past actions and the passage of time, he passed away and ascended to heaven in a divine chariot, owing to the abundance of his merits. Upon hearing this, the citizens were overcome with grief and raised a cry of lamentation. Leaving their various tasks behind, they rushed to the scene. Overwhelmed by sorrow, they beat their heads with their hands, stumbling and falling as they gazed upon their departed king.

Consumed by grief and delusion, they bowed down, clutching his feet. Some took his hands and placed them upon their own heads. Others wept loudly, striking their faces and chests, while some collapsed as if dead, driven by intense affection. His wife, Sulabha, wept with a sorrowful voice, striking her heart with her hands in deep agony. With her ornaments scattered, she fainted and fell to the ground, where she was supported by the women of the city who shared her grief. The beautiful queen lamented, crying out, "O Lord, O Lord," without shame or restraint.

She cried, "O Creator, you have no mercy; your ways are like those of a child. You bind us with affection only to leave us unfulfilled. O King, tell me where you have gone without asking me, O ocean of compassion. Every day you would say to me, 'My beloved, I am going to the throne.' Why have you been made so cruel today because of my faults? Forgive me; I bow to you here in the midst of the people, having lost all sense of shame. Take me where you have gone, for I am your beloved who always sought your happiness. How can I look upon the three worlds, now empty and devoid of a son and a husband?"

The Sage said: Sumantu and Manoranjana, his two ministers, spoke there, wondering what would become of the kingdom. They said, "O best of kings, where have you gone without consulting us? Why do you not answer, O King? Why have you taken a vow of silence? Do you not see your beloved wife, who is distraught and helpless? Let us all leave the household life and go with you, O King. Who will protect your city and your kingdom now?"

The Sage said: In the meantime, a wise Brahmin, well-versed in the essence of the Vedas and scriptures, spoke these harsh words: "You are all self-centered; there is no true friend here. The tears of friends only fall upon the face of the corpse, while the lifeless body goes to the earth of the ancestors. In this universe, who follows the dead? This Queen Sulabha weeps only out of a desire for her own survival; she does not weep for the sake of the departed. You citizens are all anxious to return to your own affairs. Have not kings of the Solar and Lunar dynasties died before? Therefore, rise and perform the king's funeral rites. Only he who performs the funeral rites is a true friend, and no one else. It is for this reason that the desire for a son is considered supreme in this world. Therefore, bring his son or someone else to begin the rites, and let everyone offer the libation of sesame seeds."

The Sage said: Then all the citizens, the two ministers, and the women, instructed by the Brahmin, performed the funeral rites. Sumantu performed everything, and all offered the sesame seeds. After bathing, they finally returned to the city. The people ate the leaves of the neem tree and, having consoled Sulabha, went to their respective homes. After thirteen days had passed, they gave garments to the Queen and feasted with joy for many days.

Once, all the citizens and the two ministers became doubtful about who should rule the subjects. At that moment, the sage Mudgala arrived. Knowing the thoughts of all, he spoke of a great elephant belonging to the king. He said, "Let Gahana take the garland made of lotuses. In the assembly, whoever's neck he places it upon shall become the king." Saying "So be it," everyone praised the words of the wise Mudgala, who possessed transcendental knowledge.

Thus ends the twenty-fifth chapter of the Upasana Khanda in the Shri Ganesha Purana.

Chapter 26 : Description of the Lineage

Vishvamitra said: On an auspicious day, under a favorable constellation and a propitious zodiac sign, when various people had gathered in the city, the Queen placed a garland of jewels in the hand of the elephant, requesting it to choose as king the person it deemed worthy in the world. Having accepted that command, the elephant, adorned with metallic pigments and praised by bards and minstrels, wandered about. Surrounded by musicians, royal soldiers, and those aspiring for the kingdom, it sniffed the people gathered in the assembly and then proceeded outside the city.

Women in the city stood with their children and husbands in front, and various men and leaders of guilds stood in many groups. All of them became dejected and returned to their own homes. As the elephant moved into the forest, some others followed it. The elephant saw the son of Kamala (the King) worshipping the elephant-faced Lord (Ganesha) and, in the presence of all the people and the gods in heaven, placed the garland upon him. At that very moment, the people offered garments, garlands, and ornaments to Daksha. Knowing the will of the citizens and the state, the King rejoiced.

Divine and earthly musical ensembles played in various ways, and the gods, filled with joy, showered auspicious flowers. The people then sat in their respective places and bowed to King Daksha, who was accompanied by his two ministers. The King distributed betel leaves and garments to the people and, after honoring the Brahmins, gave many gifts. Having honored his mother with clothes and ornaments, he caused gifts to be given to the Brahmins according to the prescribed rites.

He mounted the elephant himself, which was decorated and moved along a path sprinkled with water, adorned with flags and banners. He went to the city, preceded by his two ministers riding on horses. Bards sang praises, citizens stood by, Apsaras danced in front, and Gandharvas skilled in music ran ahead of him. The sounds of victory, salutations, and musical instruments reached the heavens. Upon reaching the palace gate, some bowed and went home. All the countless kings entered the assembly hall. The wise King sent a royal carriage for Mudgala. Seeing Mudgala arriving with the umbrella, flag, whisk, and the ministers, the King rose from his seat to welcome him.

Mudgala bowed his head, adorned with a crown, at the King's feet. The King seated him on his own throne, and upon being instructed by him, sat on another seat. Seated there, the King honored the sage with the assembly of kings and also gave a cow to the Brahmin. King Daksha said to him, "O great sage Mudgala, today your great glory has been recognized by these people. My physical beauty and the attainment of the kingdom are due to your grace. O great sage, how can I compare my previous state with this kingdom? I consider you to be Ganesha himself, O best of sages. O Brahmin, place your lotus-like hand on my head once more, so that I may remain a vessel of all desires for a long time."

Vishvamitra said: Hearing his words, Mudgala replied to him, "You will never have any fear caused by enemies. Whatever desire you cherish, it will all be fulfilled for you." Then he gave him villages, clothing, jewels, wealth, and the like. He also gave cows and garments to other Brahmins. After being honored with blessings, the Brahmins went home. The King gave many villages to the ministers and the householders. He had a larger temple of Ganesha built in that city of Kundina, replacing the small, ancient one. He then dismissed the assembly and entered his palace.

Vallabha arrived there, having heard the news reported by the people. King Virasena gave his daughter, who desired him as her husband, to the great King Daksha, whose fame was heard in the three worlds, as per the command of Ganesha received in a dream. From her, a son was born, known as Brihadbhanu. From him was born Khadgadhara, and his son was Sulabha. His son was Padmakara, and his son was Vapurdhipta. His son was Chitrasena, and from Chitrasena, you yourself were born.

Brahma said: Having heard the entire lineage from the mouth of Vishvamitra, that tiger among men, King Bhima, after satisfying and requesting the sage, asked the best of the twice-born. Bhima said, "O great sage, when will Ganesha be pleased with me? Tell me the means, O Lord, by which I may receive his grace. When shall I be fulfilled by seeing the elephant-faced God?"

Thus ends the twenty-sixth chapter of the Upasana Khanda in the Shri Ganesha Purana, entitled "Description of the Lineage."

Chapter 27 : The Description of Rukmangada’s Coronation

Vyasa said: O Sage, please tell me the remedy that the wise and compassionate Sage Vishvamitra imparted to Bhima. My mind, which was like one dead, has been revived by drinking the nectar of that story, just as a dead person is revived by nectar.

Brahma said: Listen, O best of the sages, son of Parashara, to the remedy that the sage, with a pleased mind and knowing the essence of righteousness, spoke to Bhima. He taught Bhima the great single-syllable mantra.

Vishvamitra said: Worship the all-pervading Lord, the leader of the Ganas, with this mantra. Perform your rituals in the palace built by Daksha. If Vinayaka is pleased with you, He will grant you all your desires—be it righteousness, wealth, pleasure, liberation, or anything else you seek. Go now to your city, Bhima, and do not worry.

Brahma said: Having been told this, the King bowed to him and departed. Accompanied by his wife, he saw his city and rejoiced. His ministers, army, and citizens came to meet the King; some embraced him, while others bowed from a distance or near. The King entered the city, which was adorned with rows of flags, sprinkled streets, fragrant scents, and the sounds of various musical instruments. The people said to one another, "The city shines today, just as a woman shines upon finding her husband, or as a blind man finds his sight."

Hearing this while seated in his carriage, the King, along with his beautiful smiling queen, both adorned with clothes and ornaments and being praised, entered the beautiful city filled with prosperity. After distributing clothes, ornaments, and pearls to all the people, they went to their palace. On an auspicious day, the King went to the palace of Daksha, which had been built long ago in Kaundinyapura by the wise Daksha. There, he worshipped the all-pervading Vinayaka, remaining devoted to fasting and constantly contemplating the mantra.

Whether eating, sleeping, traveling, speaking, or breathing, the King contemplated only Him with a single-minded focus. In water, on land, in the sky, in heaven, among gods, humans, or trees, in food or drink, the King saw only the supreme Vinayaka. Whomever he saw, he would praise and embrace firmly. The people of the city thought he had become possessed by a spirit.

Then, Vinayaka appeared, took the King by the hand, and said, "O King, you are liberated. Tell me what you desire." The King replied, "I know nothing other than your lotus feet." Then Vinayaka said, "O King, by my grace, you will have a son who is virtuous, golden-bodied, and beautiful. Go to your palace and remain devoted to the worship of gods and Brahmins."

The King went to his palace and did exactly as instructed. He performed the worship and offerings for the gods and Brahmins with his whole heart, praying for Ganesha's pleasure. In a short time, he was blessed with a virtuous son. He gave many gifts on the occasion of the son's birth and, as suggested by the best of Brahmins, named him Rukmangada. The boy grew daily like the moon in the bright fortnight. The King entrusted his son to a Guru for his education.

The boy grasped everything the Guru taught him just by hearing it. He was taught by Kapila, the repository of all knowledge. He became a treasure of learning, like another Ganesha. Rukmangada became very powerful and well-versed in all the scriptures. The King performed his coronation, as he was a repository of virtues, and gave clothes, jewels, and wealth to the leading Brahmins. Rukmangada developed even greater devotion to Vinayaka and chanted the single-syllable mantra he had received from his father every day.

One day, the young prince entered the forest. He wandered about hunting and killed many wild animals. Exhausted, he saw the hermitage of a sage, which was filled with various trees and creepers, and where animals had abandoned their natural enmity.

Thus ends the twenty-seventh chapter of the Upasana Khanda of the Shri Ganesha Purana, entitled "The Description of Rukmangada’s Coronation."

Chapter 28 : The Fast unto Death

Brahma said: Then Rukmangada saw the glorious sage Vachaknavi and his wife Mukunda, who was charming and spoke with sweet words. The king, being extremely exhausted, bowed to both of them. When the sage went to bathe, the excellent king requested, "O mother Mukunda, please give me some excellent cool water. Without water, my life will depart to the abode of Yama." Hearing his words, she, afflicted by lust, spoke, "I have never seen a man as handsome as you, not among the gods, Nagas, Yakshas, or Gandharvas. My heart is drawn to you because of your beautiful limbs. Give me the nectar of your lips."

The sage said: Hearing this improper request, the king, already distressed by exhaustion, replied with the self-control of a great soul, "Give up this arrogance. My mind does not engage in the sin of adultery. By the grace of Vinayaka, I am never attached to such things. I do not wish to drink water given by you, who are so wicked. I came to the sage's hermitage, and now I shall leave, O beautiful one." As he prepared to depart, she grabbed his hand and said in desperation, "He who tries to force a woman against her will goes to hell. In the Treta Yuga, Brahma granted independence to women. If you do not obey my words, you will be reduced to ashes, or I shall cause you to lose your kingdom and wander in the forests."

The sage said: Having said this, she, tormented by lust, ran and forcibly embraced him and kissed his face. Rukmangada threw her away with force. She fell to the ground unconscious, like a plantain tree struck by a storm. When she rose, the king, whose mind was detached from others' wives, said, "O noble lady, O sage's wife lacking discrimination, a woman who sets her heart on another man is destined for hell. My mind will never waver, even if the great ocean were to dry up." Rejected thus, she cursed him in anger, "Just as I have suffered, may you become a leper, since your heart is harder than a diamond and does not melt."

The king, having scolded her in many ways, left the hermitage in great distress. He then saw his own body become white like a crane, afflicted with leprosy, devoid of luster, and extremely repulsive. Immersed in an ocean of worry, he addressed Gajanana, "What offense have I committed against you? Why have I come to this state? How did I encounter this wicked sage's wife? Surely, O Lord of Success, you have allowed the wicked to flourish. You take incarnations to protect the righteous; why was this wicked, arrogant, and loose woman not destroyed? How has my body, which rivaled gold in beauty, reached this state, and due to what evil deed? I have always performed your devotion according to the rules as before. I seek no refuge other than you, O Lord Gajanana. I will not show this body or face to anyone. I shall wither this body through a fast unto death."

Having decided this, the king sat near a banyan tree. His servants ran here and there but could not find the king. As night fell, they went to their respective homes. The bond between the master and the servants became like that of the separated Chakravaka birds. Thus ends the twenty-eighth chapter named "The Fast unto Death" in the Upasana Khanda of the Sri Ganesha Purana.

Chapter 29 : The Arrival of Narada

The Sage said: One day, as the King was sitting under a banyan tree, he saw from a distance the divine sage Narada, the best of sages. The King bowed to him and requested him to rest for a moment. Narada, the ocean of compassion, descended from the celestial path. After worshipping him to the best of his ability, the King asked the sage with respect: "I am Rukmangada, the son of Bhima, and I am very powerful. While hunting, I arrived at the hermitage of Vachaknave. Being thirsty, I asked for water there. His wife, who was deeply lustful, kissed me when the sage had gone to bathe, her mind filled with desire. She told me to enjoy her, as she was tormented by the arrows of desire. By the grace of God, because I had conquered my senses, I rejected her. Distressed and cruel-hearted, she cursed me, saying, 'O wicked one, since you reject a woman who desires you, may you become a leper.'"

Brahma said: "Having heard these harsh words, I left that hermitage. Since then, I have become a white leper. O Sage, tell me the remedy for this. Because of my separation, my father Bhima will also be drowned in an ocean of sorrow." Hearing these words of the King, Narada, who knows everything, spoke with compassion, providing a remedy for the destruction of that leprosy.

Narada said: "While coming on the path, I saw a wonderful miracle. In Vidarbha, there is a famous city named Kadamba. In its palace, I saw a beautiful idol of Ganesha, known as Chintamani, which grants all desires to everyone. In front of it is a great pond, known as Ganesha-pada. A certain Shudra, who was a great leper and withered by old age, came to Kadambapura while on a pilgrimage. By bathing in the Ganesha-kunda, he attained a divine body. Accompanied by the attendants of Vinayaka, who bowed to him, he ascended a celestial chariot and went to the supreme abode, from where one never grieves or falls again. O King, I have seen it myself; go there now to bathe. After bathing and worshipping the Lord who grants all desired objects, give alms to the Brahmins, and you will be purified immediately, casting off your withered skin like a serpent."

Brahma said: Hearing these words spoken by Narada, the excellent King said nothing, as he was drowned in an ocean of joy. As Narada, the best of sages, prepared to leave, the King bowed to him, worshipped him again, and asked: "O sinless one, who attained auspicious success in that region in the past? And by whom was that beautiful idol made of precious gems and known as Vainayaki installed? O Sage, tell me, for I have great curiosity. The minds of saints like you are always inclined toward helping others; otherwise, there is no purpose seen in wandering through the worlds. The clouds rain for the world, the earth is supported by Shesha, and even the sun wanders day and night for the sake of others. O ocean of compassion, what can an ignorant fool like me say before you, who are omniscient and equal to all beings? Still, O divine sage, I ask to clear my doubts."

Narada said: "You have asked well, O King, who brings grace to the world. I am satisfied with your words, and I will tell you everything."

Thus ends the twenty-ninth chapter named "The Arrival of Narada" in the Upasana Khanda of the Shri Ganesha Purana.

Chapter 30 : The Violation of Ahalya

Narada said: Once, I went to Amaravati to see Indra. After he worshipped me according to the scriptures, he bowed down low and said to me.

Indra said: O Sage, tell me something wonderful for my satisfaction. You wander through all the worlds and know everything.

Narada said: In the mortal world, I saw the great hermitage of Gautama, filled with various trees, creepers, and flocks of birds. I saw Gautama there with Ahalya. Upon seeing her beauty, I became overwhelmed by desire. Her beauty is such that Savitri, Sachi, Lakshmi, Parvati, Urvashi, Menaka, Rambha, and the world-renowned Tilottama—none of them, nor Anasuya, Arundhati, Chhaya, Sanjna, or Aditi, the wife of Kashyapa, nor any of the Naga wives, can compare to her. Neither singing, nor worship, nor food pleases me. My celibacy is gone, and I cannot find sleep anywhere. I have come here in haste to see Amaravati, but now, without her, this Amaravati seems trivial to me.

Narada said: O King, saying this to Indra, I disappeared. When I had vanished, Indra, remembering my words in his mind, fell into a swoon, struck by the arrow of Cupid. He thought, "When will I see the wife of the sage Gautama? When will I obtain the nectar of her lips and be freed from the fire of desire? I cannot see any other way to live except by embracing her." Having made this resolution, Indra, the slayer of Jambha, went to the sage's hermitage while constantly thinking of her. He saw Ahalya just as Gautama had left to bathe.

Entering inside, he said, "Beloved, prepare the bed." She replied, stopping her prayers, "Why have you come home today? Why do you desire such a despicable act of love during the day?"

Gautama said: As soon as I went to bathe, O beautiful Apsara, you came there to bathe and appeared before my eyes, naked. With your bimba-fruit lips, beautiful limbs, and firm breasts, my mind, tormented by the arrows of desire, could not focus on prayer. Therefore, I have come to the hermitage; give me pleasure now. If not, you will see me dead, consumed by the fire of desire. I will curse you, or I will leave, or I will restrain my mind severely.

Ahalya said: Why do you ask for pleasure, abandoning your studies and worship of the gods? O Brahmarshi, this is not proper for you, yet I will obey your command. There is no greater duty for a woman than serving her husband.

Narada said: Recognizing him as her husband by his form, nature, and character, Ahalya entered the bed to unite with the wielder of the thunderbolt. Without suspicion, through kisses, embraces, and the loosening of her garments, Indra played with her, assuming the form of Gautama. However, upon smelling the divine fragrance, she became startled and deeply suspicious. She pondered in her mind, "Who is this person in a false form? Will this bring a stain upon me, just as the moon has its spots? Is my entire lineage destroyed by the union with this wicked one? How will I show this face, darkened by infamy, to the world? What fate will my beloved husband, the sage, bring upon me?"

In anger, she asked the deceiver, "Who are you, wearing a false form? If you are not my husband, whom I trusted, I will curse you." Hearing this, and fearing a curse, he revealed his true form, adorned with divine ornaments, wearing a crown and bracelets, his lotus-like face shining with the brilliance of wonderful earrings.

He then said to her, "Know me to be the Lord of Sachi. I was overwhelmed by the fire of desire upon seeing your beauty. I found no peace anywhere, so I did this. Now, worship me with respect, for I am the Lord of the three worlds." Hearing these words, the sage's wife, filled with rage, as if vomiting flames from her mouth, said to the King of the Gods, "O fool, when my husband returns and sees you in this form, I do not know what your state will be, O Shatakratu. You have destroyed my chastity, you wicked and sinful one. What state will I reach due to the curse that will arise from Gautama's words?"

Thus ends the thirtieth chapter of the Upasana Khanda in the Shri Ganesha Purana, titled "The Violation of Ahalya."

Chapter 31 : The Description of the Curse on Indra

Rukmangada asked, O Great Sage, what happened when Gautama returned? Please tell me the whole story, for I have a great curiosity about it.

Narada replied, After completing his daily rituals, Gautama returned to his hermitage. He called his wife and said, "Give me water to wash my feet. Why have you not come to greet me as usual today? Why have you not brought my seat, and why do you not speak sweetly to me?" Hearing these words, she trembled like a creeper. With her head bowed, she went out and returned after a moment to the sage. She fell at his feet in full prostration, her head touching his feet. Distraught and fearful of a curse, she slowly said to the sage, "You had left at dawn to perform your ritual bath. Then, Indra, the destroyer of demons, appeared in your form and said to me, 'I have seen a woman more beautiful than even the celestial nymphs. My mind cannot focus on meditation or daily duties. I have returned for you; grant me your love, O beautiful one.' Thinking it was you, I complied with his request. However, upon smelling a divine fragrance, I became suspicious. I said, 'Wicked one, who are you? Tell me, or you shall be reduced to ashes.' Fearing this curse, the destroyer of strength (Indra) revealed himself. Just then, I heard your voice, O best of sages. I did not come out earlier due to shame; please forgive my offense."

The sage, hearing this, became filled with anger. He cursed his wife, saying, "You have acted with a base mind; become a stone. You did not recognize my true form, my nature, or my actions. Since your mind was immersed in a lustful stranger, you shall remain a stone until Rama, the son of Dasharatha, wandering from forest to forest, touches you with his feet. Only then will you regain your original form." At that very moment, by the power of the sage's words, she turned into stone.

Hearing of her curse, Indra, the ruler of the gods, began to tremble like a mountain during an earthquake. He pondered in his mind, "What should I do now? If I hide in the ocean, a well, a pond, or a lotus, the sage will surely find me." Thus, the wielder of the thunderbolt wandered about in the form of a cat. Gautama, not seeing him in the house, at the door, or in the hermitage, wondered where the enemy of the demons had gone—the one who had defiled his wife. In an instant, the great sage realized his presence through meditation. "I will not reduce you to ashes, for you are the King of Gods, though you are a rogue. I curse you, O husband of Sachi, to bear a thousand marks of the female organ on your body."

As soon as Indra heard these words spoken in anger by the sage, he saw his own body marked with a thousand such signs. Then, the slayer of Vritra was drowned in an ocean of sorrow and lamented. Indra said, "I was taught many dharmas by the elders, but I did not respect or consider their words. My own intellect, which I thought was beneficial, has led to my destruction. The intellect of a teacher is superior, while the intellect driven by lust leads to ruin. Why did I follow Narada's words and go to that blameless woman? I am the King of Gods; how can I show my face to the world? Where has my divine body gone? What shall I say to my wife? Shame on me and shame on the god of love, who has brought me to this despicable state. Living beings must consume the fruits of their actions, whether good or bad. I shall attain the form of an insect and exhaust my sins. I shall dwell in a lotus bud in the form of an Indragopa insect."

Thus ends the thirty-first chapter of the Upasana Khanda in the Sri Ganesha Purana, titled "The Description of the Curse on Indra."

Chapter 32 : The Recitation of the Mantra

Narada said: When Indra had gone to the Nalini river, I arrived at his city and saw the gods standing there, led by Brihaspati. I told them everything regarding the cause of the curse for both of them, the union of Ahilya and Mahendra, and the resulting deformity. Due to Gautama’s curse, Indra became covered in a thousand female organs, and because of the violation of Ahilya, she turned into a stone.

Brahma said: Hearing these words from Narada, all the gods were filled with grief. Weeping in deep sorrow, their breaths heavy and irregular, the gods spoke.

The gods said: He who performed a hundred sacrifices, who conquered the demons, who protected the three worlds, and who enjoyed the auspicious position of Indra—he who worshipped many gods, Brahmins, and knowers of Brahman, and enjoyed various pleasures rare for others—where will that god stay now? How will he eat or sleep? To whom shall we go for refuge now, for our own sake or for his? Who will protect us, the position of Indra, and Shachi? How can the great sage Gautama be appeased? Separated from his own wife and remembering the offense committed against him, he is filled with rage. We see no other way but the grace of Gautama. Therefore, Narada, let us go to appease the sage Gautama.

Having decided this, the gods set out, accompanied by Narada. Reaching the sage Gautama, they stood with folded hands and praised him with various words, having taken refuge in him.

The gods said: O Sage, we do not have the power to describe your influence. Who can measure the greatness of Mount Meru or the Himalayas? Who is the foolish person who would count the drops of rain, the dust of the earth, the sands of the Ganges, the waves of the ocean, or the virtues of Vishnu? You have previously accomplished the impossible, O best of sages; you created another Indra by performing a sacrifice with the Valakhilya sages. When requested by Brahma and others, you created the end of the birds. You drank the entire ocean in a single palmful. A new creation was started by the wise son of Gadhi (Vishvamitra), and the arm of Indra was paralyzed by the great-souled Chyavana. Therefore, with all our hearts, we bow to you and serve you. Seeing you, speaking to you, worshipping you, and touching you destroys all sins. You are devoted to the welfare of others and are compassionate to the distressed. Please show mercy to us who have come for the sake of Indra.

Gautama said: Your presence is not easily attained even by those with physical eyes. It has happened through my own merit, which fulfills the desires of men. My birth, hermitage, penance, charity, body, soul, and vows have all become meaningful just by seeing you. Now, tell me what you desire that I should do. If it is possible, I will do it by the power of your remembrance.

The sage said: Hearing these words, the gods rejoiced, just as the ocean clearly swells at the rising of the moon, or as parents are delighted by the babbling of their children. They all made their request to the great sage Gautama.

The gods said: Because of the offense against the Lord, the God of Love (Kama) was reduced to ashes. You did not take the life of the offender, Indra. Now, O Sage, please ensure he regains his position. By forgiving his offenses at our request, all our desires will be fulfilled.

Narada said: Hearing the words of the host of gods, Gautama laughed and replied to all the gods.

Gautama said: The name of a fallen, sinful person should not even be taken. There is no redemption for a deceitful, wicked, foolish person who lacks remorse. Nevertheless, O gods, I will do what you desire because of your words. If you are displeased, your curse might fall upon me. A creature becomes purified when graced by many. Therefore, I will give one mantra; let them bestow it upon him. The creator of all, the destroyer of all, the protector of all, the ocean of mercy, the leader of the gods, the God of gods, who is the embodiment of Brahma, Vishnu, and Shiva—his six-syllable mantra is the bestower of great success. Once he is initiated into it, he will attain a divine body. All his marks will turn into eyes, and he will regain his kingdom, Indra. I tell you this as the truth.

Having said this to the gods, Gautama became silent. The gods worshipped him, bowed to him with joy, circumambulated him, and after receiving his permission, they departed, praising the sage, to the place where the slayer of Vritra (Indra) was. There is no one more righteous than the wise Gautama.

Thus ends the thirty-second chapter of the Upasana Khanda of the Shri Ganesha Purana, titled "The Recitation of the Mantra."

Chapter 33 : Indra Acquires a Divine Body Through the Power of the Six-Syllable Mantra

Narada said: The gods said to the slayer of Vritra, "O Indra, come out! We are here together with the divine sage Narada. We have gone to the sage Gautama, appeased him, and returned here. He has suggested a remedy and given you a boon. When a fault is committed, good people reveal it themselves, and then they find a proper remedy to nullify it. Hiding a fault increases it, while revealing it leads to its dissolution. Therefore, O Lord of the gods, come out and speak to him. Tell the divine sage that you will perform the remedy suggested by the sage. Accept the six-syllabled great mantra of Ganesha. Brahma once saw the thumb of Shiva during his marriage with Girija; his semen fell, and he became ashamed and hung his head. Maheshwara, knowing this, made him faultless through a remedy." Hearing these words from the group of divine sages, Indra came out of the lotus-stalk, O King.

Hearing the words of all the gods with respect, they saw the Lord of the gods with his body smeared with pus and blood, dirty, and foul-smelling. Seeing him in such a state, all the gods bowed to him, covering their nostrils with the ends of their garments, O best of kings. Then, Brihaspati instructed Indra, who had bathed and performed his ablutions, in the six-syllabled great mantra of Ganesha. Once the instruction was given, he attained a divine body. The thousand-eyed one shone like another sun. Then, the directions and sub-directions were filled with the sounds of musical instruments, the shouts of victory from the gods, and the songs of the Gandharvas. All the gods, filled with joy, showered flowers. The sages, led by Narada, gave their blessings. The gods embraced him with joy, and others praised him. Some of the gods said, "We are protected by you. Without you, we do not shine, just as the sky does not shine without the moon. Just as children do not find happiness without their parents, we do not find peace anywhere without you."

The sage said: Hearing these words of the gods, Indra rejoiced and, with a pleased heart, spoke the truth to the gods. Indra said: "The difficult deed I committed was due to being deluded by the words of the divine sage. I have received the unbearable fruit of it today, and I have been rescued by all of you from the outburst of my sins. I bow to all the excellent immortals and the sages of great influence.
