Please save me, as I have taken refuge in you to uplift myself. How did you make the effort to appease this sage Gautama? And how did he give the supreme mantra for my sake? Tell me all."

The gods said: "Placing the preceptor in the lead, we went to the sage, bowed to him properly, and appeased him with nectar-like, wonderful words. When requested, he gave his own mantra, by the instruction of which you, O thousand-eyed one, have become the source of all happiness for the gods. Now go to your divine city, Amaravati, and rule over all the gods and the worlds."

Indra said: "I will not go to my city without the grace of Ganesha, O best of divine sages. You all go and enjoy your divine abodes with joy, having accomplished this good deed. I have been sufficiently exposed, dissolved in shame, and have suffered greatly. It is because the sage of fierce power was appeased that I have regained my many eyes through your grace."

Thus ends the thirty-third chapter of the Upasana Khanda in the Shri Ganesha Purana.

Chapter 34 : The Description of Chintamani Tirtha

Narada said: O King, Indra, the enemy of Jambhasura, sat in a supreme posture beneath a Kadamba tree, fixing his gaze on the tip of his nose. Restraining his mind, he chanted the six-syllabled mantra. A thousand years passed for the Lord of the Maruts as he subsisted only on air. Like a mountain, he remained still, and anthills grew upon his body. Then, the Lord Ganesha, who is omnipresent, all-knowing, and of fierce brilliance, became pleased. With his own radiance, he outshone the fire, the moon, and the sun, illuminating all directions. He appeared before Indra with four arms, wearing a crown of jewels, beautiful armlets, and earrings that adorned his cheeks. He wore a garland of pearls, anklets, and a highly precious waistband. With lotus-like eyes, a broad forehead, large hands, and a garland of lotus flowers, the embodiment of all gods appeared before Indra, smeared with vermilion.

Seeing him, Indra was filled with fear, wondering what this was and how it had come to be. He worried for his life, feeling as though he were made only of bones and breath. He thought, "What great obstacle is this, and by whom was it created? My body is sweating and trembling like a leaf in the wind." The Lord, who sees all, understood his agitation and spoke to Mahendra in a sweet voice.

Vinayaka said: Do not fear, O Lord of the gods. Why do you not recognize me, O Suras? I am the attributeless, immutable, consciousness-bliss, and eternal one. I am beyond cause, unmanifest, and the cause of the cause of the universe. I am the God whom you have been meditating upon with this mantra, remaining steady. You have been tired for a long time, so I have manifested before you. Pleased by this penance, I have come here to grant you a boon. Know that the creation, preservation, and destruction of infinite universes come from me. Ask for whatever you desire, O sinless one.

Narada said: Hearing these beautiful words, the destroyer of Vritra realized that the great-bodied Lord before him was Vinayaka, the God of gods. He bowed with supreme devotion, rose quickly, and spoke to the one who is the embodiment of Brahman.

Indra said: Even Brahma and other gods do not know your true nature, O mighty-armed one, nor your qualities that govern creation, sustenance, and dissolution. My position, gained through a hundred sacrifices, is mine, yet I face many obstacles. How can I, O Gajanana, understand your greatness? Only he upon whom your grace is fully bestowed, O Maheshwara, can know your glory and have the power to describe your qualities and forms. You are the support of all, yet you have no support; you are eternal knowledge, ageless, immortal, full of eternal bliss, the master of Maya, and the destroyer of all. You are the imperishable Supreme Soul, the cosmic form, and the Lord of all. Sanaka and others attained you by knowing you through fierce penance. By the power of the six-syllabled mantra, I have seen you, O Supreme Lord. Long ago, Brahma instructed me in this mantra by your grace. Brahma told me that whenever I forgot this, I would fall from my position and suffer. Then, blinded by greed and misfortune, I insulted a sage's wife and fell into misery. Now, by that same mantra taught by Brihaspati, I have seen your true form, O Lord. I ask for one more boon, as you are the granter of all desires. May this Kadamba forest be known as Chintamani-pura. I have attained the fruit of my practice by seeing your lotus feet. Now, O Lord of obstacles, grant me this boon: may I never forget you, and may my mind always delight in your lotus feet. From this day forward, may this place be famous in the world as the Chintamani Tirtha. May those who bathe and give charity here attain Dharma, Artha, Kama, and Moksha through your grace, O World-Teacher.

The Sage said: Hearing the words of Indra, which were as deep as the sound of clouds, the Lord of obstacles and the Lord of the worlds spoke in a gentle voice.

Vinayaka said: All that you have requested will come to pass, O Lord. May you have one more boon: remain steady in your position. May you never forget me, O Lord of the gods, and whenever you are in trouble, remember me. By my manifestation, I shall always accomplish your tasks. This Chintamani-pura will become famous on earth. It will be called Kadamba-pura and Chintamani Tirtha. By bathing here, all attainments will be within reach. I, Chintamani Vinayaka, shall grant whatever is contemplated here.

Narada said: Having received this boon, Indra went to the celestial river. After performing his ablutions, he worshipped the Lord Gajanana, who was accompanied by his attendants. Having been worshipped by the Lord of the gods, the Lord vanished right there. Indra then respectfully installed a crystal image of Vinayaka, which was beautiful in every limb. He had a grand temple built of gold and jewels. Bowing and circumambulating, Indra returned to his own abode. Thus, this great Chintamani lake became famous on earth. Even today, by the command of Indra, the holy water of the Ganges flows there. After bathing in that image, one always attains the divine abode. Thus, the glory of this field, which is wonderful to behold, destroys all sins and grants all desires, has been told to you. Go there, O King, and bathe according to the rites; you will be freed from all sins, there is no doubt.

Brahma said: Then the sage left quickly after asking the King, and having blessed Rukmangada with respect, he departed.

Chapter 35 : Description of the Journey to Kadambapura

Vyasa said: O Rukmangada, tell me this delightful story: what did the king do after the divine sage Narada departed?

Brahma said: O son, after the great sage Narada had given his instructions and departed, King Rukmangada, filled with joy, looked at his four-fold army. Seeing the king, who had been transformed from a disfigured state back into his original golden, Cupid-like form, the army was astonished and asked the reason for this change. The commanders said: O great king, we have wandered through mountains, forests, and rivers, hungry and thirsty, longing to see you. At every step, we searched for you until we reached your lotus feet. Seeing your previous state, we were filled with immense grief. Tell us, O best of kings, what was the cause of that?

The King replied: I arrived here earlier, hungry and thirsty. I quickly saw the hermitage of Vachaknavi. I went there and saw his wife, a beautiful woman named Mukunda. I asked her for water, but that wicked and loose woman spoke evil words to me. She said, "Make love to me, or I shall curse you." I, being pure of heart, rejected her by force. When her husband went to bathe, that wicked woman cursed me in her rage. I then sat at the root of a tree, filled with sorrow. By the power of my past merits, I saw the sage Narada. He told me the best method to destroy my misfortune. He spoke in detail about the glory of the Ganesha Tirtha located in the Chintamani Kshetra. The sage, possessing divine vision, instructed me to bathe there. Therefore, I am going to bathe there to remove my sins. If you wish, come with me. After bathing, giving charity according to our capacity, and worshipping Vinayaka, we shall be purified by his grace and return to our city.

Shaunaka said: Hearing this, they all agreed and followed the king. Upon seeing the Ganesha Tirtha, the king’s body became divine. He shone like molten gold, just as he was before, O great sage. Then Rukmangada realized that the words of Narada were true. The king bathed there and gave many gifts to the Brahmins with supreme joy. After worshipping Vinayaka, he saw a mass of radiance—a chariot resembling the sun. The Brahmins and his servants were also there, accompanied by the attendants of Vinayaka, celestial nymphs, and Kinnaras. Bowing down, the king asked them, "Who are you, and where have you come from? Whose messengers are you, and what is your purpose here? Please tell me with respect."

Brahma said: Hearing the king's sweet words, the messengers of Vinayaka said: O best of kings, you are blessed, for you have meditated upon Lord Chintamani with all your heart. You have performed the pilgrimage properly, given charity according to the scriptures, and worshipped Chintamani; you are now fulfilled. He is called Chintamani because he grants whatever is contemplated. O virtuous one, we are also fulfilled by seeing you. O best of kings, we do not know the extent of your devotion. You have worshipped the Lord of the entire universe, Vinayaka, with your body, speech, mind, and soul. He has sent us to you. He was eager and told us, "Go quickly and bring my devotee Rukmangada to me in a chariot." Hearing this, we have come; mount this celestial chariot and come quickly with us to Vinayaka.

Brahma said: Hearing their words, King Rukmangada said: O messengers, who am I, a person of little intelligence, and who is the Lord, the indivisible, immeasurable, unthinkable, pure consciousness, the omnipresent and eternal One? He who is the cause of creation, sustenance, and dissolution, and who transcends all causes—why does He show such regard for me? I do not know if this is the fruit of the pilgrimage or the result of some great merit from a previous life. Because of that, I have had this auspicious and fruitful vision. You are truly blessed, for you see the Lord day and night. Having said this, he bowed and worshipped their lotus feet. He then prayed to them, "My father, the great king, is a protector of Brahmins, truthful, and of great prowess. How can I go without him and my mother, the beautiful-smiling one? She, too, has worshipped the Lord of Lords, Vinayaka, and since her birth, she has known no other god."

The Messenger said: If that is the case, then perform the bath in this Tirtha for them as well. Offer the merit of this bath to your father and mother. We shall then take them as well, seated in the best of chariots.

Brahma said: Hearing these words, the king made effigies out of Kusha grass. He chanted the mantra: "You are Kusha, you are the son of Kusha, created by Brahma in the past. When you are bathed, he for whom this bond is made is also bathed." After chanting this mantra, the king performed the bathing ritual for all his people, starting from the villagers, in the Ganesha Tirtha located in the Chintamani Kshetra. Then, King Rukmangada, along with his army, following the messengers' words, boarded the celestial chariot and arrived at the city of Kaundinya. The sky and all ten directions were filled with the sounds of musical instruments, Vedic chants, and the voices of Gandharvas and Apsaras. King Rukmangada bestowed the merit of the bath upon his parents. By the grace of Vinayaka, all the people who bathed there received the merit. Once the merit of the bath was offered, other chariots arrived by the command of Vinayaka. Each person boarded a celestial vehicle. Thus, Rukmangada, his father Bhima, and his mother Charuhasini, along with all the people, went to where Lord Vinayaka resided. In this way, the entire city, from the children to the outcasts, attained the heavenly realm through the merit of bathing in the Ganesha Tirtha.

Shaunaka said: O sage, I have told you everything you asked regarding the glory of the Tirtha in the Chintamani Kshetra. Any person who listens to this with devotion shall also attain that same state.

Chapter 36 : The Story of Gritsamada

Vyasa said: O Brahma, I have heard the greatness of Ganesha Tirtha and the story of Rukmangada and the residents of Kaundinya. Still, O Brahman, please tell me the auspicious story of Mukunda.

Brahma said: O son, when Rukmangada left, Mukunda burned with the fire of desire, just as a forest burns with wildfire in the summer. She found no comfort in the forest, even with the cool breeze, the place filled with flowers, or the moonlight and sandalwood paste. Laughter, songs, dance, and conversation did not please her. O sage, she was so distressed in her heart that she forgot food and water. Due to hunger, thirst, and exhaustion, she fell into a momentary sleep.

Indra, seeing her sleeping alone in the forest, distressed and overwhelmed by desire for Rukmangada, assumed the form of Rukmangada and approached her. Indra embraced her with joy, and Mukunda was greatly delighted. She, too, kissed that Rukmangada intensely, and he pressed her firm breasts with a firm grip. They enjoyed each other without hesitation, and then, feeling shy, she returned to her home. Indra, having been Rukmangada, vanished right there. Mukunda believed she had been with Rukmangada and soon conceived.

In the ninth month, at an auspicious time, she gave birth to a beautiful son, perfect in every limb and surpassing even Cupid in beauty. At his birth, the sound of his cry was so loud that it filled the ten directions, the sky, the earth, and the netherworld. Birds flew about in confusion. Vachaknavi, leaving his daily rituals, came to her. He did not know the truth about Mukunda’s story and, filled with great joy, performed the birth rituals. He gave charity to Brahmins according to his ability. After ten days, the sage performed the naming ceremony.

Named Gritsamada by the astrologers, the boy grew, and in his fifth year, the sage performed his sacred thread ceremony. He performed the four Veda-vratas for the boy, who, by the power of his Brahmanic brilliance, grasped everything just by hearing it once. He became a treasure of Vedic knowledge and skilled in his duties. Once, at an auspicious time, his father Vachaknavi initiated his son into the great mantra "Gananam Tva." He told him that this Vedic mantra is the best of all mantras mentioned in the scriptures and grants all success. "Meditate on the elephant-faced God and chant this with a steady mind. You will attain supreme success and fame in the world."

Having received the mantra from his father’s mouth, Gritsamada became devoted to chanting and meditation. After a long time, while the sage was engaged in great penance, a king named Magadha ruled in that land. He was handsome, proud, generous, a conqueror of enemies, adorned with jewels, and seated on a grand throne. He was wise, surrounded by a four-fold army, and a patron of scholars. He had two ministers who were treasures of knowledge, and his wife, Ambika, was beautiful, virtuous, and a devoted wife capable of both cursing and blessing.

When the great sages, led by Vashistha and Atri, arrived for the king’s father’s funeral rites, they also invited the pure-minded and ascetic Gritsamada. During a discussion on the scriptures, Gritsamada spoke with great maturity. Atri, in the presence of the other sages, rebuked him, saying, "You are respected as an ascetic, but you are not a sage because you were born from Rukmangada, a prince. You are not worthy of worship in our presence; go back to your hermitage."

Hearing these words of Atri, Gritsamada burned with anger as if to consume the three worlds. The sages fled like deer seeing a lion. Gritsamada then said to the sages in the assembly, "O great sages, if I were not the son of Rukmangada, I would have reduced you all to ashes with the fire of my curse."

Brahma said: Having said this, he went to his mother and asked, "Tell me, O sinful and lustful woman, who is my father? Tell me, or you will be reduced to ashes." Hearing his words, she trembled like a banana plant in a storm. With folded hands and a miserable voice, the virtuous woman said, "My son, a king named Rukmangada, who was handsome and lost his way while hunting, appeared to me. Remembering the scriptural saying that women are uncontrollable, I became attached to that king, and he is your father."

Hearing this, the sage remained silent. Ashamed and with his head bowed, he cursed his mother, "O sinful, foolish, and wicked woman, become a thorny tree in the forest, devoid of fruit and shunned by all living beings." She, filled with anger, also cursed her son, "Since you have disregarded your duty as a son and cursed me, I curse you: a son will be born to you who will be terrible, a demon of great strength who will strike fear into the three worlds."

Thus, the mother and son cursed each other. At that very moment, she left her body and became a jujube tree in the forest, avoided by birds and insects. Then a voice from the sky declared that Gritsamada was indeed born from Indra. Gritsamada then went away to perform his penance.

The person who listens to this story of Gritsamada will never face difficulties and will attain all their desires.

Thus ends the thirty-sixth chapter of the Upasana Khanda of the Shri Ganesha Purana, titled "The Story of Gritsamada."

Chapter 37 : The Narrative of Varada

Brahma said: While wandering, the sage reached a forest named Pushpaka, which was filled with various trees and creepers and adorned with many types of flowers. It was beautified by mountain streams and visited by the best of sages. Gritsamada bowed to them and, by their permission, resided there. Bathing there, he performed japa while standing on the tips of his toes, meditating with a steady mind upon the Lord, the remover of obstacles. With his gaze fixed on the tip of his nose, looking in all directions, he conquered his senses, breath, and mind, subsisting only on air. He performed severe penance for a thousand divine years. When the sage Gritsamada opened his eyes, the fire emanating from them scorched the gods. The gods became anxious, wondering whose position this might be. He stood motionless like a pillar, eating only a single fallen leaf, exerting the utmost effort. He remained with a steady mind for fifteen thousand years. Then, seeing his difficult penance, Vinayaka appeared before him to grant him a boon, just as a cow runs quickly upon hearing the cry of her calf.

Lord Vinayaka arrived quickly before Gritsamada, illuminating the universe with a radiance equal to a thousand suns. He was adorned with flapping ears, a large trunk, playful movements, and a shining crescent moon on his forehead. He wore a garland of large lotuses, was the root of all worldly actions, held a lotus in his hand, and was surrounded by bowing devotees. Mounted on a lion, ten-armed, wearing a serpent as a sacred thread, and smeared with saffron, agarwood, musk, and beautiful sandalwood, he appeared before the sage with Siddhi and Buddhi, his brilliance exceeding millions of suns. His indescribable form appeared before the sage in a playful manner. The radiance of the great sage was eclipsed by the Lord’s brilliance, just as the light of stars and the moon is eclipsed by the sun. Closing his eyes, the sage trembled, overwhelmed, and fell to the ground in a faint, forgetting his meditation.

Again, meditating on the elephant-faced, auspicious Lord, the sage, distressed, pondered the cause of the obstacle. He wondered what had suddenly arrived to cause such disturbance and why his penance, performed until now, had gone in vain. He prayed, "Protect me, O Lord of gods, O Soul of all, from this terrifying obstacle. To whom else shall I go for refuge, O Lord of the world? Why have I received such great suffering, O God? Does my mind burn because I am not worthy of being worshipped in your assembly?"

Brahma said: Hearing these words, Vinayaka spoke. Ganesha said: "Know me to be the Lord of the Ganas, who has come to grant you a boon. I am unattainable even by Sanaka and others who have practiced long disciplines. Cast aside your fear and tell me what you desire, O best of sages. I have been pleased by your penance performed on one toe."

Brahma said: Hearing these auspicious words from the God of gods, the sage bowed down like a staff, overwhelmed with joy, and spoke to the boon-giving Vinayaka with great devotion. Gritsamada said: "Today my birth and my penance have become fruitful. I have seen you directly, the embodiment of undivided bliss, the formless Brahman, who is beyond the reach of the Vedas and scriptures. What more shall I ask for, O Lord? Yet, by your command, I request one thing, O Elephant-faced One. Among the eighty-four lakh species, humans are superior, and among them, the castes are significant. Among them, Brahmins are the best, and among them, the wise are supreme. Among the wise, those dedicated to rituals are best, and among them, those who know Brahman are the highest. Grant me the knowledge of Brahman, devotion to you, and the boon of never forgetting you. Grant me superiority among all your devotees, O Gajanana. I ask for one more boon: make this forest, which is the sole abode of your devotion and capable of attracting the three worlds, famous in all three worlds and worthy of worship by gods and men. If you are pleased, O Lord of obstacles, make this forest named Pushpaka famous. May you reside here and fulfill the desires of your devotees. May this city of Pushpaka be known as Ganeshapura in all four directions."

Brahma said: Hearing these words, the Elephant-faced One spoke. Ganesha said: "Well done, O great-armed one. When I am pleased, nothing is unattainable for my devotees in the three worlds, O best of sages. All that you have asked for will be granted. I have bestowed upon you the state of a Brahmin, which is very difficult to attain. Since you have chanted the Vedic mantra 'Gananam Tva', you shall be its Rishi. You will attain fame everywhere among the gods, including Brahma and the sages like Vashistha, reaching the highest excellence. Those who remember me first and then you at the beginning of all their undertakings will attain success. Without knowledge, the rituals of the gods and sages are fruitless. You will have a powerful son, terrifying to all the gods, who will attain great fame in the three worlds and will be unconquerable by all gods except Rudra. He will be my devotee, his life dedicated to me, and he will be devoted to me. This city will be known as Pushpaka in the Treta Yuga, Manipura in the Dvapara Yuga, and Bhadraka in the Kali Yuga. By bathing and giving charity here, one will attain all desires."

Brahma said: Having granted these boons, the Lord disappeared. After the Lord vanished, the sage installed an image of Ganesha and built a beautiful temple. He established the eternal name "Varada" for it. By the grace of Ganesha, it became a place of accomplishment. Because it fulfills the desires of all, it is also called the Pushpaka region. He worshipped that image with devotion. Whoever listens to this story of the manifestation of the boon of the Lord of obstacles will attain all desires and firm devotion to Ganesha, which liberates one from the cycle of existence.

Chapter 38 : The Granting of Boons

Vyasa said: O Lord of Gods, how did Gritsamada live after that? O Creator, tell me this with effort, for I am full of faith.

Brahma said: Thereafter, all the groups of sages honored him with respect, and they bowed to that Gritsamada, the best among the sages. Because of the boon from Ganesha, they chose him for sacrificial rites, and in all beginnings, they remembered him at the start of Ganesha’s worship. Thus, that sage attained fame among the leaders of the Ganas, and he practiced supreme devotion, chanting the mantra with a steady mind.

Once, O Vyasa, that sage heard a powerful sound that made the directions, the sky, the earth, and the mountain caves resound. As he looked ahead, he saw a fearsome child, red in color, making a great sound, resembling a China rose. He was a mass of brilliance, repeatedly dazzling the path of vision. Seeing such a child, the sage trembled, overwhelmed by fear. He pondered in his mind: "What obstacle has come here? I do not know if this is a wonderful son given to me by the Lord of the Ganas." He looked at him again—the child had a beautiful face, beautiful eyes, beautiful golden armlets, a beautiful crown, and beautiful anklets. His waist was adorned with a beautiful girdle. The sage asked him, "Who are you? Whose are you? What do you wish to do? Where are your parents? Where is your home, O child, O treasure of brilliance?"

The child said: Hearing these words of his, the boy spoke to the sage. "O knower of the past, present, and future, why do you ask me? Still, bound by your command, I say: I am born from your sneeze. You are my father and mother; have mercy on me. Protect me, O sage, for some days. I am capable of conquering the three worlds; I will make the Lord of Gods subject to me. There is no doubt; you shall see my prowess."

Brahma said: Hearing these words, the sage Gritsamada, filled with both fear and joy, spoke in a gentle voice. "If he is capable of conquering the three worlds even though just born, then I shall give him my own mantra, for he is my own son. By this, the Lord Vinayaka, the Lord of the World, being satisfied, will grant him his desires, and my fame will grow." Thinking thus in his mind, he instructed him in his mantra, saying, "Perform the practice with respect. Chant the Vedic mantra, keeping your mind fixed on the Elephant-faced Lord. When he is satisfied, my son, he will grant you all your desires."

Having received the great mantra, he went to the forest for penance. He stood on one toe, without food, having conquered his senses. Meditating on the Elephant-faced Lord with a steady mind, he chanted for ten thousand years. As he chanted, fire emerged from his mouth, burning the directions. The gods and the demons living in the netherworld became afraid. Then, satisfied by his penance, the Elephant-faced Lord appeared, dispelling the darkness of the directions and covering the sun’s disc. He made his beautiful trunk, with its fine tusks, trumpet with joy. Hearing that roaring sound, the boy, as if overwhelmed, opened his eyes and saw the Lord standing before him—four-armed, massive, adorned with various ornaments, holding an axe, a lotus garland, and modakas in his hands. Dazzled by his brilliance, the boy gathered his courage and bowed. With folded hands, he prayed to the Lord.

The boy said: "Why do you dazzle me, O Lord? I am your devotee, seeking refuge. Be gentle, O Lord, and grant me all that I desire."

Brahma said: Hearing his words, the Lord withdrew his radiance. He said, being extremely pleased, "Be attentive, O child. I am the one whom you meditate upon day and night; I am here now to grant you boons. Even Brahma, Rudra, and others do not know this supreme form of mine, which is self-luminous and encompasses the world; how will humans know it? Neither all the gods and sages, nor other royal sages, nor demons, Siddhas, Gandharvas, Nagas, or Danavas know it. I am bound by your penance and have come here to grant you a boon. Ask for whatever boons you desire in your mind."

The boy then said: "I am blessed by your sight. My father is more blessed today; my birth and my penance have become meaningful. I do not know how to praise you, O Lord of Gods, since you are the creator, sustainer, and destroyer of all worlds. By your light, the sun, fire, and the moon shine. You animate the moving and non-moving by your own greatness, O wise one. Even the Vedas do not know your great majesty. If you are pleased to grant me a boon, then grant me this, O Elephant-faced Lord: Give me the unique power to conquer the three worlds, O Lord. May the gods, demons, Gandharvas, humans, serpents, Rakshasas, sages, Kinnaras, and Charanas always be subject to me. May whatever I think in my mind always be fulfilled. May Indra and the other guardians of the worlds always serve me. Grant me many enjoyments here and liberation at the end. And I ask for another boon: May this city, by your command, become famous because I have performed severe penance here. May it be known as Ganeshapura, the granter of desires to the people."

Ganesha said: "You shall conquer the three worlds. You will have no fear from anyone, and all will always be subject to you. I grant you three cities—made of iron, gold, and silver. They will be impenetrable to all gods, and they will move according to your will, except for Shiva. Your name will become famous in the world as Tripura. When Shiva pierces your city with a single arrow, only then will you attain liberation; there is no need to think about this. All your other desires will be fulfilled by my grace."

Brahma said: Having granted these boons, the Lord disappeared right there. The demon Tripura became sad due to the separation from the Lord, but he felt immense joy after receiving the desired boons. He then strove with force to conquer the three worlds.

Chapter 39 : The Defeat of Indra

Vyasa said: O Brahma, what did Tripura do then, intoxicated by the boon he had received? Please tell me that entire curious story in its entirety.

Brahma said: Then, he had an image of Gajanana (Ganesha) made from Kashmiri stone and installed it according to the scriptures by Brahmins well-versed in mantras. He built a grand, divine palace adorned with gems and pearls in the center of Ganesha-pura. He worshipped the Lord with sixteen offerings, countless salutations, praises, and prayers. After seeking forgiveness from the Lord of Lords and obtaining His permission, he went outside and gave many donations to the Brahmins as was appropriate. That place in Bengal became known as Ganesha-pura, the granter of all attainments to everyone.

Then, that demon Tripura, arrogant from the boon of Gajanana, began to rule the human world, intent on attacking the gods. Foot soldiers, horses, elephants, and charioteers from all ten directions came to him for service, being very powerful. Kings became his servants out of compliance, while those who opposed him met their death, unable to fight him. After conquering the earth, he went to Amaravati. Then Indra, surrounded by hosts of gods skilled in various forms of warfare, mounted Airavata and set out to fight, fully prepared.

The powerful demon divided his four-fold army into three parts. He spoke to the giant-bodied, diamond-toothed demon, who was skilled in archery, mace-fighting, weapon-fighting, missile-fighting, and wrestling, saying, "You become the ruler of the human world." To the powerful demon Kalakuta and Vajradanshtra, Tripura said, "Take this one-third of the army and go to the netherworld. Subdue all the Nagas, led by Shesha, and bring them under my command. I, with the remaining third, will attack Shakra (Indra) and all the gods."

Bhimakaya and Vajradanshtra departed as ordered. Accompanied by the four-fold army, he himself arrived at the Nandana garden. His soldiers broke the divine trees despite being warned. Staying there, the demon king sent messengers to Shakra, saying, "Bring Shakra here quickly for an audience. Tell him my words: 'Go to the mortal world now. I will protect you there with peace; give me Amaravati. If you have a mind to fight, then come to me quickly.'"

They went and told Shakra of the demon Tripura's actions. Hearing their words, Indra trembled like a mountain struck by a thunderbolt, or like a tree shaken by the wind. Distressed by worry and wondering what this was, he burned with the fire of anger, his eyes turning red, as if consuming the worlds and drying up the oceans. He told the messengers, "Go, and depart quickly for battle."

The slayer of the enemies of the gods himself mounted Airavata and roared. With that great sound, he shook the three worlds. Hearing that, the messengers returned as they had come. The gods also prepared themselves, holding various weapons and swords in their hands. Some held Bhindipala (javelins), some held Shaktis and spears, others held clubs and swords, some held bows and arrows, while others held maces, shields, or staffs.

Thus, accompanied by the host of gods, the wielder of the thunderbolt went out, having performed auspicious rites with Brahmins amidst the sound of various musical instruments. Tripura, knowing of his intent to fight through the messengers' words, prepared his joyful four-fold army. Taking an uncountable number of troops, he rode out on horseback. The two heroic armies saw each other. There was a great uproar with the trumpeting of elephants, the neighing of horses, the clashing of chariots, and the sound of various instruments.

Then, urged by a mere roar from Tripura, the heroes fought the gods, and a great battle ensued. There was no distinction between friend and foe; they struck each other down. In that fierce battle, many demons died. The gods also fell, oppressed by the demons' weapons; those soldiers looked like flowering Kimshuka trees on the battlefield. Some lay without beds, others without limbs; camel-riders, elephant-commanders, charioteers, horsemen, and foot soldiers all fell.

Then the demons, intent on fleeing, went in the ten directions, just as deer, desiring to live, flee upon seeing a lion. Then, stopping his army, the enemy of the gods himself, with the great flames of the fire of anger, roaring like another cloud, approached Shakra, as if consuming the earth and sky. With his own sword, he struck the hand of the wielder of the thunderbolt. The thunderbolt fell from his hand; it was as if a miracle had occurred. Taking it, the demon struck the elephant Airavata with it. Airavata fled due to the blow. Then, Hari (Indra) struck the demon chief with his fist. He fell to the ground for a moment, then rose quickly and struck Shakra with his fist, knocking him to the ground.

Then, rising, Maghava (Indra) said to the demon in anger, "Now, prepare yourself for a wrestling match, O Lord of Asuras." Then, filled with wonder and arrogant of his strength, he said, "Why are you so merciless with your own life, O Lord of Gods? Even the lives of worms, insects, and moths are considered very dear. Go, O God, I have given you a place on the earth."

Brahma said: Hearing those words, the slayer of Vritra said, "If I do not release you from life, O enemy, then I shall go to the earth, following your command, you wretch. You yourself, with your head severed, will go to the earth today, you villain." As the Lord of Gods spoke thus, the Lord of Demons struck him in the heart with his fist. Then a battle ensued between them, like that of Chanura and Krishna, both desiring victory. They struck heart with heart and hand with hand. They struck knees with knees and thighs with thighs. They struck head with head and elbow with elbow. They struck back with back and foot with foot.

Grabbing his feet, the demon whirled him around repeatedly and threw Shakra far away, so that he could not be seen anywhere. He himself mounted the four-tusked king of elephants. Then all the gods, tormented by the fear of the demon, went to the caves of the Himalayas, searching for Shakra. "Where has the Lord fallen? How shall we see the Almighty?" Thinking thus and wandering, they saw him. Seeing the Lord of Gods coming down, the hosts of gods bowed to him, while others embraced him. Some worshipped him, some fanned him, and some massaged his feet with devotion.

All the gods lived there in hiding. The demon, mounted on Airavata, went to Amaravati. He himself sat on Indra's throne and distributed the divine positions to the enemies of the gods, one by one, with respect. Hearing the sounds of divine instruments and Gandharva music, served by Kinnaras, he enjoyed himself with the hosts of Apsaras.

Thus ends the thirty-ninth chapter of the Upasana Khanda in the glorious Ganesha Purana, titled "The Defeat of Indra."

Chapter 40 : Tripurasura's Defeat of Brahmadeva, and the Gods' Penance

Brahma said: Having conquered the celestial realms, the demon Tripura reached the abode of Brahma. Having heard earlier from the gods about the prowess of the demon, Brahma went to his lotus-navel, and Vishnu went to the Ocean of Milk. The demon had two sons, Prachanda and Chanda. He established Prachanda as the supreme ruler in Brahmaloka, and he himself made Chanda the master of Vaikuntha. Then he went to Kailasa and shook it with his arms. Terrified, Girija (Parvati) embraced Shankara tightly. Then the great demon, desiring battle, went to Kailasa. Pleased by the demon's valor, the great Lord Mahadeva, who grants happiness to his devotees, came out to grant him a boon. Seeing the demon Tripura, he asked him to choose a boon. He asked, "If you are pleased, give me Kailasa today, and you go to the Mandara peak, as is my desire." Shankara gave him Kailasa for a short time and went to the Mandara mountain surrounded by his attendants. Tripura, having ascended the peak of Kailasa, rejoiced. Having thus brought the gods under his control, he returned to the netherworld.

The powerful and huge-bodied demon then went to the earth and forcibly brought all kings and sages under his control and bound them. He destroyed all the sacrificial fire pits, the places that gave satisfaction to the gods, and especially the holy pilgrimage sites. He terrified the ascetics by imprisoning them. He constantly hated the performance of Swaha, Swadha, Vashatkara, the study of the Vedas, and good conduct, being filled with pride. Then Vajradamshtra brought all seven netherworlds under his control. He brought Shesha, Vasuki, Takshaka, and all other serpents—both poisonous and non-poisonous—under his command. Vajradamshtra enjoyed all the jewels and sent them away. He always rejoiced with the serpent women, enjoying various pleasures, and then went to Tripura with the jewels. Proclaiming his control over the netherworld, he received even greater honor. He took precious clothes, villages, and many servants. Thus, having brought the three worlds under his control, the demon rejoiced.

All the gods, living in caves, worried day and night, "How and when will his death occur? We do not know from whom or why he received this boon." O best of sages, while the gods were thus distressed, Narada, who travels through the three worlds at will, arrived there. Seeing the miserable gods, he descended from the sky. Seeing him, all the gods stood up with respect, embraced him, bowed to him, and worshipped him in order. After he rested, they asked him about the boon of Tripura.

The gods said: The three worlds, including the moving and non-moving, have been conquered by Tripura. Our abodes have been taken, and our hair has been shorn by him. To whom shall we go for refuge today? How can his death occur? Tell us, O sage, who gave these boons to Tripura?

Narada said: I shall tell you briefly about the great deeds of this demon. He performed supreme penance for a thousand divine years. He pleased the Lord Ganesha, the leader of the gods. He granted him invincible and terrifying boons against gods, sages, ancestors, ghosts, Yakshas, Rakshasas, and Pishachas. No fear was given from serpents, except for the one Lord Shankara. Therefore, all of you should respectfully please the Lord of the gods, the elephant-faced one. All of you should worship Vighnesha, the giver of all success.

The gods said: O best of sages, please tell us how to worship that wise Lord of gods.

Narada said: I will tell everyone a single-syllable mantra. With that mantra given by me, all of you should perform the ritual with devotion and a steady mind. As long as that Lord Gananayaka does not appear before you, he himself will tell everyone the way to his death. I see no other way, so follow my advice.

Brahma said: Having said this and instructed them in that mantra, the sage, devoted to playing the veena, departed instantly. Then all the great gods became intent on meditating on Ganesha. Some stood on one leg, some sat in the lotus posture, some in the hero's posture, and some with closed eyes. Without food and controlling their breath, they chanted the mantra taught by the sage. After a long time, the elephant-faced one, the ocean of compassion, seeing the long-standing ritual of these gods, appeared before them as the grantor of boons. He wore a shining golden crown and beautiful earrings. He held his tusk in his hand, wore a radiant waist-girdle and armlets, and held a noose, a goad, an axe, and a lotus in his hands. He was adorned with red sandalwood, musk, vermilion, and the moon. He shone with the brilliance of lightning and the light of ten million suns. Seeing the auspicious Lord Vinayaka suddenly, some were overwhelmed by his radiance and became fearful. Some gods bowed to the elephant-faced one, while others worshipped him, speaking with voices choked with joy. Some praised the Lord to destroy their distress.

The gods said: Salutations to you, the embodiment of the ultimate truth; salutations to you, the cause of everything; salutations to you, the dweller in all senses. Salutations to you, the embodiment of all beings; salutations to you, the creator of all beings, O Lord of gods. Salutations to you, the awakener of all intellects; salutations to you, the origin and dissolution of the world. Salutations to you, the supporter of the world, O Lord of all; salutations to you, the cause of all causes. Salutations to you, invisible to those who know the Vedas; salutations to you, the giver of all boons. Salutations to you, beyond the reach of words; salutations to you, the remover of obstacles. Salutations to you, who destroys the desires of the non-devotees; salutations to you, who knows the desires of the devotees. Salutations to you, the Lord of the desires of the non-devotees; salutations to you, skilled in the order of the world. Salutations to you, the cause of the destruction of demons; salutations to you, the destroyer of distress. Salutations to you, the best of the compassionate; salutations to you, the embodiment of knowledge. Salutations to you, the destroyer of ignorance; salutations to you, the giver of prosperity to devotees. Salutations to you, the remover of prosperity from the non-devotees; salutations to you, the liberator of devotees. Salutations to you, the binder of the non-devotees; salutations to you, the one with a divided form. Salutations to you, the awakener of truth; salutations to you, the best of those who know the truth. Salutations to you, the witness of all actions; salutations to you, the Lord of the qualities.

Brahma said: Thus praised by the gods, the Lord, the elephant-faced one, was greatly pleased and spoke, gladdening the best of the gods.

Ganesha said: I am pleased by your hymn and penance, O gods. I grant you all your desires; choose them, O lords of gods.

The gods said: If you are pleased, O Lord of gods, kill the demon Tripura, who has taken all our rights and remains in power. You yourself gave him the boon of fearlessness from all the gods. Therefore, we have fallen into distress; please release us from this distress quickly. We have come to you for refuge; this is our only boon.

Ganesha said: I will remove all your fear from that terrible demon. This hymn composed by you is very pleasing to me. It will be known as the "Sankashta Nashana" (Destroyer of Distress). It will grant all desires to those who read or hear it. Whoever reads this at the three junctures of the day will never encounter distress.

Brahma said: Having granted this boon to the gods, the Lord of the world disappeared right there while the sages and gods were watching.

Thus ends the fortieth chapter of the Upasana Khanda in the Shri Ganesha Purana, titled "The Description of the Hymn."

Chapter 41 : The Arrival of Narada

Vyasa said: O four-faced Brahma, tell me, what did the boon-giving Lord Ganesha, who accomplishes all tasks, do when asked by you?

Brahma said: Then, the elephant-faced Lord, in the form of a brahmin, went to Tripura. The best of the twice-born saw the demon sitting on a magnificent throne. The demon stood up, offered salutations, and made him sit on his own seat. Having worshipped him, he asked, "From where have you come, O brahmin? What is your knowledge, what is your name? Tell me, O brahmin, what is your purpose? If you have any need, we shall fulfill it."

The brahmin said: O demon, we are travelers who know everything and are aware of all Vedas. We wander for the welfare of the people, moving according to our own will. I am known in the three worlds by the name Kaladhara. Desiring to see your glory, I have arrived at your palace. Having seen all your wealth, I am now satisfied. Such wealth is not found in Kailasa, Vaikuntha, Brahmaloka, or even in the realm of Indra.

The demon said: Is your name merely Kaladhara, or do you know what that name implies? If you know the wealth of all worlds that you praise, show me the most magnificent one among them. Having seen it, I shall grant you your desired wish, even if it be my own life. O sage, I never speak a falsehood, even in jest.

Kaladhara said: O enemy of the gods, what would you gain by seeing the wealth of others? I am pleased with your humility, so I shall grant you a portion of it. I give you the golden, silver, and iron cities of Tripura. Dwell there happily for a long time, O demon. It is impenetrable by gods, Gandharvas, humans, or serpents. It will grant you all desired objects and fulfill your wishes. However, when at some point in time, Lord Hara pierces it with a single arrow, it will meet its destruction.

Brahma said: Having said this, he took his bow, held an arrow, and created the three cities, resembling the three worlds. They were filled with wonderful, beautiful palaces, ponds, and gardens, frequented by various birds, and capable of granting all desires. Deluded by Maya, the demon was extremely happy living there. The demon roared like a cloud, shaking the three worlds. Filled with pride and arrogance, thinking there was none greater than himself, he disturbed the three worlds and spoke to the brahmin. "Ask for the most difficult thing to obtain, and I shall give it to you, O best of brahmins." Having said this, the brahmin, though desireless, spoke to the demon.

The brahmin said: I went to Kailasa and saw a magnificent image of Ganesha, worshipped by Shiva, which grants all desired objects. If you are the lord of the demons, bring that image and give it to me. I have not seen such an image anywhere in the three worlds. Therefore, my mind is attached to it, O best of demons. Having obtained it, I shall be fulfilled. I will spread your fame throughout the three worlds, for there is no donor greater than the one who grants what is desired from Tripura.

The demon said: I consider even Shankara as a servant; I do not count the gods. I shall bring that image and give it to you, O best of brahmins.

Brahma said: Having said this, he worshipped Kaladhara with respect and gave him ten villages, cows, clothes, and ornaments. He gave him abundant pearls, precious gems, corals, and fine blankets. The demon gave him hundreds of male and female servants adorned with various ornaments, along with excellent horses, chariots, and golden-eyed silver chariots. Having accepted these gifts, which were given under the influence of his power, Kaladhara departed for his hermitage, bringing joy to his wife and all the inhabitants of the ashram.

Brahma said: Thus, Narada told the entire story to the gods. They, too, waited for the right time, passing the days.

Thus ends the forty-first chapter of the Upasana Khanda in the Shri Ganesha Purana, titled "The Arrival of Kaladhara."

Chapter 42 : The Description of the War

Vyasa said: O four-faced Brahma, what did that demon do after the moon had departed? How was the beautiful idol of Chintamani brought to him? Tell me all of this in detail, for I never tire of hearing the divine plays of the elephant-faced Lord.

Brahma said: O best of sages, I shall tell you everything that the demon did after that; listen with full attention. He sent two messengers to Shiva, who was residing on Mount Mandara, and instructed them, saying, "Go and convey my words with respect. Tell the Lord of Girija that the beautiful and wish-fulfilling idol of Chintamani is in his house; therefore, hand it over to the king of demons peacefully. Everything wonderful that exists in the netherworld, heaven, or on earth has been brought to my house by that demon. Go quickly, O gods, and let us go to the mighty demon. If you do not give it peacefully, then that powerful demon will take it by force, and you will suffer sorrow." Hearing these words of the demon, the two messengers went to Shiva.

They spoke to the Great Lord as instructed by the demon king. Hearing these words of the messengers, the three-eyed Lord was filled with rage. He said to the messengers, "O messengers, I forgive you only because you are mere messengers; otherwise, you would have been reduced to ashes like Kamadeva. What is the use of that demon, who is like a blade of grass to me? Let him come; if he wishes to die, let the war begin at my doorstep. This idol cannot be attained by him even in a hundred lifetimes. Does the fire of dissolution ever find peace because of a moth? Can a mouse move Mount Meru with its strength? Does the great ocean ever dry up by the removal of a little water?"

Brahma said: Hearing the words of Shiva, the messengers returned to the demon as they had come and conveyed the message spoken by Shambhu. Hearing this, the demon, who was well-versed in the meaning of words, blazed with the fire of anger, as if burning the three worlds. He ordered his four-fold army to prepare for war. The army suddenly marched out towards Mount Mandara. It covered the earth like an unbridled ocean. With countless weapons shining like many suns, roaring like terrible clouds, and causing the heart of Death itself to tremble, the demon mounted his great vehicle, which resembled a celestial chariot.

Desiring to kill Shambhu, he approached with the speed of the mind. He wore a beautiful armor made of large gems, along with earrings, armlets, a pearl necklace, rings, a golden waist-band, and a priceless crown inlaid with jewels. His loud voice made the heart of Hara tremble. He carried his bow, quiver, a tortoise-shaped shield, and a firm sword. He shone with a divine spear that the great demon carried. Gandharvas and Apsaras sang and danced, while bards and chroniclers walked before him with joy.

Hearing from the messengers that the demon had arrived with countless forces, eager for battle and driven by time, the trident-wielding Shiva worshipped the elephant-faced Lord. After bowing, circumambulating, and placing the Lord in the forefront, he marched from his abode to the battlefield with eyes glowing with rage. Those heroes, making the ten directions resound with their war cries, attacked each other with weapons, desiring mutual destruction.

The two armies clashed, and a darkness of dust arose, making it impossible to distinguish friend from foe. A fierce battle ensued in which nothing could be discerned. The battlefield was soaked in the blood of slain elephants, horses, chariots, and warriors. When the dust settled, the heroes fought each other individually with spears, swords, sharpened arrows, daggers, fists, axes, and iron maces.

A river of blood flowed, with hair as its moss, shields and tortoise-shells as its fish, heads as its lotus flowers, umbrellas as its whirlpools, and headless trunks as its trees. It brought joy to the heroes and delight to the vultures and jackals. Seeing this river, the powerful Girisha went toward the demon, and the demon, mounted on his chariot, went before him. Seeing their leaders engaged, the other warriors of the demons and the followers of Shambhu fought each other with various divine weapons, missiles, and even trees. I shall now briefly describe their battles.

Thus ends the forty-second chapter of the Upasana Khanda of the Shri Ganesha Purana, titled "The Description of the War."

Chapter 43 : Shankara's Defeat and Parvati's Departure to the Himalayas

Brahma said: Then, the leaders of the gods and the demons engaged in individual combat. Prachanda fought with Shanmukha (Kartikeya), and Chanda fought with Nandi. Pushpadanta, who was valiant, fought with the giant-bodied demon, and Bhirushundi fought with Kalakuta, whose poison was as lethal as death. Virabhadra and Vajradanshtra, both possessing immense strength, fought each other. Indra, the valiant one, fought with the minister of the demons. Jayanta, who was invincible in battle, fought with Bali, the son of the demon king. Shukracharya, the best among those who know the science of weapons, fought with the preceptor of the gods.

It is impossible for me to describe the numerous duels between the gods and the demons, even in hundreds of years. Charioteers fought with charioteers, elephant riders with elephant riders, horsemen with their equals, and foot soldiers with foot soldiers. They fought in a fierce battle, filled with the sounds of various musical instruments, the neighing of horses, the roaring of elephants, the shouting of warriors, and the clatter of chariot wheels. Some engaged in various forms of wrestling, discarding their weapons and striking each other’s limbs.

Prachanda struck Shanmukha with nine sharp arrows drawn to his ears. Kartikeya cut them down before they could reach him and struck Prachanda with five arrows. Prachanda fainted and fell to the ground, his mind reeling. Nandi struck Chanda with five sharp arrows, and he suddenly fell to the earth, unconscious. The giant-bodied demon struck Pushpadanta with ten arrows, but Pushpadanta cut them down with his own sharp arrows and struck the demon with three, causing him to fall to the ground. Bhirushundi struck Kalakuta with five arrows, and the powerful Virabhadra, filled with rage, struck Vajradanshtra with four sharp arrows. Vajradanshtra warded them off and struck Virabhadra with three arrows, which Virabhadra immediately cut down before striking Vajradanshtra with three arrows, causing him to fall.

Indra struck down the demon minister with his thunderbolt. The son of the demon king, raising his sharp sword, rushed toward Jayanta, intending to kill him as he stood among the fallen warriors. Seeing him approaching, Jayanta cut his sword with an arrow. Jayanta then quickly struck the demon prince with three arrows; hit by them, the demon vomited blood and fell down, overcome by unconsciousness.

When all the armies were broken on all sides and the demons were seen fleeing, tormented by the gods, some of the victorious Pramathas chased after them. As the gods were winning and the demon army was scattered, the demon who resided in Tripura himself arrived. Having fought with weapons, both sides then began to fight with celestial missiles. The demon released the Varuna-astra (water weapon), and a terrible downpour occurred. Due to the dense mist, nothing could be discerned. The battle became tumultuous and terrifying, with lightning flashes revealing the positions of friends and foes.

Seeing his entire army tormented by the rain and wind, and fleeing in all ten directions for fear of the falling rocks, Shiva quickly released the Vayavya-astra (wind weapon). Great clouds were scattered into pieces in the sky by the mighty winds. The demon army was whirled about in all directions by the wind; their feathers, crests, and turbans were blown far away. Some chariots, horses, elephants, and foot soldiers fell and were crushed, while uprooted trees and vines covered the soldiers.

The demon warded off that wind with the Pannaga-astra (serpent weapon). Drawing his bow to his ear, he took an arrow made of grass, invoked it with mantras, and released the Agni-astra (fire weapon) upon Shiva’s army. A shower of embers fell suddenly, burning everything. Everyone thought it was the end of the world, tormented by the garlands of flames. From the blazing flames, a terrifying, giant-bodied being appeared, his head scraping the sky. He had a mouth with fearsome fangs, a loud roar, and was consumed by hunger. He flicked his terrifying tongue, which was a hundred yojanas wide, and with the wind from his nostrils, he whirled the elephants in the battlefield. He began to devour the army like a king of birds devouring snakes.

Tormented by that being, Shiva’s army fled, reached Shiva, and cried, "Save us, save us!" Giving them assurance and saying, "Do not fear," Shiva warded off the fire. With the release of the Parjanya-astra (rain weapon), the Lord of the Mountain (Shiva) struck down that terrifying being with a single arrow. But the being rose again and began to devour Shiva’s soldiers. The Pramathas, overwhelmed by fear, fled. Stumbling, falling, and gasping for breath, they trembled. Shiva, finding himself without support, retreated into a cave.

The heroes led by Shanmukha followed him. The demon, thinking that Girija (Parvati) was alone on the mountain and wishing to capture her, left the battlefield and went to Kailasa. Seeing him coming from afar, Girija trembled. She went to her father and asked, "Will the demon take me away?" Hearing her words, Shiva took her to a secret, inaccessible place and hid her, making her fearless and unknown to others.

The demon then arrived at the golden mountain, wishing to capture her, but he could not find Girija anywhere. While wandering, he saw a beautiful image of Chintamani, shining like a thousand suns and adorned with various ornaments. Thinking it to be the most beautiful in the three worlds, he immediately took it and returned to his own place. Surrounded by bards singing praises and the sound of various musical instruments, the powerful and victorious demon returned to the netherworld.

As the demon was traveling, that image of Chintamani suddenly vanished from his hand, which seemed like a miracle. Considering it a bad omen, he returned to his city, deeply distressed and filled with great anxiety.

Chapter 44 : The Description of Penance

Vyasa said: What did Shambhu do after being defeated by Tripura? How did he conquer that victorious demon, Tripura?

Brahma said: Then Shambhu fell into deep contemplation. He worried about the earth being devoid of offerings and rituals. He wondered when the gods would return to their homes free from distress and by what means the invincible demon could be defeated. While he was thus anxious, Narada, the best of sages, arrived by chance to see Shankara and all the gods. Seeing him, the Lord rejoiced as a mortal would upon receiving nectar, and he worshipped the sage according to the rites after offering him a seat. Embracing him, Shiva, deeply anxious for the welfare of the gods and the destruction of the demon, spoke thus.

Shiva said: The demon has caused great destruction to all the gods with his power. Defeated in battle, all the gods have fled. They have gone in ten directions, O Brahma, and I do not know where anyone is. Even my weapons were shattered by his weapons thousands of times.

Brahma said: Hearing the words of Shiva, the sage, considering the defeat of the Lord of the three worlds a great wonder, spoke to him.

Narada said: O Omniscient One, Lord of all knowledge, Lord of all, Creator of all, One with all faces, Destroyer of all, Controller of all! You are the Lord capable of doing, undoing, or changing anything, endowed with qualities like anima, and enjoying the six divine opulences. What can I say to you, who are the best of all speakers? I, a sage addicted to singing and wandering the three worlds incessantly, speak only after considering your words. Having said this and meditated for a moment, the sage spoke again to Shiva.

The Sage said: You did not worship the Lord of the Ganas (Ganesha) before setting out for battle. That is why you, the wielder of the Pinaka bow and the one with fire in your eye, faced defeat. Now, worship the Lord of Obstacles, the remover of impediments. Propitiate him, obtain his boon, and then go to battle with respect. You will surely defeat that demon; there is no doubt about this.

Brahma said: Long ago, the Lord had worshipped Ganesha with great penance. Pleased, the remover of all obstacles had granted him a boon, saying, "Except for Maheshwara, no one shall be the cause of your death."

The Sage said: Therefore, O Girisha, pierce his three cities, which move according to his will, with a single arrow. This is the prescribed way to victory.

Hearing this remedy from the sage, Shiva felt incomparable joy. Remembering the words of Gajanana mentioned earlier, he spoke to the sage.

Shiva said: You have spoken the truth, O Brahma. By your words, I have remembered. O sage, the two mantras—the six-syllable and the single-syllable—which are the destroyers of all troubles, were taught to me by him long ago. Because my mind was engrossed in battle, I did not chant them nor remember them. I did not remember the Lord Gajanana, who removes all obstacles. Vinayaka is the cause, creator, protector, and destroyer of all.

The Sage said: Propitiate that great Lord, Gajanana.

Brahma said: Having sent Narada away, Shiva went to perform penance. In the Dandaka forest, he sat in the lotus posture and began to chant, controlling his senses and focusing his mind. Shankara performed severe penance for a hundred years. Then, from his lotus-like face, a supreme being emerged. He had five faces, ten arms, the moon on his forehead, and the luster of the moon. He wore a garland of skulls, serpents as ornaments, and was adorned with a crown and armlets. With the brilliance of fire, the sun, and the moon, and wielding ten weapons, he eclipsed everything. Dazzled by that radiance, the Lord saw the fierce form standing before him—the five-faced Vinayaka, another Shiva. Seeing him, the Lord wondered, "How have I become two? Or has this Tripura come here in my own form? Among the thirty-three crore gods, is there another with five faces? Or is this a long dream I am seeing? Or has this Gajanana come to grant me a boon?" He was the Lord, the remover of all obstacles, whom I meditate upon day and night.

Brahma said: Hearing those thoughts, the elephant-faced Lord spoke. The Lord realized that the one who had been contemplated was indeed the remover of obstacles. He said: "The divine sages and the four-faced Brahma do not know my true form. Neither do the Vedas and Upanishads, let alone those who know the six scriptures. I am the creator, protector, and destroyer of the entire universe. I am the Lord of the three qualities, from Brahma down to the stationary and moving beings. I am pleased with this penance and have come to grant you a boon. Ask for whatever boons you desire, O Great Lord."

Chapter 45 : The Boon of Shiva

The Sage said: Then, when the Lord of Obstacles, the remover of impediments, was pleased and wished to grant boons to Shiva, what did Lord Sadashiva ask for?

Brahma said: Hearing the words of Ganesha, Shiva spoke, remembering the elephant-faced one as his own true form and the bestower of boons.

Shiva said: My ten eyes are blessed today, and so are my arms, because of your worship. My five heads are blessed by your bowing, and my five faces are blessed by your praise, O God. Earth, water, air, directions, fire, time, space, taste, form, smell, touch, sound, mind, and the senses—the Gandharvas, Yakshas, ancestors, humans, divine sages, all groups of gods, Brahma, Indra, Rudra, the Vasus, and the Sadhyas, all are born from you, as is the entire moving and non-moving world. You create this universe with a focused mind, protect it all through the quality of Rajas, and dissolve it through the quality of Tamas. You are the Lord of qualities, eternal, desireless, and the witness of all actions.

Brahma said: Then, by the command of Shiva, I spoke to Lord Ganesha, and I gave him a name, which you should hear, O wise one. Your name is the first seed of the alphabet, the form of Om, the root of the Vedas. Since you are the Lord of the Ganas here, let your name be Ganesha.

When the Lord of the Ganas was called "Om," Shiva was pleased and granted him boons. He said: Whoever remembers you at the end of every task shall be free from obstacles. Without remembering you, no one, not even the smallest creature, shall attain the fulfillment of their desires. Whether in Shaiva, Vaishnava, Shakta, or Saura rituals, or in any auspicious or inauspicious Vedic or worldly task, you shall be worshipped first with effort. Because you are the Lord of auspiciousness for all people, including Yakshas, Vidyadharas, and serpents, you are the embodiment of auspiciousness; therefore, you are the creator of well-being for your devotees. In the past, because I did not worship you and forgot you during the battle with the great demon, I suffered defeat. Therefore, I have taken refuge at your feet. Forgive my offense, O all-powerful one, and grant me victory in all times of conflict. Those who do not worship you in any way, O God, may they remain dull and poor. Those humans who worship you with devotion shall attain the success that grants all desires.

The Sage said: Having heard this, the one who knows the essence of all words spoke to the Lord of the Mountain: Whenever you remember me, I shall come to your side, O Lord of Uma. With my name-seed as the mantra, invoke a single arrow, and with that very power, destroy the three cities of the demons, turning them into ashes.

Then, the Lord of the Ganas, with a pleased heart, gave his thousand names to Shiva, who had bowed to him with devotion, as they grant victory and fulfill the desires of people. He said: Recite these during the time of battle, and you will quickly destroy the demons. By chanting this three times a day, all the desired goals of men will be fulfilled.

Hearing the words of the elephant-faced one, Shiva rejoiced, worshipped him properly, established the great Ganesha, and quickly built a high and sturdy temple. After satisfying the gods, sages, and Siddhas, and giving gifts to the best of the Brahmins, the Lord of the Mountain worshipped the boon-granting Ganesha again and bowed to him. He told everyone: Let this Manipura become famous in all worlds. With these words spoken by the Lord and all others, the leaders of the Ganas disappeared.

When the sages, gods, and Ganesha had vanished, Shiva also went to his own abode, accompanied by his Ganas. Surrounded by Gandharvas, Yakshas, and celestial damsels, he told the whole story to Girija (Parvati) with great joy. Hearing those nectar-like words from the Lord of the Mountain, the gods, sages, and their wives rejoiced. The great Yogis also felt that the three cities had been destroyed and that they had attained their own abodes through the grace of the Lord of the Mountain.

Thus ends the forty-fifth chapter of the Upasana Khanda of the Shri Ganesha Purana, titled "The Boon of Shiva."

Chapter 46 : The Recitation of the Divine Thousand Names of Shrimad Ganesha

Vyasa said: O Brahma, please tell me how Ganesha instructed those thousand names, for the sake of the grace of the world.

Brahma said: Long ago, when Lord Shiva was intent on conquering the three cities (Tripurasura), he became obstructed by obstacles due to failing to worship Ganesha. Realizing the cause of these obstacles, he worshipped Mahaganapati with devotion and proper rites. Being pleased with Shiva’s worship, Mahaganapati himself revealed these thousand names, which remove all obstacles and grant the fruits of all desires.

(The ritual procedures of the mantra, including the Rishi, Deity, Seed, Power, and Pin, are then described, followed by the Nyasas and the meditation on the Lord, who is red in color, three-eyed, holding a noose, goad, axe, and granting fearlessness, accompanied by Siddhi and Buddhi.)

Shattrimshattattvasambhutirashtatrimshatkalatanuh, Namadekonapanchashanmarudvargonirargala. Panchashadaksharashreni, Panchashadrudravigraha, Panchashadvishnushaktisha, Panchashanmatrikalaya. Dvipanchashadvapuhshrenistrishashtyaksharasamshraya, Chatuhshashtyarnanirneta, Chatuhshashtikalanidhi. Chatuhshashtimahasiddhayoginivrindavandita, Ashtashashtimahatirthakshetrabhairavabhavana. Chaturnavatimantratma, Shannavatyadhikahprabhu, Shatananda, Shatadhritih, Shatapatrayatekshana. Shatanika, Shatamakha, Shatadharavarayudha, Sahasrapatranilaya, Sahasraphanabhushana. Sahasrashirsha, Purusha, Sahasrakshah, Sahasrapat, Sahasranamasamstutya, Sahasrakshabalapaha. Dashasahasraphanibhrit, Phanirajakritasana, Ashtashitisahasraughamaharshistotrayantrita. Lakshadhipapriyadharo, Lakshadhipamanomaya, Chaturlakshajapapritah, Chaturlakshaprakashita. Chaturashitilakshanam, Jivanam, Dehasamsthita, Kotisuryapratikashah, Koticandranshunirmala. Shivabhavadhyushtakotivinayakadhurandhara, Saptakotimahamantramantritavayavadyuti. Trayastrimshatkotisurasreni, Pranatapaduka, Anantadevatasevyo, Hyanantamunisamstuta. Anantanamananantashriranantanantasaukhyada, Anantashaktisahito, Hyanantamunisamstuta. Anantamunisamstuta, Om Namah. 

Om, thus the thousand names of Vinayaka have been spoken. Whoever recites this daily at the hour of Brahma (early morning) will have all worldly and spiritual happiness in their hands. Longevity, health, wealth, courage, strength, fame, intelligence, wisdom, steadfastness, radiance, good fortune, and beauty are attained. Truth, compassion, forgiveness, peace, courtesy, and righteousness are achieved. One gains mastery over speech, fame, and the ability to defeat opponents in debate. All obstacles are destroyed, and one prospers with children and grandchildren. 

This stotra is the means to attain Dharma, Artha, Kama, and Moksha without effort. It removes the fear of malevolent spirits and enemies. It grants the happiness of an empire and destroys all conflicts. It pacifies bad dreams and pleases an angry master. It is the means to attain the eight great siddhis and knowledge of the three times. In the battlefield, it is the sole bringer of victory. It cures all diseases and removes all sins. Even a person of low birth or one who has fallen, if they recite this thousand-name mantra once, they attain success. 

One who desires worldly fruits and recites this stotra of Mahaganapati attains all desired enjoyments. After enjoying these, they become a follower of Ganesha, dear to Mahaganapati, and are cherished by Shiva and Gauri. By the grace of Ganesha, one becomes a knower of past lives, devoted to Dharma, and a universal monarch. The one who recites it without desire, with devotion, attains supreme yoga, knowledge, and detachment, and eventually merges into the divine abode of Vinayaka, enjoying eternal bliss. 

This stotra is dearer to me than even the root mantra. On the fourth day of the bright fortnight of the month of Bhadrapada (my birth day), one should worship me with Durva grass, perform Tarpana, and offer oblations with the eight substances with devotion. There is no doubt that all their desires will be fulfilled. Whether recited, heard, studied, or meditated upon, it grants all prosperity here and hereafter. One who carries this stotra is protected by the eight crores of Ganas born of Shiva. 

There is no fruit attained by all other charities, vows, pilgrimages, or sacrifices that equals the fruit of remembering the thousand names of Ganesha. One who recites these thousand names at sunrise, noon, or sunset, or continuously, becomes a master of wealth, powerful in speech, and highly renowned. Even a destitute person, if they recite this with a focused mind for four months, will eradicate the poverty of seven births and attain great wealth. This is the command of the Supreme Lord. 

Om. Gananjaya, Ganapati, Heramba, Dharanidhara, Mahaganapati, Lakshaprada, Kshipraprasadana. Amoghasiddhi, Amita, Mantra, Chintamani, Nidhi, Sumangala, Bija, Ashapuraka, Varada, Shiva. Kashyapa, Nandana, Vacha, Siddha, Dhundhi, Vinayaka. With these twenty-one names, one who offers worship with devotion attains the grace of the Lord of Obstacles. 

I bow again and again to Him whose feet are worshipped by the best of gods, to the embodiment of incomparable auspiciousness, to the granter of great success, and to the elephant-faced Lord. O Ganapa, Heramba, Dvaimatura, Gajanana, Bhalendu, Dhumrakrit, Bhanu, Lambodara, Vinayaka, Brahma, Ekadanta, Vighnesha, Vishnu, Kapila, Nirguna, Kala, Siddhidanta, save me from the ocean of worldly existence. May the Lord of Ganas, whose feet are adorned with tinkling bells, whose character is praised by the great, and whose cheeks are wet with the flow of rut, destroy all my sins.

Chapter 47 : The Victory of Shiva

Vyasa said: O Brahman, when the thousand names were obtained and Ganesha was pleased, what did Hara do then? Please tell me.

Brahma said: Upon receiving the boon from Ganesha and the instruction of the names, Shankara was overjoyed; He danced and roared with a mighty sound. He summoned His Ganas and the gods and ordered them to prepare for battle. The gods, filled with great joy, went to Shiva. Brahma, Kubera, Purandara (Indra), Agni, Vayu, Soma, Varuna, Aryama, the Gandharvas, Yakshas, planets, and Kinnaras all bowed to Shiva and praised Him.

The gods said: O Mahadeva, Lord of the world, Giver of joy to the universe, when shall we see the great demon slain by you? We have been displaced from our realms by that destroyer of the world.

Brahma said: Hearing these words of the gods, Pinaki (Shiva) set forth with joy. Meditating on Ganesha in His mind and determined to fight, He reached the battlefield with the gods and Gandharvas. Meanwhile, the demon’s spies reported that Girija-pati (Shiva) had arrived with the divine army to wage war. The demon’s forces armed themselves with weapons and armor. The demon, roaring loudly and adorned with battle ornaments, delighted his heroes with gifts of clothing, garlands, and jewels. As the warriors stood in their various chariots, the great demon himself ascended the Tripuras (the three cities), causing the directions and sub-directions to tremble.

Then a great battle ensued between the two armies. With various weapons and iron arrows that pierced the vital parts, a river of blood flowed, blocking the path. Warriors struck by weapons appeared like flowering Kimshuka trees. Some, in fierce enmity, killed their foes alive; others grabbed hair and forcibly severed heads. The dust raised by the feet of running chariot-warriors, horses, and elephants instantly covered the sky. In the terrible darkness, nothing could be discerned. The heroes fought fiercely, abandoning all hope of life, determined to die.

When the dust settled, the dead gods were seen. As the divine army fled and the demon army rejoiced, Purandara (Indra), the wielder of the thunderbolt, arrived, proud of his martial prowess. Seeing the Vajra, the demons and Danavas fled. Indra crushed the Asuras with his thunderbolt strikes, and they surrendered their lives under the impact. Some had broken legs, some broken necks, others torn abdomens, and some had their shoulders and arms severed. The horses of the heroes were cut down, and many were struck in the thighs or had their ankles severed. The blood of the slain created many rivers.

Seeing his army slaughtered, the Tripurasura, roaring slowly, came forward to fight Indra. Seeing Indra, he said, "Why do you wish to die? Go away from the battle; I do not strike those who flee, O Vasava. What power do you have to fight me, O Lord of Shachi? Tell me, will a goat fight a lion? Come, fight if you have the strength, or else leave in peace."

When Indra stood his ground, the demon showered him with many arrows. The demon drew his bow and struck the divine army. From a single enchanted arrow, countless shafts emerged, crushing the gods and Gandharvas and filling the sky. The darkness became continuous with the web of arrows, and the gods fell to the earth, maimed. Indra fell to the ground, oppressed by the strikes.

When all the leading gods were unconscious, Maheshwara did not approve of the demon’s boasting and roaring. Shiva mentally praised the demon’s valor. At that moment, Narada arrived to witness the scene. Honored, he said to Shiva, "Listen, O Nilalohita. You need not worry about the killing of Tripura. I shall tell you the means of his destruction; perform that, O Maheshwara. Previously, he performed difficult penance for Brahma. Ganapati was pleased and granted him all his desires. Whatever he asked, the God gave without hesitation: three cities that move at will, supported by a single arrow, immovable by any god, and impenetrable. He also said this secret: 'By one arrow, your three cities will be pierced, and from that, you will meet your death.'"

After Narada, the best of sages, departed, Shiva made the unconscious gods alert. Reminded by Narada, He remembered the words of the son of Gauri (Ganesha). He began great efforts to slay the demon king. By His own will, the God was capable of total destruction. Girisha made the earth His chariot, the sun and moon His wheels, the stars His decorations, the mountain Meru His bow, the imperishable Lord His arrow, and the Ashvini Kumaras His charioteers.

After sipping water and meditating on the God in His mind, He chanted the thousand names with the single-syllable mantra and invoked the fierce Pinaka bow. When Shiva invoked the great arrow, which was the form of Vishnu, the earth, Shesha, the forests, and the mountains trembled. All the birds flew about, crying out loudly. By the sound, even the gods and humans were bewildered. When He released that arrow, the sky, the earth, and the netherworld were instantly scorched by chains of flames. Seeing this, the demon king, along with his army and cities, perished. The arrow sped forward and burned the three cities of the demon. The energy that was in the demon's body merged into the body of Bharga (Shiva) as all the armies of gods, demons, and Rakshasas watched.

Then a voice from the sky declared that the demon had been slain by Shiva. The gods and sages praised the Three-Eyed Lord. The Gandharvas sang, the Charanas were intent on the Vedas, the Apsaras danced, and the Kinnaras played music. Narada and other divine sages showered flowers. By Shiva’s command, the gods, free from anxiety, returned to their respective abodes. After bowing to Maheshana, the slayer of Tripurasura, the sages also became free from distress and intent on their own duties.

When the demon was slain, the people on earth, proficient in the Vedas and Vedangas, became full of enthusiasm again. They performed Agnihotras, sacrifices, charities, and vows. Shiva, having divided the great chariot, was bowed to by all, including Shaila, Ganesha, Skanda, and all the attendants. Amidst shouts of victory, the sound of musical instruments, and the beating of divine drums, He returned to the decorated Kailasa, the king of mountains, with joy. From then on, His name "Tripurari" (the enemy of Tripura) became clearly famous.

Thus, the power of the mantra of the great Ganapati has been described, and the influence of the thousand names has been revealed. Brahma said: This has not been known to anyone else, nor has it been told to anyone. By reading or hearing this, one attains the fruit of all desires.
