Thus ends the forty-seventh chapter, named "The Victory of Shiva," in the Upasana Khanda of the Shri Ganesha Purana.

Chapter 48 : The Manifestation of Amba

Vyasa said: I have heard the great narrative concerning the destruction of Tripura. Still, I wish to hear where the Mother of the Universe was situated. How did she manifest, and on which lunar day was the demon king destroyed? O Grandfather (Brahma), tell me all of this in detail.

Brahma said: The great demon was destroyed on the evening of the full moon of the month of Kartika (Bahula). The fierce battle that lasted throughout the day has already been described. Because the destroyer of the enemies of the gods was worshipped by all the deities on that day, humans on earth perform the offering of lamps. Because much merit is gained from bathing, charity, chanting, and fire sacrifices on that day, it is known as Bahuli. Those who do not celebrate the festival of the destroyer of Tripura on that day do not attain victory anywhere and their merits are consumed. Therefore, those who worship Shiva on the morning of that full moon have their sins committed during the night dissolved. O Sage, sins committed from birth are destroyed by midday worship, and sins accumulated over seven lifetimes are destroyed by worship during the twilight (Pradosha).

Hear from me the story of the manifestation of Shiva (the Goddess). Knowing of her death from Shiva, she disappeared out of fear. The Mother of the Universe manifested from the mouth of a cave in the Himalayas. She saw the mountain, which was terrifying and crowded with lions, tigers, and deer. Not seeing Shiva, her mind was distressed by separation. She lamented greatly, terrified, crying out, "O Father, O Shiva!"

"How do you not recognize me, O Sadashiva, even though you are omniscient? I am alone in this terrifying forest, crying like a curari bird. When will I have your vision? Have you forgotten me? I am unable to sustain my life or bear this separation, O Hara. Father, where are you? Do you not hear my grief? Without you, to whom shall I go for refuge, or what shall I do? Unite me again with Shiva. Know that I have now been reborn from your womb. Search for that very Sadashiva for me quickly; otherwise, I shall abandon this body here on this mountain."

A fisherman, having heard her excellent words as she wept, went and informed Himavan. The fisherman said: A certain young woman with a beautiful waist, adorned with all ornaments, is there. She wears radiant circular earrings. A crown shining with various gems is on her head. On her forehead is a four-cornered ornament adorned with sixteen pearls. She wears a flower garland rich in gold and gems, and a string of pearls hangs down. Her parting is beautiful, and she wears a golden pearl in her nose. She has beautiful armlets on her arms and bracelets on her hands, and every finger is adorned with a ring shining with gold and gems. She wears a necklace of pearls over a shining bodice, and a golden, gem-studded girdle over her silk-clad waist. On her ankles are golden chains and beautiful tinkling anklets, and each toe is adorned with excellent ornaments. Such a flawless-bodied woman is weeping and is extremely distressed. I saw her and asked her, but she does not speak to me; she only takes your name.

Brahma said: Hearing this, Himavan hurried to his daughter. The wise one spoke to her, consoling her with meaningful words. Himavan said: Why do you grieve, O beautiful-browed one, you who are the cause of creation, sustenance, and dissolution? You are perfect in all attributes, endowed with all power, and sinless. O Maheshwari, you are capable of doing, undoing, and performing anything. You have attained all desires and dwell within all beings. You are not separated from Shiva, for you are the indweller of all, O auspicious one. Nevertheless, I will unite you with Shiva, the giver of auspiciousness.

Saying "Arise," he took her and came to his own palace. Parvati rejoiced there, seeing Mena and her relatives. Yet, she remained full of sighs, eager to see Shiva. Bowing to her father, she said: Tell me an effective method. What vow, charity, or penance, however difficult, should I perform to attain Shiva? I will perform that excellent penance as before, O Father.

Brahma said: Her father, having reflected in his mind, spoke to her. Hear, O Sage, the method that brings success quickly. Himavan said: Listen, Parvati, I will tell you the effective method for attaining Shiva: the worship of the Lord of Obstacles (Ganesha), which grants righteousness, wealth, desire, and liberation. It was practiced by Indra and other gods, and by Narada and others, and they attained their respective positions. The power of creation was given to Brahma by that Great Soul. The power of protection was given to Vishnu by the Lord of All. The power of destruction was firmly given to Shiva by Him. The power of supporting the earth was given to Shesha by the Lord of All, who removes all obstacles. Even Brahma and the sages do not know His true form. He is beyond the reach of words and beyond the reach of the mind. This Lord is visible in the form of the Elephant-faced one. Therefore, He is worshipped in all undertakings in that very form. Worship that Lord of All by the path I have described.

Thus ends the forty-eighth chapter named "The Manifestation of Amba" in the Upasana Khanda of the Shri Ganesha Purana.

Chapter 49 : Description of the Worship of the Earthen Image of Ganesha

Parvati said: O Ocean of Mercy, O King of Mountains, my father, tell me quickly the method of worshiping Ganesha, the Lord of all and the Teacher of the universe, by which I may attain the eternal Shiva. This will also be a great benefit to the people in the mortal world.

Himavan said: O Goddess, due to my affection for you, I shall reveal this supreme and auspicious secret for the benefit of all; listen with a focused mind. Rising at dawn, one should go to the southwest direction, having first covered the ground with grass, wood, or leaves. Avoiding fertile land, one should perform the act of evacuation and then cleanse oneself as prescribed. After cleaning the teeth and tongue, one should go to a river, pond, lake, or well to bathe. First, perform the physical bath, then the mantra-bath. Apply a tilak of clay, sandalwood, or saffron. Wearing clean clothes, sit with a focused mind after completing your daily duties.

Take beautiful, smooth clay, free from small stones and ants, and moisten it with water. Knead it well to create a beautiful image of Ganesha yourself, complete in all limbs and adorned with four arms. The image should hold weapons like the axe and be beautiful and firm. Place it on a pedestal, wash your hands, and prepare all the materials for worship, such as water, the eight types of scents, unbroken rice grains, red flowers, and incense. Bring one hundred and eight beautiful, fresh, blue or white blades of Durva grass, each having three, five, or seven leaves.

Prepare various auspicious offerings: ghee lamps, oil lamps, and diverse food items such as modakas, cakes, laddus, sweet rice pudding, fine rice, and various side dishes. Prepare betel leaves mixed with camphor, areca nut powder, catechu, cardamom, cloves, and saffron. Bring seasonal fruits like jambu, mango, jackfruit, grapes, and bananas, as well as coconuts and various types of dakshina (offerings of gold).

Having gathered these materials, sit in a secluded place on a seat made of cloth, deer skin, or kusha grass. Perform bhuta-shuddhi (purification of elements) and prana-pratishtha (infusion of life). After securing the directions, bow to the gods, beginning with Ganesha. Perform the internal and external matrika-nyasa according to the scriptures, and show the mudras (gestures) as taught by the guru. Then, perform the mantra-nyasa and shadanga-nyasa.

After purifying the worship materials, meditate on the elephant-faced Lord: the single-tusked, large-eared, four-armed deity holding a noose, a goad, and modakas in his hands. He wears a garland of red flowers around his neck and hands. He is the bestower of boons to his devotees, served by Siddhi and Buddhi, the giver of dharma, artha, kama, and moksha, and praised by Brahma, Rudra, Vishnu, Indra, and the great sages.

"O Foundation of the Universe, worshipped by gods and demons, Lord of the helpless, Omniscient, worshipped by the celestials, come here. I offer you this divine golden throne adorned with various gems; please be seated. O Lord of gods, Lord of all, accept this water collected from all sacred rivers, mixed with scents, flowers, and rice, for washing your feet. O Lord of unfailing power, accept this arghya (offering) of coral, pearls, areca nuts, gems, betel leaves, gold, and eight scents, along with flowers and rice, and make it fruitful. O Lord, accept this excellent water, requested from all sacred rivers like the Ganga and scented with camphor, cardamom, and cloves. Please accept this oil, scented with champaka, ashoka, bakula, malati, and mogra flowers, for your comfort. I offer this milk, which comes from the Kamadhenu, the life-giver of all, pure and essential for sacrifice, for your bath. Accept this excellent curd, derived from cow's milk, which is dear to all. Accept this ghee, produced from butter, the cause of all satisfaction, a limb of sacrifice, and food for the gods, for your bath. I offer this honey, produced from the essence of flowers, which increases all vitality and nourishment, for your bath. Accept this beautiful sugar, derived from sugarcane juice, which removes impurities, for your bath. Accept this jaggery, derived from sugarcane, which is the source of all sweetness, tasty, dear to all, and nourishing, for your bath. Accept this madhuparka (a mixture of honey, curd, and ghee) brought in a bronze vessel for your worship. O Lord, accept this water, collected from all sacred rivers and scented, for your bath. O Lord, accept this pair of red garments, which are priceless and very fine, offered by me to cover your form. O Lord, accept this sacred thread made of silver and gold, adorned with gems, offered with devotion. I apply these various ornaments made of gold and adorned with many gems to your limbs as per your command. I apply this excellent red sandalwood mixed with eight scents to your twelve limbs; please show mercy. I offer these rice grains mixed with red sandalwood on your forehead for beauty; please accept them, O Lord of the universe. O Supreme Lord, accept these flowers—patala, karnikara, bandhuka, red lotus, mogra, and malati. Accept this beautiful garland woven with various lotus flowers, leaves, and bilva leaves. Accept this ten-ingredient incense of guggulu, which brings all fragrance and destroys all sins. O Lord of gods, accept this auspicious lamp, which is the destroyer of darkness in all worlds; salutations to you.

Accept this food offering (naivedya) consisting of various cooked dishes, sweet rice pudding, vegetables, fine rice, curd, milk, ghee, cloves, cardamom, black pepper, and various fried snacks. Accept this meal with all respect, including the soup and the water for drinking, which brings satisfaction. When you are satisfied, the universe is satisfied, O Great Soul who is eternally content. I offer this scented water for your mouth-cleansing. I offer this water again for the purification of your hands and mouth. O Lord of gods, accept these fruits: sweet pomegranate, lemon, jambu, mango, jackfruit, grapes, banana, ripe jujube, dates, coconut, orange, fig, citron, and cucumber. I offer water again for the purification of your hands and mouth. Accept this beautiful sandalwood paste for your hands and body, and this excellent scented powder. Accept this fragrant, holy, and beautiful powder made of various aromatic substances. Accept this forehead ornament, colored with lac. Accept this betel leaf mixed with camphor, areca nut, catechu, cardamom, cloves, and saffron. I place this golden dakshina before you for the fulfillment of any shortcomings in the worship. O Supreme Lord, accept this beautiful garland woven with auspicious red, yellow, and white aquatic flowers. I offer twenty-one blades of green or white Durva grass. I perform twenty-one circumambulations; may my sins be destroyed at every step, O Lord. O Supreme Lord, accept these lamps placed in vessels of copper, silver, or bronze. Accept this five-fold light (arati) lit with five lamps. Accept this lamp, which shines like the moon; as its smoke is not seen, so may my sins be destroyed.

After sitting down with a steady mind, one should praise Him with various hymns, suktas, and the thousand names. O Lord of the poor, Ocean of Mercy, served by the hosts of gods, praised by Brahma, Shiva, Indra, Shesha, Parvati, Gandharvas, and Siddhas, O Lord who is the only expert in removing all obstacles, O Lord of the three worlds, please make my devotion fruitful and forgive all my offenses.

Having worshipped the image in this way and prostrated fully, one should chant the mantra that bestows all accomplishments."

Chapter 50 : The Discourse on the Chaturthi Vow in the Dialogue between Himavan and Girija

Parvati said: O Lord of Mountains, I do not know the mantras. Please tell me yourself, so that I may attain the grace of Ganesha and reach the auspicious state.

Himavan said: O Devi, there are various mantras of Ganesha that grant different kinds of success. They are countless, but I shall tell you a few. After sitting in the lotus posture and controlling all your senses, perform the ritual of Nyasa, and then perform Japa according to your desire. For the one-syllable mantra, one should chant a lakh or half a lakh times. For the six-syllable mantra, one should chant one lakh or ten thousand times. Similarly, for the five-syllable, eight-syllable, and the twenty-eight-letter mantra, one should chant ten thousand times.

In this way, people chant various mantras for the fulfillment of their desires. But O Parvati, listen to my words with a focused mind. Accept the excellent one-syllable or six-syllable mantra. Begin this vow on the fourth day of the bright fortnight of the month of Nabha (Bhadrapada). Perform this observance for a month, and your task will be accomplished. You will clearly attain the auspicious state and whatever else you desire.

A single earthen idol worshiped by a woman or a man grants desires, wealth, sons, and cattle. A mortal can achieve the impossible by worshiping two idols. By worshiping three idols, one gains a kingdom, jewels, and all prosperity. He who worships four idols attains Dharma, Artha, Kama, and Moksha. By worshiping five idols, one becomes a universal monarch. By worshiping six idols, one becomes the creator, sustainer, and destroyer of the universe. By worshiping seven, eight, or nine idols, one becomes all-knowing. By the grace of the Lord, one comes to know the past, present, and future.

By worshiping ten idols, one is served by the thirty-three crore gods, including Agni, Indra, Shiva, and Vishnu, as well as the sages led by Sanaka. By worshiping eleven, one becomes the lord of the eleven Rudras. By worshiping twelve, one attains a kingdom. In times of great distress, one should perform worship with increasing numbers. By worshiping up to one hundred and eight, one attains the respective fruits. By performing daily worship of a lakh, one attains great liberation.

One who desires freedom from imprisonment should worship five idols; O sage, by the grace of Ganesha, one is released within twenty-one days. A person who worships seven idols daily for five years becomes free from great sins and becomes a devotee of Ganesha. A man who worships an earthen idol from birth until death is known as Ganesha himself, and his very sight destroys all obstacles. One should worship Ganesha, the Lord of the Ganas, for all desires; no other god is as pleased by worship as He is.

For those suffering from all kinds of diseases, the worship of three excellent idols for nine days removes all pain. Whether made of gold, silver, copper, brass, bronze, pearl, or coral, the idol grants everything. O Devi, by performing this vow, you will attain all your desires.

On the fourth day of the month of Bhadrapada, a great festival should be celebrated with respect according to one's means. At night, a vigil should be kept with stories, music, and singing. In the clear morning, after bathing, one should worship the Lord Ganesha, the giver of boons, as before, and then begin the Homa. One should offer oblations into the sacrificial pit or altar, equal to one-tenth of the Japa, followed by the Purnahuti and animal sacrifice (symbolic).

Afterward, honor the teacher with cows, land, clothes, and wealth. Then satisfy the Brahmins and conclude the remaining Homa. Offer Tarpana equal to one-tenth of the Homa, and feed Brahmins who are learned in the Vedas, along with their wives. Give them ornaments, clothes, and Dakshina according to your capacity. Give ornaments and garments to women. Do not be stingy for the satisfaction of the Lord of the Ganas, who is accompanied by Siddhi and Buddhi; perform the rituals according to your means.

Then, eat with your friends and relatives with respect. On the next day, place the idol on a carriage with joy. Adorn it with umbrellas, flags, banners, and whisks, accompanied by youths engaging in mock combat. Go to a large body of water, immerse the idol, and return to your home with music and singing.

Chapter 51 : The Dialogue between Himavat and Parvati

Parvati said: O Father, I am pleased by the nectar of your words, but I have a doubt; please dispel it, O Himalaya. Who performed this vow in the past? Who told it to whom, and what fruit did they attain? Tell me this in detail to clear my doubts. Those who ask, those who speak, and even those who listen to these auspicious stories of the Elephant-headed Lord are all blessed; their lives, births, knowledge, and actions become fruitful.

Brahma said: Being asked thus by her, Himavan spoke about the vow of the boon-giving Ganesha, which is performed by many.

Himavan said: Listen, Parvati, I shall tell you the ancient dialogue regarding this vow, which grants all accomplishments, along with its history. Once, while the Lord of the World was seated comfortably on the best of mountains, Kailasa, playing happily with the gods, Gandharvas, and great sages, the radiant Skanda bowed to him, praised him, and asked:

Skanda said: O Lord of Lords, Lord of the World, and dispeller of fear for your devotees, I have heard many divine stories through your grace, yet I am not satisfied, just as one is never satisfied after drinking nectar. Now, tell me of a vow that grants all desires, by the performance of which the accomplishments of the practitioners are held in their hands through the grace of the boon-giving Lord.

Shiva said: You have asked well, O Skanda; it is beneficial for all. Out of love for you, I shall tell you of this vow, which grants great accomplishments on earth. It is the best of all vows, dear to Vinayaka, and it fulfills all human aspirations. Without sacrifices, charity, or japa, this vow grants all accomplishments and increases one’s lineage. Whether it is a king, a prince, or a minister, this great vow quickly brings them under control. By the influence of this vow, a man is instantly freed from great and minor sins accumulated over many births. He becomes a vessel for all accomplishments on earth; there is nothing equal to this for pleasing Ganesha.

Skanda said: O Father, in which month should this great vow be performed? What is the procedure, and who performed it in the past? Tell me all this if you have compassion for me.

Shiva said: In the month of Bhadrapada (Nabha), on the fourth day of the bright fortnight, one should bathe and go to the teacher's house. After bowing to the teacher and worshipping him according to the rules with garments and ornaments, one should begin the vow with his permission. O Guru, this vow of Ganesha grants all accomplishments; you are Ganesha himself, the granter of desires. After receiving instructions, one should go to the banks of the Ganga or a sacred pond and bathe according to the rules. Using a paste of white mustard and sesame, along with Amla, one should bathe and return home. Sitting on a pure seat, one should worship the Lord of the Ganas according to the teacher's instructions and then begin the vow.

From the fourth day of Shravana until the fourth day of Bhadrapada, one should worship an earthen idol of Ganesha daily. Observing the vow of celibacy, one should perform this excellent vow by fasting or eating only once a day, or by eating at night, or by eating only what is obtained without asking. One should eat food suitable for offerings (havishya) at the fourth watch of the day, with a focused mind, chanting the Ganesha mantra—be it the six-syllabled, two-syllabled, one-syllabled, ten-syllabled, or twelve-syllabled mantra. One should chant a hundred thousand or ten thousand times daily. One should perform a homa (fire ritual) with one-tenth of that amount, meditating on the Elephant-headed Lord day and night without fatigue.

On the fourth day of the month of Bhadrapada, one should make an idol of Ganesha out of gold—or half or a quarter of that weight—depicting him riding a peacock or a mouse. One should prepare a pavilion and a heap of grain. One should place a gold, silver, or copper pot, and upon it, a vessel of the same metal. Covering it with two pieces of cloth, and placing five leaves and five gems inside, one should perform the worship of the pedestal and install the Lord. Meditating on the Elephant-headed Lord with Vedic and previously mentioned mantras, one should invoke him with great joy. Offer him a seat, water for washing feet, and water for sipping. Offer gems, bathe him with the five nectars (panchamrita) and fragrant water. Offer red garments and a sacred thread. Decorate him with various ornaments, incense, lamps, and various offerings like sweets, rice, and milk pudding. Offer him betel leaves, gold as a donation, a canopy, and a fan. After the waving of lights (arati) and offering flowers with mantras, one should recite hymns and praise him with his thousand names, then worship the Brahmins.

After staying awake at night with singing and dancing, one should bathe in the morning, perform daily duties, and worship the Lord again as before. Then, perform the homa with various substances and worship the teacher. Offer cows, land, sesame, and gold to the teacher, and give generous donations to other Brahmins. Feed one hundred and eight Brahmins, or at least twenty-one if one lacks the means. Give food to the poor and the blind, and after receiving their blessings, eat with friends and relatives in silence.

Shiva said: Thus, O Skanda, I have told you the auspicious vow of the boon-giving Lord, which grants worldly enjoyment and liberation. I shall tell you an ancient history regarding this. Long ago, there was a king named Kardama who was devoted to righteousness. He ruled the earth up to the oceans with his own power. Once, by destiny, the sage Bhrigu went to his house. The king rose, honored him, and after the sage had eaten, the king asked: "O Bhagavan, knower of all truths, tell me: who was I in my previous life? What merit did I perform to attain such a kingdom, free of thorns, which no other king has attained or will attain? I am worshipped even by gods, Gandharvas, and demons. Look at my wealth, which rivals that of Kubera. Whatever I desire, I see in my palace. Tell me by what action I attained this, so that I may perform that meritorious deed again."

Bhrigu said: "O best of kings, I shall tell you through the power of meditation. In your previous life, you were a poor, pure Kshatriya. To support your family, you did various works, but they bore no fruit. Troubled by the harsh words of your wife and children, you left them and went into the deep forest. Wandering there, you saw the sage Saubhari seated in a Siddhasana, served by great sages, teaching the great knowledge that destroys sorrow. Seeing him and the other sages, you fell like a stick on the ground. When you were welcomed and seated, you asked him: 'O Master, I am tormented by the sorrows of the world and the harsh words of my family. My mind finds no detachment. Tell me a way to cross this ocean of sorrow.'

Shiva said: Hearing this, Saubhari, full of compassion, thought of a way to destroy the king's sorrow and told the Kshatriya about the vow that removes all sins. The sage said: 'Perform the vow I shall tell you with a firm mind. By its performance, all sorrows vanish. Brahmins, Kshatriyas, Vaishyas, and sages have performed it and attained the highest accomplishment—the boon-giving Ganesha, who grants righteousness, wealth, desire, and liberation.' The Kshatriya asked: 'Who is this Ganesha? What is his nature, form, and character? How was he born? Tell me all if you are gracious to me.'

The sage said: 'He is the eternal Brahman, without passion, without sorrow, the embodiment of knowledge, the ultimate truth, without beginning, middle, or end. The wise call him the Lord of the Ganas. Know him to be the one from whom Omkara originated, from whom the Vedas and the world came, and by whom all this is pervaded. Brahma, desiring to create the world, performed difficult penance for a hundred years to please him. Pleased, the Lord appeared, and Brahma worshipped him with various offerings and divine gems. He gave him Siddhi and Buddhi as his wives and bestowed upon him the one-syllabled mantra. Having attained this boon, Brahma created the entire world. Vishnu, too, was pleased by the six-syllabled mantra in the past. By making an idol of Ganesha and performing this vow for one year according to the rules, Hari attained his desires and ruled the world. Know Ganesha to be the universal form, the cause of all causes. Worship him with effort, and you will be freed from all sorrows.'

The Kshatriya asked: 'At what time should this excellent vow be performed, and by what procedure? Tell me, O best of sages, I shall do as you say.' The sage said: 'Begin the vow on the fourth day of the dark fortnight of Shravana and continue with great devotion until the fourth day of Bhadrapada. Worship the earthen Lord of the Ganas daily with sixteen offerings and feed the Brahmins. You will attain all your desires.' Having heard this, you performed the vow, O King. When the vow was completed in the hermitage of Saubhari, your house became divine, filled with people, servants, wealth, and cattle, by the grace of Ganesha. You returned home, and your wife, who was waiting for you, was amazed to see the transformation. Thus, you realized the power of the boon-giving Lord, and by the influence of that vow, you attained this kingdom in this life."

Shiva said: Hearing the words of Bhrigu, King Kardama was filled with joy and performed everything as the sage had instructed. By the influence of this vow, the king attained knowledge and detachment. After enjoying the pleasures of the world and placing his sons on the throne, he went to the abode of Ganesha, from where one never returns. O Skanda, this is the best of all vows, which grants all desires. There is no other vow or charity like this. Perform this vow if you wish. It has been performed by gods, demons, Gandharvas, sages, and humans—including Damayanti, Nala, and King Chandrangada. They all attained their desires and reached the abode of Ganesha.

Himavan said: Thus, I have told you everything about this great vow along with its history. Meditate on the boon-giving Lord of the Ganas and perform it. By this vow, O noble one, you shall attain Shankara. I have told you this out of love; do not reveal this vow to the unworthy.

Chapter 52 : The Description of Nala's Vow

Parvati asked: O Father, why did Nala perform this vow? Who was Nala? Tell me, for my mind finds peace in listening to his story.

Himavan replied: In ancient times, there was a great king named Nala in the land of Nishadha. He was a devotee of Brahma, a knower of the Vedas, a hero, a generous donor, a wise man, and wealthy. He was skilled in chariot warfare, proficient with the sword, bow, and arrow, and always protected by his armor. He was powerful, honored by the gods, capable of traveling through the three worlds, and pure of heart. Even Shesha (the serpent) falls silent when attempting to describe his virtues. There was no count to his horses, elephants, or charioteers. Because of his fear, even the gods like Indra and the guardians of the directions trembled.

He had a wife named Damayanti, who was a temple of beauty. Brahma, having created all beings and gathered the essence of beauty from everywhere, created her. Because she was created by him, she is known as Damayanti, and she surpassed all women in the three worlds in beauty. She was adorned with various ornaments, bedecked with many jewels, wore a necklace of pearls, and was full of virtues and grace.

Nala had a minister named Padma-hasta, who was heroic, equal to Brihaspati in intellect, and equal to Angira in statesmanship. In stature, he was like Mount Meru, and in depth of character, he was like the ocean. Once, the great-minded King Nala was sitting in his assembly hall, surrounded by other kings. Beautiful Apsaras were dancing before him, and bards were singing his praises in the presence of the Brahmarshis. At that very moment, the sage Gautama arrived. The king rose respectfully, offered him a seat, and worshipped him with great devotion. Then Nala asked him:

Nala said: O Master, O Great Sage, I am blessed by your presence. My birth, my kingdom, my parents, my lineage, and my life have all become meaningful today. Please tell me the reason for your arrival.

Gautama replied: O King, I had a great desire to see your glory. The gods in heaven—Brahma, Indra, Vishnu, and Shiva—all sing your praises. You are blessed to be in the mortal world, praised by both mortals and gods. I am always satisfied, but seeing your worship and your glory, I am even more content. Now, give me permission; I shall return to my hermitage.

The King said: O Knower of the Vedas and Vedangas, O Preceptor of all scriptures, O Ocean of Mercy, stay for a moment and resolve my doubt.

The Sage said: Well asked, O Great King. I shall stay out of affection. Even the Nagas, kings, and gods do not transgress your command.

The King said: O Brahmin, I am amazed myself when I look at my own glory. By what merit or penance did I attain this? Tell me truly, who was I in my previous life?

The Sage said: Beyond the land of Gauda, in a city called Pippala, you were a Kshatriya in your previous life—poor, yet wise and pure. You were severely taunted by your wife, children, and friends with sharp words. One night, without asking anyone, out of despair, you went into a dense forest. You wandered through forests filled with trees, vines, lions, tigers, elephants, and deer, and saw lakes with cool water. While wandering, you saw an ashram. It was the hermitage of the ascetic Kaushika, filled with the chanting of the Vedas. You went there and bowed to the sage with devotion. Kaushika, compassionate to the poor and helpless, lifted you up. Knowing you were suffering, he gave you a blessing. He said, "The elephant-faced Lord (Ganesha) will be your benefactor." Hearing that gentle blessing, you were filled with supreme joy. You asked the Brahmin for the means to fulfill all desires—the destroyer of poverty, the giver of worldly enjoyment and liberation. Kaushika then told you about the worship of Ganesha.

Kaushika said: O King, perform the vow of Ganesha for one month. Make an earthen image of Ganesha and worship it according to the prescribed method. Listen to the story daily for a month, and you will attain success.

The Sage said: Hearing this, the king asked Kaushika again.

The King said: I do not know the elephant-faced Lord. Tell me his true form so that I may worship him.

The Sage said: When asked, the great sage spoke of the Supreme Brahman, whose form is indescribable, and explained how the elephant-faced Lord is the creator of all the manifestations of the world.

Kaushika said: He who is the creator of all worlds, the father, mother, and teacher of the universe, he who is to be meditated upon by Brahma, Indra, Shiva, and Vishnu—that is the elephant-faced Lord.

The Sage said: Hearing these words, you bowed to the sage, took his leave, and returned home. Starting from the fourth day of the bright fortnight of Shravana, you performed the excellent vow. You made an earthen image of Ganesha as instructed. Whether standing, speaking, silent, walking, sleeping, or eating, you meditated on the elephant-faced Lord and attained supreme success. By the power of this vow, you became wealthy, possessed of many elephants, chariots, horses, cattle, and servants. You gave gifts to satisfy the gods and joyfully built a magnificent palace for Ganesha. After enjoying life as you wished, you passed away and were born in the land of Nishadha as King Nala. That is why wealth remains with you, and you are honored by the three worlds. Now, give me permission; I have answered what you asked.

Himavan said: After Gautama left, King Nala performed the vow. Trusting the sage's words, he made a beautiful image, listened to the story of Ganesha daily, worshipped him with devotion, and attained all his desires through the power of this vow. Thus, O daughter, I have told you how Nala performed the vow taught by Gautama in his previous life, the power of which no one can fully describe.

Chapter 53 : The Tale of Chandrangada

Himavan said: Now I shall narrate what King Chandrangada and his wife Indumati did, O beautiful one. In the region of Malwa, there was a famous city named Karna. There lived King Chandrangada, who was extremely valiant. Endowed with qualities like anima (the power to become minute), he was a knower of the essence of all scriptures. He was a performer of sacrifices, a donor, highly learned, and well-versed in the Vedas and their auxiliary branches. His court surpassed the divine Sudharma assembly in splendor, and the brilliance of his jewels dazzled the eyes of those who beheld them. The garments in his palace were so pure that they appeared multicolored due to the reflection of the jeweled pillars.

His queen, Indumati, was virtuous, highly fortunate, and devoted to serving her husband. She was righteous, observant of vows, and served her parents-in-law with dedication. She was diligent in household duties, worshipped the gods and guests, and was beautiful in every limb. Although the King was righteous, his ministers often urged him to avoid the great sin of animal slaughter. Once, by destiny, he went hunting in a forest filled with hyenas, wolves, boars, deer, and birds. Clad in a blue tunic and turban, wearing leather finger guards, and armed with a sword and shield, the powerful King rode his swift horse, holding his bow and arrows. Surrounded by heroic ministers and servants, he hunted deer and boars, sending them back to the city.

While wandering in the forest, the King was spotted by powerful demons. Seeing them—with faces like mountain caves, sunken eyes, gaping mouths, and reaching the sky—the King trembled as if struck by a shivering fever. Seeing them, all the heroes and servants fled; some perished, and others fell unconscious. A cruel demoness, seeing the handsome King, was overcome with lust, embraced him, and kissed him. She devoured his ministers and servants. In the meantime, the King fled and plunged into a lake. The demoness could not see him there. He was seized by serpent-maidens (Nagakanyas) and taken to their palace in the netherworld.

Adorned by the serpent-maidens with garments and ornaments, he was asked, "Where have you come from? Who are you, whose son are you, and what is your name? Tell us the truth, O best of men." Hearing this, the King replied, "I am Chandrangada, the powerful son of Hemangada, residing in the city of Karna in the Malwa region. Terrified by the demoness, I entered the deep water and was brought here by you. I have told you what you asked. My people were devoured while I was hunting; I am alive now only by the grace of the water." Hearing this, they said to the King, "Be our husband, and all your desires will be fulfilled. The company of us serpent-maidens is very rare."

Hearing their words, the noble King said, "I have taken a vow of monogamy; how can I abandon it, O mothers? I shall tell you the duties of kings born in the Solar dynasty. Righteous kings and gentle sages do not desire another's wealth, another's harm, or another's wife. They do not desire slander. Study, sacrifice, charity, and protecting those who seek refuge are the duties of the three upper castes, and the three duties starting with sacrifice are for the twice-born. Hospitality is considered the highest duty for all castes." Hearing the King's words, all the serpent-maidens cursed him, saying, "May you be separated from your wife for a long time." Distressed and overcome with lust, they bound him in chains.

The demoness, having consumed the water and the aquatic creatures in the lake, was still not satisfied. The lotus-eyed Queen heard this news from a messenger who had escaped the demoness. Hearing that the King had vanished, she fell to the ground in grief and fainted. Her companions fanned her and sprinkled her with cool water. Weeping, she sat and struck her chest and head, lamenting, "Where has my beloved husband gone, leaving me, who always spoke kindly to him? Where will my lord eat or sleep? Leaving behind his golden bed covered with precious spreads, how will my beloved bathe without his fragrant oils? Who will protect the people without him? Who will delight the subjects? Has the treasure of virtues and valor set today? I see the directions as empty without that great soul. Where shall I find happiness, and where will he go? There is no happiness for women in this world or the next without their husbands. Who will protect those who seek refuge and the destitute?"

Lamenting in this way, deeply distressed, she tore off her ornaments, broke her bracelets, and fainted.

Chapter 54 : Dialogue between Indumati and Narada.

Parvati said: O Father, tell me in detail what the people did when she fainted, for it brings joy to my heart.

Himavan said: At that time, all the citizens, skilled in various forms of speech, wiped away their tears and comforted the queen.

The people said: O Mother, arise! Do not grieve, and do not let your mind dwell on your son. Tears of grief burn the departed soul; therefore, do what is beneficial for your husband. O beautiful-faced one, no one is seen to be immortal among mortals. Just as people discard worn-out garments and take new ones, the embodied soul similarly discards the body and assumes another, which is auspicious. O noble lady, it is a great wonder that a person who is himself subject to death grieves for another who has died, not realizing that his own body is also destined for separation. Everything in this world, from Brahma down to the stationary objects, including the oceans and all living beings, is subject to the control of fate and time. Knowing this to be perishable, abandon your grief and arise, O noble daughter. Your husband, who was virtuous and pious, must have attained liberation. If he is alive in heaven, he will eventually go there. Even a person of great merit who goes to heaven must return. Or, let us ask some sage who has arrived here; he knows the past and the future and will tell us everything. We shall then do whatever is necessary.

Himavan said: Thus instructed by the people, Indumati was comforted for a moment. She wiped her tears with her garment and sent away all the people who had gathered. Abandoning all signs of her auspicious married state, she became extremely emaciated. She remained weeping, grieving, sighing, and fainting repeatedly. Then, after twelve years, the divine sage Narada, wandering at will, came to her house. Seeing him, she lamented immediately, speaking of her husband's deeds, and told him of the twelve years of suffering that had passed. Hearing her weeping, the sage spoke to gladden her.

Narada said: Your husband is staying somewhere; you should not grieve for him. Cover your head with a blue cloth and wear earrings. Adorn your ears and forehead with auspicious vermilion, wear bangles on your wrists, and a mangalsutra around your neck.

Himavan said: Trusting the words of the omniscient and truthful sage, she immediately had everything brought and, feeling joyful, did as he said. Having called all the Brahmins and worshipped Narada first, she worshipped them all and gave many gifts. She had musical instruments played and sent sweets to every house. With joy, the auspicious Indumati gave orders to the people by distributing clothes and betel leaves. Bowing down again to the divine sage Narada with reverence, the princess asked for the means to attain her husband.

Indumati said: O Sage, where is my husband, and how is he? By what means, O knower of the Vedas, can I have his vision? Please show me grace, O Sage, and tell me that means—be it a vow, charity, or penance, even if it is difficult to perform.

Narada said: I shall tell you briefly about the supreme vow. Begin it with great joy on the fourth day of the bright fortnight of the month of Nabha (Shravana). In the morning, after brushing your teeth, bathe in a river, pond, or well with a firm resolve. Wearing clean white clothes, go home and make a beautiful earthen image of Ganesha with four arms. Worship Him with the sixteen offerings with a steady mind. Either eat once a day or fast until the fourth day of the month of Bhadra, according to your ability. If you have the means, celebrate a great festival with music, dance, and by feeding Brahmins. Perform this vow in this way, O auspicious one, and you shall be reunited with your husband, who is currently held captive by the Naga maidens in the netherworld. I tell you the truth; my words will not be otherwise.

Himavan said: Thus instructed by the sage, she began the vow with reverence. When the sage had departed and some days of the month of Nabha had passed, she made a beautiful earthen image of the elephant-headed Lord. She performed the worship in the aforementioned manner with divine fragrances, divine garments, divine flowers, various divine offerings, fruits, gold, lamps, handfuls of flowers, prostrations, and hymns. She pleased the Lord through the remembrance of His name and meditation, accompanied by music, dance, and the feeding of Brahmins. She performed this supreme vow, eating only a small amount of milk, from the fourth day of the bright fortnight of Nabha until the fourth day of Bhadra, as per the words of the sage Narada, for the sake of regaining her long-lost beloved.

Chapter 55 : The Union of Shiva and Parvati

Himavan said: When her vow was completed through the grace of Ganesha, the minds of the Naga maidens in the netherworld changed. They released the king and worshipped him according to the prescribed rites, bestowing upon him garments, ornaments, and vast wealth of various gems. Giving him a horse as swift as thought, they sent the king away. Upon emerging from the lake and tying his horse to a great tree, the king bathed. While doing so, he was seen by some citizens who approached him, bowed, and asked who he was. They inquired about his origin, where he had come from, and his name. Hearing their words, the noble king, filled with sorrow, asked after the welfare of Indumati and his son. The people then recognized him and embraced him with joy. They told the king that his wife, having completed her fasting vow, had just returned home, thin and frail, clinging to life only for the sake of their son. Some went to the city and announced the auspicious news. Hearing of the king’s arrival from the lips of trusted people, she was immersed in an ocean of bliss, like a yogi who has attained the knowledge of Brahman.

She then sent ministers and soldiers to escort him, and she adorned the city with colorful banners and flags. She had the streets sprinkled with water, prepared the assembly halls with great care, and adorned herself with fine clothes and jewelry. After satisfying many Brahmins with gifts of cows, land, and gold, she took a lamp in her hand to perform the auspicious welcome, just as a wife does for her husband. Accompanied by the sound of music and singing, she approached the king. The ministers went ahead, bowed to the noble king, and embraced him with joy. When the king sat down, they sat at his command. After satisfying everyone with inquiries about their welfare and honoring the people with betel leaves and garments, the king himself went to the camp of Indumati. He performed the rituals with Brahmins who had seen twelve years of study. After the auspicious prayers and the worship of Ganesha, he worshipped Lord Shiva properly and gave gifts to the Brahmins. He broke a coconut and went before her, seeing Indumati there like the remaining digit of the moon.

After she and the other young women performed the ritual of waving lights before the king, she showered him with parched grain and flowers. Wiping away her eyes, which were flooded with tears of joy, they spoke to each other, filled with both happiness and sorrow. They recounted the agony of their separation, but were consoled by the wise words of those whose nature could not be swayed. They brought the king and seated him upon a great elephant, adorned with various ornaments, banners, and a canopy. Surrounded by foot soldiers and shining with four bells, a hundred men carrying staffs went before him to clear the way. Many others followed, carrying weapons and riding horses and chariots. A hundred thousand elephants accompanied the king on his left and right. Dancers, musicians, and bards went ahead, followed by the elephant corps as they entered the city with great respect. The city was so filled with the dust raised by the army that the sun’s brilliance was obscured, and in the decorated city, nothing else could be seen.

Then, bowing to each other, they went to their respective homes. The prominent guests were taken to the royal palace and honored by the king. After giving them garments and betel leaves, he sent them home. The king then fed the Brahmins and dined with his kinsmen. That night, they slept on a beautifully crafted bed covered with precious spreads and pillows. They spoke of their sorrows, lamenting again and again, until they were consoled by the priest and fell into a peaceful sleep. Having heard of the greatness of the Vinayaka vow, the noble king decided to perform it himself, just as his wife had experienced. When the month of Shravana arrived, King Chandrangada performed this vow with a great festival.

The Sage said: Hearing these words of his father, Parvati was filled with great joy. When the month of Nabhas arrived, she performed the vow with devotion. Following the prescribed rites, she made an image, worshipped it with effort, consumed only milk, and meditated upon the Elephant-headed Lord. Then, the mind of Shiva became restless, and the wielder of the trident himself arrived at Parvati’s hermitage. On the fourth day of Ganesha, when the auspicious vow was complete, the Goddess saw Hara mounted on his bull in her hermitage. She rose and bowed to his lotus feet with joy, worshipping Shiva, the benefactor of the world, according to the rites. Filled with love, Parvati spoke to Mahadeva.

The Goddess said: Why did you leave me? Have you forgotten me? Every moment of separation from you felt like a kalpa, O Lord. I performed this vow of Ganesha as instructed by my father, and through the grace of the Giver of Boons, I have attained your vision.

Brahma said: At that very moment, Himavan arrived and respectfully placed the hand of Sati into the hand of Shiva. Then, the gods and Gandharvas respectfully worshipped the Elephant-headed Lord and the divine couple, Shiva and Parvati, who bring auspiciousness to the virtuous. Divine drums sounded, and a shower of flowers fell. Everyone bowed to the Elephant-headed Lord and praised him with various hymns. With shouts of victory, Shiva also praised the Elephant-headed Lord. Placing Parvati on his left side, he mounted his bull and quickly departed for the peak of Kailasa, while everyone else returned to their own abodes.

Brahma said: Thus, O Vyasa, I have told you everything you asked regarding the greatness of the Lord of the Ganas and his vow. Now, I shall tell you another story, by hearing which a man is freed from all sins and attains his desires.

Chapter 56 : Through the Power of Chanting Ganesha's Name, the Sinful Weaver Assumes the Form of Bhrushundi

Bhrigu said: O King, I have told you the entire greatness as I heard it from the mouth of Brahma through Vyasa. Listen to it again.

Somakanta said: O great sage, how did Vyasa, who possesses infinite wisdom, hear it from the mouth of Brahma? I am not satisfied, so please tell me.

Bhrigu said: Having heard the story, Vyasa asked Brahma with reverence, and O sinless one, Brahma also narrated it to him as he asked with respect.

Vyasa said: O Brahma, tell me again the supreme story of the Lord of the Ganas (Ganesha). My thirst to hear the excellent story of the Lord of Obstacles increases more and more.

Brahma said: O Vyasa, listen to another story of mine, full of wonder, which was experienced by Shurasena and others regarding the Elephant-faced Lord.

In the middle region, there was a king named Shurasena, who was very powerful. He lived in the beautiful city of Sahasra, and he was well-versed in the Vedas and their auxiliary branches. He was wealthy, handsome, generous, a performer of sacrifices, and a protector of the people. He possessed the three powers of kingship, was honorable, and was established in the six qualities of statecraft. He was clever in employing the four political expedients, a destroyer of enemies, endowed with a four-fold army, and devoted to the twice-born and the gods. The entire circle of the earth was always under his control, and his city shone on the earth as superior even to the city of Indra.

He had a virtuous wife named Punyashalini, whose beauty had no equal in the three worlds. By the virtues of her chastity, even Arundhati felt shy, and by her practice of renouncing envy, she surpassed even Anasuya.

Once, the king, surrounded by his ministers and heroic warriors, was sitting in the royal court when he saw a traveler in the sky. It was a celestial chariot, bright as fire, dazzling to the eyes, and the courtiers along with the king were absorbed in listening to its music. Bewildered, they sent messengers to find out what it was. The messengers went to see the chariot, which was as brilliant as the sun. Because of the gaze of a leper, who was the son of a merchant and a royal messenger, that chariot fell to the ground.

The messengers returned and informed the king about the chariot, which was full of virtuous celestial beings. It was shining brightly but had fallen to the ground due to the evil gaze. The king, filled with great joy and accompanied by his retinue, mounted his horse and went there, eager to see the chariot, considering it a great fortune. Surrounded by his people, they reached the vicinity of the chariot amidst the sound of many musical instruments. Seeing Indra there, they dismounted and bowed down.

The king, adorned with various ornaments and accompanied by all the gods, spoke to the destroyer of Bala with folded hands: "Blessed is the earth today, blessed is my birth and my wealth. O Lord of Shachi, my ancestors and all my senses are blessed today. Because of your appearance in the mortal world, along with your followers—you, to whom the gods like Brahma and Shiva are subservient—it is as if a hundred Ashvamedha sacrifices have been performed. I do not know by what merit you have been seen by all the people today. And why has this chariot of yours fallen to the earth? O God, you should resolve this doubt of mine. Where have you been, and where are you going? Tell me, O Lord."

Indra said: "O King, listen to the great wonder that Narada told me. I will tell you, so listen with a focused mind. Narada said: 'I went to the mortal world, to the ashram of Bhrushundi, who was staying in the form of Ganesha, chanting incessantly. By meditating on Him, I saw the wonderful miracle of His form, and I worshipped that sage who had taken the form of the Elephant-faced Lord. After bowing to that sage and asking his permission, I came to see you. O Indra, such likeness has not been seen elsewhere on earth.'

Indra said: 'I, having worshipped Narada and sent him away, went with great eagerness to see the sage in that form. I mounted my excellent chariot, which was as fast as the wind, and after seeing and worshipping the sage who had the form of the Elephant-faced Lord, I bowed to Bhrushundi. After accepting his worship, I set off with my retinue, wishing to go to Amaravati. As this chariot reached the vicinity of your city, it fell to this part of the earth due to the gaze of that sinful leper. I have told you everything, O King.'"

Shurasena said: "O Indra, by what penance did that Bhrushundi attain the form of the Elephant-faced Lord? Tell me that, O Lord, for I have no satisfaction in hearing that nectar of a story."

Chapter 57 : The Story of Bhrushundi
Indra said: "I shall now narrate to you an ancient story about how one attained resemblance to Gananatha through devotion.

In the Dandakaranya forest region, there was a city named Nandura. In that city lived a wicked fisherman, notorious by the name of Nama. From childhood, he was engaged in theft, and in his youth, he committed adultery. He would steal openly and even swear false oaths, deceiving others and causing divisions among them. He was eventually expelled from the village because he was addicted to gambling and alcohol.

He then took shelter in a distant mountain cave in the forest, accompanied by his beloved. There, he killed many travelers on the roads. In this way, he amassed great wealth and adorned his wife and children with various ornaments, satisfying them through his deceitful ways.

He carried weapons—swords, shields, nooses, excellent bows, and iron-tipped staffs on both ends—and with his quiver full of arrows, he would hide in treetops or caves and kill many people. He would collect various items, garments, and ornaments, take them home, and sell them in the city. He enjoyed all worldly pleasures freely in his house. Thus engaged in sinful activities, he also killed animals in the forest.

One day, while chasing someone for about a league, he stumbled and fell to the ground. Rising with great difficulty, the wretch slowly continued on his way. While traveling, he saw the auspicious Ganeshvara Tirtha. He bathed there merely to relieve his fatigue. Then, as he proceeded toward his own territory, he saw Mudgala on the path.

Mudgala was chanting the mantra of Gananatha along with His name. The fisherman, drawing his sword, approached Mudgala with the intent to kill him. But at that moment, all his weapons fell from his firm grasp, and the sword too slipped away. The wicked man's mind was instantly transformed by the power of Mudgala's devotion to the elephant-faced Lord.

The great sage laughed upon seeing him in that state and asked, "Why have your weapons fallen from you, even though they were tied securely? Tell me."

Indra continued: "Through bathing in Ganesha Tirtha and by seeing the sage, he became endowed with knowledge and detachment. He spoke to Mudgala."

The fisherman said: "I consider it a great wonder that by bathing in this pond, my mind has been completely transformed, O Brahmana, especially by seeing you. Since childhood, my mind has been wicked and inclined toward sin. Countless sins have I committed until today, O Lord. Now, by your grace, my mind has become detached. I shall not take up these weapons again, for they have fallen from me. Please show me complete compassion and rescue me from the ocean of worldly existence. The righteous always show mercy even to the fallen and the sinners. The company of the virtuous is never in vain, O great sage, just as contact with a treasure is never futile even among base metals."

Indra said: "Thus addressed, Mudgala, filled with compassion, spoke to him, remembering the sin of abandoning those who seek refuge."

Mudgala said: "You have no eligibility for acts of charity and such prescribed rites. Nevertheless, out of grace, I shall teach you the chanting of the sacred name. It is the supreme practice of Gajanana, which grants all accomplishments to people."

Then the fisherman bowed to the sage Mudgala. He placed his hand upon his head and instructed him in the name-mantra: "Obeisance to Ganesha." He planted a staff before him on the ground and lovingly told him: "Until I return, keep chanting this name until this staff sprouts. Sit in one place, sustain yourself on air alone, with a concentrated mind. At dawn and dusk, offer water at the base of the staff."

Indra said: "Thus instructed by Mudgala, the fisherman, despairing of life, remained there after the sage had disappeared. Sitting alone in the forest, he chanted the name-mantra, keeping the sage's staff before him, seated in the shade of a tree. Without food, without desire, controlling his senses and remaining self-restrained, he continued thus. At the end of a thousand years, the staff sprouted. Then he awaited the sage's arrival, his body covered by an anthill and entwined with creepers.

After that, Sage Mudgala, by divine providence, came to that place. Remembering the staff and the fisherman, he saw the excellent sprouted staff and the fisherman whose body was covered by an anthill. The sage observed that he had undertaken a penance most difficult even for all sages. The best of sages removed the anthill from his body and sprinkled his entire body with consecrated water.

Immediately, his body became divine, and the great sage said to him, who had attained resemblance to Ganesha through the practice: 'You are chanting the auspicious name of Vinayaka, though with two arms.' His eyes then opened, awakened by the sage. From his eyes arose fire that blazed like lightning into the sky, ready to burn the three worlds, but the sage restrained it.

He then bowed to the compassionate sage, his guru, and embraced him with joy, like a son embracing his father. Mudgala instructed the fisherman who had emerged from the anthill and accepted him as his son. He gave him the name Bhrushundi, for his trunk had emerged from the earth.

Mudgala then gave him the one-syllable mantra and granted him boons: 'You shall be the best of sages. You shall be worshiped by Indra, the gods, Gandharvas, and Siddhas. Just as Lord Gajanana is meditated upon and is the destroyer of sins, so shall you too become renowned as Bhrushundi. Whoever has your vision shall become accomplished. Your lifespan shall be a hundred thousand kalpas by my word.'

As Mudgala granted him many such boons, Indra and other gods, as well as Narada and other sages, came to see him. Bowing to him, they said: 'O Bhrushundi, by seeing you, our birth, knowledge, parents, penance, and fame have become meaningful. You are indeed Gananatha Himself; you are worthy of our worship.'

He worshipped them all, bowed to them, and dismissed them. Then, sitting again in the lotus posture, he chanted the one-syllable mantra, having established before him the enchanting image of Ganesha. He worshipped it daily with the sixteen offerings. His hermitage shone with ponds, lakes, trees, and creepers, where lions and deer, mongooses, and serpents lived without enmity.

After a hundred years, Gajanana became pleased and said: 'You are of my own form. Why do you perform penance? You have already accomplished all. At the end of your lifespan, you shall attain absorption into me. This region shall become renowned as Name. It shall grant various accomplishments to those who perform rituals, O mantra. By seeing my image here, one shall not be subject to rebirth. One without sons shall obtain sons, and one seeking knowledge shall attain wisdom.'"

Indra said: "Thus, O best of kings Shurasena, everything you asked has been narrated to you. What else do you wish to hear?"

Thus ends the fifty-seventh chapter of the Upasana Khanda of the Shri Ganesha Purana, entitled "The Story of Bhrushundi."

Chapter 58 : The Narration of the Sankashta Chaturthi Vrata

Brahma said: Having heard these excellent words of Indra, Shurasena, pleased by the nectar of the discourse, asked Vyasa again.

Shurasena asked: By what means can your chariot, O Lord of Gods, move through the sky? Please tell me the way, or tell me what I should do for you.

Brahma said: Having been asked this question again, the slayer of demons (Indra), smiling, spoke these words for all the worlds to hear.

Indra said: O best of kings, is there anyone in your city, be it a Brahmin or a Kshatriya, who observes the Sankashta Chaturthi Vrata? If the merit earned by such a person through this vow for a year is duly offered to you, O King, then your chariot will move; otherwise, it cannot be moved even by ten thousand men.

The King asked: O Sage, tell me about the Sankashta Chaturthi Vrata. What is its merit, what is the fruit, what is the procedure, and whom should one worship? By whom was it performed in the past, and through whose performance was success achieved? O Shunasira (Indra), please tell me all this in detail out of compassion.

Shakra (Indra) said: In this regard, they cite an ancient history, the dialogue between King Kritavirya and the great-souled Narada. There was a powerful king on earth named Kritavirya, who was truthful, generous, a performer of sacrifices, honorable, and a great warrior. He was self-controlled, moderate in eating, and a servant of gods and Brahmins. O King, there was no counting of his horses, elephants, warriors, charioteers, and archers. In his palace, there were golden beds and vessels; there was never even a copper vessel used for cooking. Twelve thousand Brahmins dined in his rows daily. He had a wife named Sugandha, who was devoted to righteousness, chaste, dedicated to her husband, and the most beautiful in the three worlds. She was adorned with various ornaments and loved Brahmins, gods, and guests. O King, that couple remained childless. For the sake of a son, they performed all kinds of charities, vows, penances, and various other observances, and conducted sacrifices with many offerings. They visited various holy places and regions, yet they did not have a son due to the sins committed in previous lives.

Once, the sorrowful king called his ministers and, having entrusted the kingdom, the seal, the treasury, the people, and the provinces to them, he and his wife went to a great forest. Clad in bark and deerskin, the couple engaged in penance. They controlled their senses, limited their food, and lived only on fallen leaves and air. Seeing them, who were visible only by their eyes, the sage Narada spoke to the father of Kritavirya, who was in the realm of the ancestors: "Because of his childlessness, your son is on the verge of death. If he had a son, he would be a guide to the heavenly realm. If Kritavirya had a son, he would live, or even if he died, he would attain heaven." Having said this, Narada left and saw a wonder on earth.

He saw the parents, son, wife, and daughter of Bhrushundi, all facing downwards in the terrible Kumbhipaka hell, surrounded by flames of fire. They were being beaten by the messengers of Yama and were crying out in various ways. Hearing their cries, the compassionate Narada came and told Bhrushundi about their suffering.

Narada said: Even the gods led by Shakra and sages like Kapila go to seek the vision of the one who has the form of Ganesha. How is it that your parents, wife, sons, daughters, and servants are being cooked in the Kumbhipaka hell due to your faults? And you, being full of knowledge, how do you not realize this? Make an effort to liberate your ancestors, O great sage.

Shakra said: Bhrushundi, deeply distressed by hearing the words of the sage and pained by the suffering of his ancestors, burned like fire. He thought of a way to liberate them. He then gave them the merit born of the Sankashta Chaturthi Vrata. Having contemplated through meditation, Bhrushundi, who knew the unseen, meditated on the elephant-faced God, held sacred water in his hand, and spoke, praying to Lord Gajanana for the sake of his ancestors.

Bhrushundi said: If I have performed your vow with devotion, O Lord of the Ganas, then by its power, liberate my ancestors quickly. Having said this, he poured that water into the hand of Gajanana. As soon as the water was poured, by the grace of Gajanana, they all ascended to celestial chariots, appearing as divine beings. Served by Apsaras, praised by Charanas, and sung to by Gandharvas, they reached the abode of Ganesha. Others who were sinners in Kumbhipaka also ascended to chariots and went to the realm of Ganesha.

Thus, I have described to you the glory of this vow. By the merit of just one day, they all attained a good destination. If someone performs the Sankashta Vrata with devotion throughout their life, even Shesha (the serpent) would not be able to count the merit of that person. Therefore, by its power, my chariot shall move.

Chapter 59 : The Narrative of the Chaturthi Vow

The King asked: After emerging from the Kumbhipaka hell and attaining heaven, what means did the father of Kritavirya employ at that time?

Shakra replied: He went quickly to Brahmaloka and saw the lotus-seated Brahma. Distressed after hearing from Narada about the extinction of his lineage, he bowed and asked the cause of the lineage's end. He said, "My son is extremely righteous, generous, and a performer of sacrifices. He is devoted to gods and guests, and highly respectful to those worthy of respect. O Creator, he made various efforts for the sake of a son, yet he did not have one. O Lord of gods, he handed over the kingdom to his subjects and stayed in the forest, surviving only on air. He is reduced to mere bones and will die today or tomorrow. O Lord, please tell me the means, out of compassion, by which his sins from previous births may be destroyed, so that I may bring him there for the growth of my lineage."

Hearing these sweet words, Brahma said: Listen, I shall tell you the previous birth of your son. In that same city, there was a low-caste man named Sama, whose very sight destroyed one's merit due to his extremely evil deeds. Once, out of greed for wealth, he killed twelve peaceful Brahmins on the road and left them in a cave. He took all their belongings and returned home at night. O King, on the fourth day of the dark fortnight of Magha, at moonrise, he called his son quickly, saying "Ganesha, Ganesha," and ate with him joyfully, having had no food or water. In time, that son died at night on the fourth day of the dark fortnight at moonrise. Due to the merit of the Sankashta Chaturthi vow performed unknowingly, he went to the blissful abode of Ganesha. Riding a magnificent chariot, watched by groups of celestial nymphs, praised by those in the chariots, and worshipped with divine flowers, he attained the status of your son, Kritavirya, on this earth due to the remainder of that merit. O sinless one, his son will be born after the destruction of that heap of sins.

Hearing the words of Brahma, the excellent King trembled and asked again for the means to destroy sins. The father of Kritavirya asked: O Creator, how can the sin of Brahma-hatya be destroyed? Tell me, O ocean of mercy, even if it is very difficult.

Brahma said: If your son performs the vow known as Sankashta Chaturthi properly, he will be liberated from that sin.

The King asked: O Brahmin, how is it performed, and in which auspicious month? Tell me everything, O Lord, so that the sin may be destroyed.

Brahma said: If the fourth day falls on a Tuesday in the dark fortnight of Magha, one should begin this vow at an auspicious moment at moonrise. After brushing teeth and taking twenty-one baths, one should perform daily duties and then chant the mantra. One should remain silent, fasting, and avoid criticizing others. One should strictly avoid evil deeds, betel leaf, drinking water, and backbiting. At the end of the day, one should bathe with a paste of sesame and myrobalan. One should chant the single-syllable, six-syllable, or Vedic mantra for the pleasure of Ganesha according to the rules. One should meditate on the elephant-faced God of gods with a steady mind. Then, for a moment, one should worship the Lord of Ganas with sixteen offerings and various food items—modakas, cakes, fried snacks, laddus, dumplings, milk puddings, various delicacies, and lickable or chewable foods. One should offer various fruits, betel leaves, sacrificial fees, twenty-one blades of durva grass, lamps, and flowers. At moonrise, one should offer arghya to the lunar day with mantras, first to the elephant-faced God and then to the Moon. After offering worship, bowing down, and seeking forgiveness, one should feed twenty-one Brahmins with devotion, or ten or twelve if unable, and satisfy them with gifts. After listening to the story, one should eat in silence. One should spend the rest of the night with music and singing. O King, if this vow is performed with effort for one year, all his sins will be destroyed and he will have an excellent son. A man will attain whatever other desire he contemplates. All troubles will be destroyed, and there will be no fear from enemies. Staying at the root of a Shami tree, fasting and chanting, one should perform this vow until moonrise. The blind, dumb, dull, or lame will attain their desires. There is no doubt that one will obtain a wife, sons, wealth, and a kingdom. By eating ghee-laddus and other items in the months starting from Shravana for a year, one attains the highest success. In Shravana, seven laddus; in Bhadrapada, eating curd; in Ashvina, fasting; in Kartika, drinking milk; in Margashirsha, fasting; in Pausha, drinking cow urine; in Magha, eating sesame; in Phalguna, ghee and sugar; in Chaitra, Panchagavya; in Vaishakha, a hundred-petaled lotus; in Jyeshtha, eating ghee; and in Ashadha, eating honey.

The father of Kritavirya asked: Why is the Angaraka Chaturthi (Tuesday Chaturthi) considered special? O Brahma, please tell me, for I am bowing to you. I am not satisfied hearing the auspicious story of the elephant-faced God.

Chapter 60 : The Legend of the Angaraka Chaturthi Vrata

Brahma said: O King, listen attentively as I describe the glory of the Angaraka Chaturthi. In the city of Avanti lived the great sage Bharadvaja, who was well-versed in the Vedas, Vedangas, and all the scriptures. He was constantly devoted to the Agnihotra sacrifice, dedicated to teaching his disciples, and lived on the banks of a river, absorbed in his spiritual practices.

One day, he saw a beautiful celestial nymph (Apsara) and, overcome by sudden desire, he fell to the ground, struck by the arrows of Cupid. In his agitated state, his semen fell and entered a crevice in the earth. From that, a child as radiant as a China rose (Japa flower) was born. The Earth, out of affection, raised him with great care.

When the boy reached the age of seven, he asked his mother, "Why is my body reddish? Who is my father? Tell me now." The Earth replied, "You were born from the semen of the sage Bharadvaja that fell into me, and I have raised you." The boy then asked to see his father. The Earth took him to Bharadvaja, bowed down, and said, "O Sage, this is your son, born of your own energy. Please accept and raise him." The Earth then returned to her own abode, and Bharadvaja, having received his son, embraced him with joy, kissed his head, and placed him on his lap.

At an auspicious time, the sage performed his Upanayana (sacred thread ceremony). After teaching him the Vedas and Shastras, he instructed him in the sacred mantra of Ganesha, telling him to perform penance to please the Lord. "He will be satisfied and grant all your desires," the sage said. The boy went to the banks of the Narmada, sat in the Padmasana posture, controlled his senses, and meditated on Heramba (Ganesha). He chanted the supreme mantra, subsisting only on air, and became very thin.

He performed this severe penance for a thousand years. On the fourth day of the dark fortnight of the month of Magha, when the moon rose, Ganesha appeared before him. He had ten arms, divine garments, a crescent moon on his forehead, and held various weapons. He had a beautiful trunk, shining tusks, fan-like ears, and was adorned with many ornaments, shining like ten million suns. Seeing the Lord before him, the boy stood up, bowed down, and praised the Lord of the Universe.

Bhauma (the boy) said: "Salutations to the destroyer of obstacles and the creator of obstacles. Salutations to the Lord of gods and demons, the source of all power. Salutations to the eternal, the one beyond qualities, the destroyer of ignorance. Salutations to the Lord of the knowers of Brahman, the one who sustains and dissolves the universe. Salutations to the support of the world, the protector of the three worlds, the form of Brahman. Salutations to the one who is both manifest and unmanifest, the remover of all faults. Salutations to the Lord of the Ganas, the Supreme Lord."

Pleased by this praise, Ganesha spoke in a gentle voice to delight the boy. Ganesha said: "I am pleased with your severe penance, your devotion, and this hymn. Even though you are a child, your patience is great; therefore, I grant you the boons you desire."

Bhauma replied: "O Lord, my birth and my vision of you are blessed. My knowledge and my lineage are blessed. This entire world and my penance are blessed because I have seen the Lord of all. If you are pleased, O Lord of gods, let me reside in heaven, and I wish to drink nectar with the gods. May my name be auspicious and famous in the three worlds. Since I have seen you on this Chaturthi, may this day be forever holy, a remover of all distress, and a granter of desires to those who observe the Vrata, by your grace."

Ganesha said: "You shall drink nectar with the gods, O son of the Earth. You will be known in the world by the name Mangala. Because you are the son of the Earth and reddish in color, you shall be called Angaraka. Those on earth who observe the Angaraka Chaturthi will gain the merit of a year's worth of Sankashti Vratas. There is no doubt that all their tasks will be accomplished without obstacles. O conqueror of enemies, you will become the King of Avanti. Because you have performed this supreme Vrata, whoever recites this story will attain all their desires."

Having granted these boons, the elephant-faced Lord disappeared. Bhauma then installed an image of the Lord with a trunk, ten arms, and a beautiful form. He built a temple for Ganesha and named the deity Mangala Murti. That place became a granter of desires for all, bestowing liberation through worship and devotion. Later, Ganesha sent his attendants to bring Bhauma to him. They brought him in his physical body to Ganesha, which was a miraculous sight.

Bhauma became famous in the three worlds, and because he performed the Sankashti Chaturthi on a Tuesday (Angaraka), it became known as the Angaraka Chaturthi. He attained heaven and drank nectar with the gods. Because he grants all desired objects, the Mangala Murti is also known as Chintamani. Located to the west of the Parinera city, he is famous as the Chintamani, the remover of all obstacles. Even now, he is worshipped by the Siddhas and Gandharvas, and he grants all desired objects, including sons and grandsons, to his devotees.

Chapter 61 : The Story of the Curse and Grace of the Moon

Brahma said: O King, once I went to Kailasa, the abode of Shiva. While sitting in the assembly, I saw Narada arrive. He presented a unique fruit to Shiva. Both Ganesha and Kumara (Kartikeya) requested that fruit from Shiva. Shiva asked me to whom he should give the fruit. I suggested that he give it to the child, Kumara. Shiva gave it to him, which angered Ganesha. I then returned to my abode to continue the work of creation.

There, Ganesha, the remover of obstacles, created a great disturbance. He assumed a terrifying form and frightened me. When the Moon saw me in such a distressed state, he laughed at Ganesha, who had the face of an elephant, along with his attendants. Enraged, Ganesha cursed the Moon, saying, "Because of my words, you shall become invisible in the three worlds. Anyone who sees you will be afflicted with a great sin." Having cursed him, Ganesha returned to his abode.

The Moon became dull, miserable, and filled with sorrow. He thought, "How did I, in my ignorance, act like a child? I have become invisible, dull, and discolored to everyone. How will I be beautiful and worshipped again?" Meanwhile, the gods heard of the great curse on the Moon. They went to where Ganesha was and prayed to him, saying, "O Lord of Lords, you are the one to be worshipped by the worlds. You protect, destroy, and create as you wish. You are the Lord of the three worlds. We have come to you for refuge. Please protect us. The whole world is afraid of your curse. How did such a great misfortune befall the Moon? Please grant grace to the Moon and the world."

Hearing their words, Ganesha, who grants dharma, desire, wealth, and liberation, was pleased and said, "I am pleased with your prayers, O gods. Ask for a great boon; I will grant even that which is difficult to attain in the three worlds." The gods replied, "We all desire your grace upon the Moon. If he is graced, the whole world will be graced." Ganesha said, "Let the Moon be invisible for a year, or half a year, or even a quarter of a year. Ask for another boon, O gods."

The gods bowed down to Ganesha. Ganesha, moved by their devotion, said, "How can I go back on my word? I cannot abandon those who have sought my refuge. Even if Mount Meru moves, the sun falls, fire becomes cold, or the ocean crosses its boundaries, my word shall not be false. However, listen to what I say: Whoever sees the Moon on the fourth day of the bright fortnight of Bhadrapada, whether knowingly or unknowingly, shall be cursed and suffer great sorrow."

Hearing this, the gods rejoiced. They bowed, showered flowers, and went to the Moon. They told him, "You were very foolish to laugh at Ganesha. You have put the three worlds in danger by your offense. Because you offended Ganesha, the Lord of the three worlds, he has decreed this for the welfare of all. We have pleased him with great difficulty. You shall not be seen on the fourth day of Bhadrapada. You should also seek refuge in Ganesha. By his grace, you will become pure and attain great fame."

Hearing this, the Moon went to seek refuge in Ganesha, who is dear to those who seek him. He chanted the supreme, single-syllable mantra that destroys all sins. Following the advice of Indra, he performed severe penance for twelve years on the southern bank of the Ganga. Pleased, Ganesha appeared before him, adorned with red garlands and sandalwood, four-armed, and radiant as a million suns. Seeing his brilliance, the Moon trembled, but gathering courage, he bowed and prayed with devotion.

The Moon said, "I bow to the elephant-faced Lord who removes all obstacles. O Lord of the world, please forgive the offense I committed out of ignorance.
