Have compassion on me, who has sought your refuge." Hearing this, Ganesha was pleased and granted him boons. "Your form will be as it was before. However, the person who sees you on the fourth day of the bright fortnight of Bhadrapada will surely be cursed. You will be invisible on that day, as I told the gods. But on the fourth day of the dark fortnight, you shall be worshipped. You will be beautiful and dear to me, residing on my forehead as a digit. Every month on the second day, you will be visible."

Having received these boons, the Moon became as he was before. Together with the sages and gods, he installed an image of Ganesha and named him "Bhalachandra" (the one who wears the moon on his forehead). They built a golden temple adorned with gems and worshipped him with sixteen offerings. The gods and sages declared that this place would be famous as a site of accomplishment (Siddhiksetra). After bowing to Ganesha, they all returned to their respective abodes with joy. Ganesha then disappeared, and the Moon, with a happy heart, returned to his own realm.

Chapter 62 : The Tale of the Durva Grass

King Kritavirya said: O Lord, you have explained the reason for the worship performed on the fourth day of the dark fortnight of the moon. Now, I wish to hear the reason behind offering the sprouts of Durva grass. Tell me, why is the Durva sprout so dear to the Lord of the Ganas (Ganesha)?

Brahma said: O best of kings, listen as I tell you the fruit obtained by offering Durva sprouts to Ganesha. There was a famous city named Jambe in the southern region. In that city lived a Kshatriya named Sulabha, who was wealthy, charitable, powerful, wise, and respected. He was self-controlled, devoted to the truth, and well-versed in all the Shastras and Vedas. He was constantly devoted to Ganesha and spent his time in prayer. His wife, Sumudra, was renowned for her beauty, which put the Apsaras to shame. She was virtuous, devoted to the gods and guests, and always followed her husband’s will. She was the most respected among all chaste women.

One day, the couple, having bathed and purified themselves, sat together discussing the Puranas. At that moment, a Brahmin named Madhusudana arrived, seeking alms. He was a constant meditator on the Supreme Lord, but due to poverty, he was ragged and nearly naked. Seeing him, Sulabha laughed out of ignorance. The great sage, agitated by the mockery, became red with anger, as if burning the three worlds. He cursed him, saying, "O fool, you who laughed at a Brahmin with open teeth, become a bull, a beast of burden, always suffering!"

Hearing her husband being cursed, Sumudra was overcome with rage. Like a serpent struck by a foot, she cursed the Brahmin, "Since you have cursed my husband out of ignorance, you shall become a donkey and eat filth!" Hearing this, the Brahmin, in his cruelty, cursed her back, "Since you have cursed me, you shall become a Chandali (outcaste woman), poor, full of vices, and a consumer of filth."

Having cursed each other, they left their bodies. Sulabha became a bull, constantly yoked to a plow, never finding a moment of rest due to the Brahmin’s curse. The Brahmin, Madhusudana, was born as a donkey, and Sumudra became a cruel Chandali, violent and wretched. Once, while wandering in the south of the city, she saw a magnificent temple of Ganesha, surrounded by trees, vines, and birds. There, great yogis and devotees of Ganesha lived, following strict vows. Some sought worldly desires, while others sought liberation.

During the fourth day of the month of Bhadrapada, a great festival for Ganesha was held in that city. A torrential rain, signaling a great deluge, began to fall. Frightened by the rain, the Chandali went from house to house but was driven away by the people. She then went to the temple, holding a fire in her hand. There too, she was beaten by some and driven away by the yogis. She lit a fire with grass to warm her shivering body. By divine providence, a sprout of Durva grass fell onto the head of Ganesha’s idol. The donkey, shivering from the cold, also went to the temple. The bull, freed from the plow by fate, also arrived at the temple.

Both the bull and the donkey ate the grass that the Chandali had gathered. As the people slept, a fight broke out between them near the idol. During their struggle, with their hooves and horns, they accidentally knocked more Durva sprouts onto the trunk and feet of Ganesha. The Lord was pleased. The Chandali then arrived with a stick, beat the bull and the donkey, and consumed the offerings herself. Hearing the sound of their hooves, the sleeping people woke up, beat them with sticks and fists, and drove them away.

The people, fearing the touch of the Chandali and the donkey, bathed the idol of Ganesha with consecrated water and worshipped Him with great devotion. The wicked trio—the bull, the donkey, and the Chandali—were beaten severely by the people and, finding the temple doors closed, had nowhere to go. As they wandered and cried out in their agony, the heart of the Lord Ganesha was moved. He thought, "Through their actions, they have performed my worship. By offering the Durva grass and circling me, they have worshipped me. Even though they are wicked, they have offered me Durva on the fourth day of the bright fortnight of Bhadrapada. Therefore, they are worthy of my grace."

Ganesha then sent a divine chariot, adorned with his own Ganas, Gandharvas, and Apsaras, filled with music and flowers. The Ganas placed the three of them into the chariot, and by Ganesha’s command, they were instantly taken to His divine abode. All the people watched in astonishment, saying, "This is the result of their past merits." The yogis, leaving their meditation, asked the Ganas, "How did these great sinners attain such a divine state? We have spent countless ages in meditation and have never seen the Lord. Tell us how we may also attain the abode of Ganesha."

Chapter 63 : The Greatness of Durva Grass

The Ganas said: Listen, all you yogis, with a steady mind, to that which even Shesha and the four-faced Brahma are unable to describe. Who else is capable of speaking of it? We shall speak according to our ability. Even Brahma, Shiva, and the others always offer praise to Him; who then can clearly describe the glory of that Lord of the Ganas? Nevertheless, O sinless ones, listen to His divine play. The glory of the blades of Durva grass is not known even to the sages and the gods; it cannot be attained through sacrifices, charity, penance, vows, or offerings. In this regard, they cite an ancient story, a dialogue between Indra and the great-souled Narada.

Once, Narada went to see Indra, eager to meet him. Having been worshipped with supreme devotion and seated, the slayer of Vritra (Indra) asked the sage with great respect about the glory of Durva grass. Indra asked: O Brahmin, O sage, why is the great Durva grass especially dear to the great-souled Lord Ganesha? The sage replied: I shall tell you the supreme glory of Durva grass as I have known it. Long ago, in the city of Sthavara, there lived a great sage named Kaundinya, a devotee of Ganesha endowed with the power of penance. To the south of his village, there was a very beautiful hermitage, surrounded by creepers and trees, with large lakes filled with blooming lotuses, frequented by bees, swans, ducks, cranes, tortoises, and water-fowl.

There, absorbed in meditation, he began intense penance, placing before him a great four-armed image of Ganesha, which was serene, bestowing boons, adorned with Durva grass, and well-worshipped. He chanted the supreme six-syllabled mantra that pleases the deity. His wife, named Ashraya, filled with doubt, asked him: O Lord, why do you offer a heap of Durva grass to the elephant-faced God every day? No one is pleased with mere grass. If there is any merit in this, please tell me out of compassion.

Kaundinya said: Listen, my dear, I shall tell you the supreme glory of Durva grass. Long ago, in the city of Dharma, there was a great festival. All the gods, Gandharvas, Apsaras, Siddhas, Charanas, Nagas, sages, Yakshas, and Rakshasas were invited. As Tilottama was dancing, her garment fell to the ground. Yama saw her beautiful and large breasts and became tormented by desire, restless and shameless. He wished to embrace her and kiss her face. Ashamed, Yama left the assembly with his head bowed. As he walked, his semen slipped and fell to the ground. From it, a man with a distorted face was born, making cruel gnashing sounds, terrifying the three worlds. He burned the entire earth, his matted hair touching the sky. The three worlds trembled greatly at his sound.

At that moment, all the assembly members went to Vishnu. Having offered various praises according to their ability, they prayed to Him for the welfare of all worlds. He went with them all to the auspicious Ganesha. Knowing of the demon's destruction, they all praised Him. The gods and sages said: Salutations to the embodiment of obstacles, salutations to the remover of obstacles. Salutations to the one who is everything, salutations to the witness of all. Salutations to the great God, salutations to the origin of the world. Salutations to the ocean of compassion, the cause of the world's protection. Salutations to the complete one, the cause of universal dissolution. Salutations to the granter of boons to devotees, the giver of all, salutations again and again. Salutations to the one who is the refuge of the lonely, the fulfiller of all desires. Salutations to the knower of the Vedas, salutations to the creator of the Vedas. To whom else shall we go for refuge? Who else can remove our fear? How have the people encountered this untimely dissolution? O elephant-faced Lord of gods, O remover of obstacles, O imperishable one! When everyone is facing death, why do you neglect us?

Hearing these words, Ganesha, the ocean of compassion, appeared before them in the form of a child, the destroyer of fear. He possessed lotus-like eyes and a face resembling a hundred moons. His radiance was like that of a million suns, his body surpassing a million Cupids. His teeth surpassed the beauty of jasmine buds, and his lips surpassed the bimba fruit. He had a high nose, beautiful eyebrows, a conch-like neck, a broad chest, and two strong arms reaching his knees. He had a deep navel, a shining belly, a beautiful waist, and thighs surpassing the beauty of the plantain tree. His calves and ankles were beautiful, and his lotus feet were radiant. Adorned with various ornaments, he was dressed in precious garments. Seeing the God before the city, the gods and sages stood up with shouts of victory. They bowed down to him on the ground like the gods before Indra.

The gods and sages said: Who are you? Where have you come from? What is your task? Tell us, O Lord. We recognize you as Brahman himself in the form of a child, having appeared to save us from the terror of Analasura, the destroyer of the wicked, who has stopped our rituals. Hearing these words, the child-formed Ganesha spoke to all the gods and sages with a smiling face. The child said: You are full of knowledge, and what you have said is true. I have come with speed in the form of a child by my own will, O sages, to kill that wicked one who torments others. I shall tell you the plan for his destruction; do as I say, O sinless ones. Upon seeing him, you must not urge me to fight. You should watch the great spectacle of his and mine.

Hearing these words of grace, they were all filled with joy. They said to each other: We do not know his prowess. Has the Lord descended in the form of a child to kill him and save the three worlds from suffering? Having said this, they all bowed to him with respect. At that very moment, the demon, resembling the fire of time, arrived, burning the ten directions and consuming the world of men. There was a great uproar from the people who were crying out. Seeing him, all the sages fled. They said to the child: Run away quickly! Otherwise, this great fire will surely kill you today, just as a Timingila fish eats fish or Garuda eats snakes. Hearing these words, the Supreme Lord Ganesha, in the form of a child, stood firm like Mount Himavan. The sages went far away, leaving the child there.

Chapter 64 : The Description of the Glory of Durva

Ashraya said: When the celestial sages had departed and the child remained still like a mountain, what wonder occurred there, born of the fire of universal destruction? O great sage, tell me all of this in detail.

Narada said: Having been asked this question by her, Kaundinya, the best of sages, spoke. Listen to what I say regarding what he told the husband of Shachi (Indra).

Kaundinya said: While the child with the elephant face remained still like a mountain, the demon Analasura arrived, as unshakeable as the fire of universal destruction. At that moment, the mountain itself trembled along with the child. With a sound like the roaring of clouds, the birds fell from the branches of the trees to the ground. The oceans dried up, trees were uprooted, and due to the great shaking, nothing could be discerned.

At that very moment, the Lord in the form of a child, by the power of his illusion, swallowed that demon who was in the form of fire, just as Agastya had swallowed the ocean. Then the Lord thought, "If this fire remains in my belly, it will burn the three worlds." Seeing this great wonder, Indra gave him the moon to pacify that fire. The gods and sages praised him as Bhalachandra (one with the moon on his forehead). Even then, the fire in his belly was not pacified.

Then Brahma gave him Siddhi and Buddhi, his mind-born daughters, who were beautiful, lotus-eyed, and graceful. He said, "Embrace them, and your fire will be pacified." Upon their embrace, the fire was pacified slightly. Then the Lord of Lakshmi gave him a soft lotus, and all the gods and humans called him Padmapani (one with a lotus in his hand).

When the fire was still not fully pacified, Varuna sprinkled cool water upon him. Then Shiva gave him a thousand-hooded serpent. Because he tied it around his belly, he became known as Vyalabaddhodara (one with a serpent tied around his belly). Even then, his throat remained burning with fire.

Eighty-eight thousand sages approached him. Each of them offered twenty-one blades of Durva grass, which were like nectar, and placed them on his head. Then the fire was pacified. The Supreme Lord was pleased, being worshipped with the sprouts of Durva. Knowing this, all the gods worshipped the elephant-faced Lord with many Durva sprouts, and the Lord was delighted.

He said to the sages and gods, "My worship, whether performed with great devotion or little, is in vain without the sprouts of Durva. Without Durva sprouts, the fruit of worship is not attained by anyone. Therefore, if my devotees offer even one or twenty-one blades of Durva with devotion at dawn, the great fruit they obtain cannot be attained by hundreds of sacrifices, gifts, accumulation of vows, severe penances, or even by the merits of crores of births."

Kaundinya said: Hearing these words, the gods again worshipped the Supreme Lord, the elephant-faced God, with Durva sprouts. He roared with joy, filling the heavens and the earth. Being pleased, he granted many boons to all the gods, sages, and humans, and then concealed his child form. The gods called him Kalanala-prashamana (the pacifier of the fire of destruction).

The gods built a temple, installed the image of the elephant-faced Lord, and joyfully named him Vighnahara (the remover of obstacles). By the grace of this Vighnahara, bathing, charity, and penance performed here become infinite. Because he attained victory here, the city was named Vijaya, and because he destroyed the obstacles of all, he became known as Vighnaharta.

Kaundinya said: Thus, I have told you the entire excellent glory of Durva. By listening to and reading this, all sins are destroyed. Listen, O beloved, as I narrate this ancient history.

Thus ends the sixty-fourth chapter of the Upasana Khanda of the Sri Ganesha Purana, titled "The Description of the Glory of Durva."

Chapter 65 : The Taking Away of King Janaka's Virtue

Kaundinya said: Once upon a time, when the elephant-faced Lord Ganesha was sitting comfortably, the sage Narada arrived to see him after a long time. Having prostrated before him with his whole body, Narada said, "My life has become meaningful today, as my virtuous deeds have culminated in your divine vision, O Gajanana." Having said this, the sage stood before him with folded hands. Gajanana took his hand and made him sit on a seat. The glorious Gajanana and the great sage were both pleased. Narada, the best of sages, then said to the Lord of the Ganas, "I have come to inform you of a wonder that has arisen in my heart, and after bowing to you, I shall return."

Gajanana asked, "What wonder have you seen, and what is on your mind? Tell me everything in detail, and then you may return to your hermitage."

Narada said, "In the land of Mithila, there is a king named Janaka, who is the best of rulers. He is very proud, generous, and well-versed in the Vedas and their auxiliary branches. He is always engaged in feeding the poor and worshiping the Brahmins with various ornaments, clothes, and abundant offerings. He gives much wealth to the destitute, the blind, and the needy. Whatever the beggars ask for, he provides. Yet, the wealth of that noble soul never diminishes. Is it because of your satisfaction, O Gajanana, that his wealth increases? I went to his house to see this wonder, but he mocked me due to his pride in his own knowledge of Brahman. I told him, 'You are blessed, O best of kings, for Gajanana grants whatever you contemplate through your devotion.' But he replied with pride, 'I am the lord in the three worlds. I am the giver, the enjoyer, the protector, and the one who makes others give. There is nothing in the three worlds other than my own form. I am the doer, the cause, and the instrument, O best of sages.'"

Narada continued, "Hearing his words, I said to him in anger, 'There is no other creator of the world than the Lord. Why do you perform this duty with such hypocrisy, O King? I will soon show you the proof of this, O sinless one.' Having said this, I came to you, O Elephant-faced one."

Kaundinya said: Hearing the sage's words, the Lord honored him with offerings, ornaments, divine flowers, and sandalwood. Having received the sage's permission, he went to Vishnu in Vaikuntha. Gajanana, though omniscient, assumed a wretched form to test the king's devotion and arrived in Mithila. He appeared covered in wounds, bleeding, looking inauspicious, infested with flies, toothless, and suffering. Seeing him walking in such a state, people covered their noses with their clothes, and some spat at him. Stumbling, fainting, and falling, accompanied by a group of children, he reached the king's gate and said to the guards, "Tell the king that a guest has arrived—a hungry, old Brahmin seeking food."

The guards went and told King Janaka. The king, wanting to see the spectacle, ordered them to bring him in. The guards brought the ragged, dirty Brahmin to the king. From a distance, Janaka saw the old Brahmin trembling, covered in flies, bleeding, and sweating from exhaustion. Janaka wondered, "Is this the Lord himself in this form? If he has come to test me, and if I have any merit, I shall satisfy his mind, and it will not be otherwise."

As the king was thinking this, the guards brought the Brahmin in. The Brahmin said, "I have come here after hearing of your fame, which is as white as the moon's rays. Give me food, O King, for I have been hungry for a long time. Give me food until I am satisfied. You will gain the merit of a hundred sacrifices, O Lord of men."

Kaundinya said: Hearing this, the king took him inside, worshiped him according to the scriptures, and served him delicious food. The Brahmin consumed everything in a single mouthful. He asked for more, and the king gave him a mountain-like portion. The Brahmin consumed that too in one bite. All the food that was prepared, enough for ten thousand people, was given to him, and he devoured it instantly. Rice was put into countless pots to be cooked, and whatever was served to him, he ate it all. Then the people said to the king, "This must be a demon; why give him so much? No merit is gained by feeding demons." Others said, "Even if he eats the three worlds, he will not be satisfied. O King, give him grain instead."

Then all the grain from the house, the fields, and the village was brought and thrown before him. Yet, the guest in the form of a Brahmin, who ate everything, was not satisfied. Then the guards told the king that no more grain could be found anywhere. Hearing the guards' words, while Janaka stood with his head bowed, the Brahmin left, saying "Svasti" (peace), still unsatisfied. He went from house to house asking for food. The people told him, "The king has taken all the grain from everyone's house. You have eaten it all, O Brahmin; go wherever you please."

The Brahmin said, "I have heard of his fame in the world; there is no giver greater than Janaka. I came seeking satisfaction, but I am still hungry. How can I leave?" As the people remained silent, he wandered about and saw the beautiful mansion of Virochana and Trishira. He entered it as if he were the master of the house, even though it was devoid of all provisions and metal vessels.

Chapter 66 : The Glory of Durva

Kaundinya said: Those two, wearing only the earth as their seat and the sky as their garment, were free from all worldly attachments. They performed all their daily activities using only water and lived without ever begging for food. A Brahmin, assuming a form to test their purity, observed them. Their hut was surrounded by swarms of flies and mosquitoes, yet they worshipped the image of the Lord of the Ganas (Ganesha) with flowers and leaves. Seeing their unwavering devotion, the Brahmin approached them and said, "O sinless ones, listen to my words. Having heard of the fame of the King of Mithila, I came here hungry, hoping to be satisfied, but I did not find satisfaction there. One cannot preserve purity through hypocritical actions. If there is anything in your house that can satisfy my hunger, please give it to me."

The couple replied, "If even the great Emperor could not satisfy your hunger, what can we, who are poor, offer you? Tell me, how can the ocean, which is not filled by countless rivers, be filled by a single drop of water?" The Brahmin said, "Even a small thing given with devotion brings great satisfaction, whereas much given without devotion or with hypocrisy is useless." The couple replied, "O best of Brahmins, I swear by you, there is nothing in our house. We gathered these Durva grass sprouts this morning to worship Ganesha, and after the worship, only one remains." The Brahmin said, "Give me that one, for if given with devotion, it will be enough for my satisfaction."

Kaundinya continued: Hearing this, Virochana gave him the single Durva sprout with devotion. The Brahmin was instantly satisfied. Although he had been offered various delicacies like rice, milk pudding, and many other dishes, he had been unable to find satisfaction. Yet, when he accepted that single Durva sprout offered with love, his internal fire of hunger was immediately extinguished. He attained supreme satisfaction in an instant and, filled with joy, embraced the three-headed one (Ganesha).

The Brahmin shed his disguise, and the elephant-faced Lord appeared in his true form—four-armed, lotus-eyed, and adorned with his trunk. Holding a lotus, an axe, a garland, and a tusk in his hands, wearing a magnificent crown, glowing earrings, divine garments, and fragrant ointments, the pleased Lord said to the couple, "Ask for whatever boon your hearts desire." They replied, "O Lord, wherever we are born, may we have firm devotion to you. Or, grant us liberation from this difficult ocean of existence. We desire nothing else, O Elephant-faced One."

Kaundinya said: Hearing their words, Ganesha said, "So be it," and after embracing his devotee again, he vanished. For this reason, I offer this Durva sprout to Him. The Lord, who could not be satisfied by countless feasts, attained supreme satisfaction with a single Durva sprout. Thus, I have told you the auspicious glory of offering Durva, which grants all desires when heard. Whoever listens to or narrates this story with devotion will be blessed with children, wealth, and happiness in this world and the next. They will attain devotion to Ganesha, and those without desires will attain liberation.

The Ganas said: Hearing this story, Ashraya still had a doubt in her heart. Sensing this, Kaundinya said to her, "O sinless one, listen to my words to remove your doubt. I know what is in your heart. Take one Durva sprout and go quickly to Indra. First, offer him a blessing, then ask for gold. Take an amount of gold equal in weight to the Durva sprout and bring it here. Do not take more or less than that weight." The Ganas said: Hearing his words, Ashraya took a single Durva sprout and, devoted to her husband's command, went to Indra.

Thus ends the sixty-sixth chapter of the Upasana Khanda of the Sri Ganesha Purana, titled "The Glory of Durva."

Chapter 67 : The Glory of Durva

The Ganas said: Commanded by that sage to fulfill her desired objective, Ashraya took a single sprout of Durva grass and approached Indra. She said to him, "O Lord of the Gods, please give me auspicious gold. I have come to ask this of you at the behest of my husband."

Indra asked, "Why have you come here? If a command has been sent, I would have sent gold according to my capacity." Ashraya replied, "O God, I shall accept only as much gold as equals the weight of this Durva sprout, neither less nor more." Indra said, "Messenger, take her quickly to Kubera’s palace; he will give her the auspicious gold equivalent to the Durva sprout."

The Ganas said: By the order of the King of Gods, the divine messenger went with her to Kubera’s palace. The messenger said, "O God, Indra has sent this virtuous wife of a sage to you. Please provide her with gold equal to this Durva sprout. I must now depart, salutations to you."

Kubera thought, "I consider this a great wonder. The sage, Indra, and Ashraya, blinded by delusion, do not realize how little a Durva sprout weighs. Why would she not ask for a large amount of gold?"

The Ganas said: Kubera gave her a large amount of gold, but fearing her husband’s displeasure and the risk of taking too little or too much, she placed the Durva sprout on a goldsmith’s scale. The gold was not enough to balance the sprout. A merchant’s scale was brought, but it did not balance either. Even on an oil-presser’s scale, the gold could not match the sprout. A vessel was tied, and gold was placed on one side, but the Durva sprout remained heavier. Kubera kept adding more and more gold, but it could not equal the sprout. He gave all the wealth from his treasury, which was like a mountain, yet it could not balance the Durva sprout.

Astonished, Kubera called his wife and said, "At my command, step onto the scale. If it does not balance, I shall step on it myself to preserve my integrity." Following her husband’s order, the chaste wife stepped onto the scale, but even she could not balance it. Kubera then offered his entire city. Still, the sprout remained heavier. Hearing this from the messenger, Indra arrived on his elephant. He placed his own wealth and even himself on the scale, but the Durva sprout did not rise.

Puzzled, Indra remembered Vishnu and Shiva, wishing for their presence. They arrived, and both ascended the scale, but the Durva sprout still did not rise. Then, Shiva, Vishnu, Kubera, Varuna, Indra, Agni, and Vayu all gathered around Kaundinya. The gods, sages, Siddhas, and Vidyadharas arrived at the end of the day like birds returning to their nests. They bowed to the sage with anxious hearts.

They all said, "O Sage, by your sight, our sins have vanished. Through your past merits, our welfare is assured. It is clear today that your wife’s power has surpassed us all. We do not know the glory of this Durva sprout; even the three worlds could not balance a single sprout. What is the glory of the Durva sprout that you offered with devotion on the head of Gajanana (Ganesha)? No sacrifice, charity, vow, penance, or study of the Vedas is equal to it. Who can truly know your glory, you who are a constant devotee of Gajanana?"

Having said this, they all worshipped Gajanana first, then worshipped the sage and his wife, singing and dancing. They chanted, "Neither Brahma, Vishnu, Shiva, Indra, Vayu, Agni, the Sun, Yama, Shesha, the Moon, the Ashvins, the Lord of Speech, Garuda, Kubera, nor Angira can fathom the glory of your unknown form, O God."

Having satisfied the God of Gods, Gajanana, and having taken leave of the sage, they all returned to their respective abodes. Ashraya, having learned of the supreme glory of the Durva, became firm in her husband’s words and worshipped Gajanana with Durva grass. She bowed to her husband Kaundinya, the speaker of truth, and feeling blessed, she lamented her own previous doubts.

Ashraya said, "There is no one as wicked as I, who doubted your words. O Lord, you who are all-knowing and compassionate to all beings, you have acted rightly. Please forgive my offense; I have taken refuge in you."

The next morning, they both rose, bathed, and worshipped the God with Durva grass. Knowing the supreme glory of the Durva with single-minded devotion, they worshipped the God of Gods morning and evening, abandoning other sacrifices and vows. Gajanana, knowing their devotion, was filled with supreme grace and granted them his own abode.

The Ganas said: This supreme glory of the Durva is unfathomable. Even Shesha, Vishnu, and Shiva cannot describe it fully. The three worlds were balanced by a single leaf of it. By remembering the word "Durva," the three types of sins are destroyed, for in remembering it, one remembers Gajanana. Thus, the glory of the Chintamani region is described; hearing, reciting, or meditating upon it grants worldly enjoyment and liberation.

For this reason, the auspicious journey of the three was sent. The Durva reached the God through the mouth of a donkey, through a bull, and through a bundle of grass brought by a Chandala woman to ward off the cold. Even by a strong wind, the Durva reached Gajanana. Because the Durva is dear to him, Vinayaka was pleased and granted them his presence due to their sinlessness. The Lord is pleased even by the scent of Durva; what more can be said of offering it with devotion?

Brahma said: Thus, the King heard the glory of the Durva from the messenger. The sages had never seen or heard of such a thing. They bathed, took Durva sprouts, and worshipped Vinayaka. Their servants also worshipped Gajanana with Durva. They all attained divine bodies, radiant as the sun, hearing the sounds of divine instruments. Mounted on excellent chariots, adorned with divine garments, some reached the abode of Vinayaka, assuming his form.

Many citizens came to witness the great festival and worshipped him with twenty-one Durva sprouts each. Having enjoyed their pleasures, they all reached the abode of Ganesha. Their chariots rose upward due to the accumulation of their merit. Therefore, a devotee of Ganesha must perform worship with Durva. A person who does not worship him with joy using Durva should be considered a Chandala and will suffer in many hells. No human should ever look at the face of such a person. Whoever worships the God of Gods, Gajanana, with Durva, even a sinner who sees him attains purification. If many Durva are not available, one should worship with even a single one; there is no doubt that this worship is multiplied a crore times.

Brahma said: Thus, O King, the various glories of the Durva have been told to you. This history destroys sins upon hearing. It should not be told to those of wicked mind, but may be imparted to a beloved son.

Indra said: Having heard this supreme account from the mouth of Brahma, Indra was overjoyed. He bowed to the Lotus-born (Brahma) and, by his command, returned to his own place, filled with wonder.

Thus ends the sixty-seventh chapter of the Durva Mahatmya in the Upasana Khanda of the Sri Ganesha Purana.

Chapter 68 : Kartavirya's Observance of the Sankashta Chaturthi Vrata

Shurasena said: O Indra, tell me again the story of the Lord of the Ganas. What did the father of Kritavirya do after that?

Indra said: O best of kings, having heard the narrative of the Sankashta Chaturthi and the account of the Durva grass, the king became filled with anxiety. Thinking that there is no salvation for one without a son and wondering how he would ever have one, Kartavirya saw his father in a dream. With choked voices, they could not speak to each other, but they embraced with hearts overwhelmed by love. Then, taking his son by the hand, the father placed him on a couch and said, "My son, you have labored much for the sake of a son. I will tell you one remedy, O sinless one, which was told to me by Narada when he returned from the mortal world. At that time, I went to the abode of Brahma. I bowed to Brahma, the knower of all, and asked, 'O Lotus-seated one, how shall I have a son and progeny?' He told me of the excellent Sankashta Chaturthi vow."

Brahma said: When this vow is performed, O best of kings, all sins are destroyed, and there is no doubt that you will have progeny.

The father said: "It was written exactly as Brahma had spoken. Take this book and perform the vow precisely as prescribed. Until the year is completed, the Lord Siddhivinayaka, the remover of all distress, will be pleased. When He is pleased, you will surely have a son." Having said this, the king’s father disappeared. Then the powerful King Kritavirya awoke. He saw the book in his hand and remembered the meaning of the dream. He shed tears from his eyes, overwhelmed by both grief and joy—grief from the separation from his father and joy at receiving the book.

The ministers arrived and, surrounding the king, said: "O King, cast aside your negligence and be attentive. Abandon your sorrow and tell us the cause of your grief. We too are filled with intense sorrow because of your distress."

Indra said: Hearing the words of the ministers, Kritavirya said to them, "I saw my father in a dream, and my mind is troubled by it. He gave me a book explaining the Sankashta Chaturthi vow and then vanished instantly. I grieve for his absence like a poor man who has lost his wealth. He told me to perform this vow for the sake of a son. When I awoke, O ministers, I saw the book in my hand. I am shedding tears out of wonder, joy, and sorrow."

The ministers said: "He who is the father of all—humans, serpents, and demons—is the very same Gajanan (Ganesha) who has appeared in the form of your father to be pleased with you. He has told you the remedy for progeny, O best of kings. Otherwise, how could there be a book, and what validity would a dream have? Without His grace, even a dream is considered to be the opposite."

Indra said: Hearing the words of the ministers, the king, with an attentive mind, called the scholars and his friends and asked them, "O Brahmanas, tell me the meaning of this book through His grace." Seeing the book, they spoke its meaning in the entire assembly.

The Brahmanas said: "O King, this is a great dialogue between Brahma and Kritavirya. The Chaturthi that destroys all distress is described here. The worship of Ganesha at moonrise is explained in detail. The glory of the Angaraka Chaturthi is also mentioned many times. The offering of Arghya to the deity of the lunar day with mantras, the feeding and worship of twenty-one Brahmanas, and the giving of various gifts to them are prescribed. The fruit of offering Durva grass and the specific fruit of white Durva are described. You have obtained this vow by great fortune, O sinless one. It has not been seen or heard of in the world, and it will be beneficial. By hearing and remembering it, the distress of men is removed."

Indra said: Hearing this from the mouths of the scholars, the king and all the people were filled with wonder and joy, and they worshipped the best of Brahmanas. He gave them clothes, ornaments, jewels, wealth, and grain in abundance. The king then called Atri, his family guru, and after worshipping him according to the rules at an auspicious time, he received the auspicious one-syllable mantra. With singular devotion, meditating on Ganesha and having conquered his senses, he chanted that mantra. He performed the vow that destroys distress for the pleasure of the Lord of the Ganas and for the sake of obtaining a son.

Thus ends the sixty-eighth chapter, titled "Description of the Vow," in the Upasana Khanda of the Sri Ganesha Purana.

Chapter 69 : The Procedure for Sankashta Chaturthi Vrat

Shurasena said: O Lord, how did Lord Brahma instruct Kritavirya to attain success? Please tell me about the excellent Sankashta Chaturthi Vrat.

Indra said: The king, the father of Kritavirya, went to the peaceful Satyaloka and bowed before the omniscient, four-faced Lord Brahma, and asked: O God of Gods, Creator of the Universe, Remover of the sorrows of those who bow to You, I ask You to tell me what is in my heart. How can people who are in distress, whose minds are agitated by anxiety, or who are suffering from the separation of loved ones, achieve success in their tasks? How can they attain the fruits of their labor, sons, prosperity, and wealth? O Lord, what should humans do to destroy all their troubles?

Brahma said: Listen, O King, I will tell you about the Vrat that grants all success. By merely performing this, a person attains what they desire. One should bathe during the day with medicinal herbs and white sesame seeds with a joyful mind. After meditating on Lord Ganesha, one should make a firm resolve and worship Him with devotion using mantras derived from the Agamas. This should be performed on the fourth day of the dark fortnight, at the time of moonrise.

The King asked: O Brahma, please tell me in detail how to worship the Lord of Lords, Ganesha, as I ask this with devotion.

Brahma said: After completing daily duties, at night when the moon rises, in a clean place smeared with cow dung and decorated with a canopy, one should worship the seat of Ganesha with vermilion and unbroken rice grains. Place a pitcher (Kalash) adorned with five gems there. On top of it, place a vessel made of gold, silver, copper, or bamboo. Cover it with a cloth and, according to the Agamic rites, draw a Yantra on it. Place a golden idol of Ganesha, possessing all auspicious signs, upon it.

Meditation: Meditate on the elephant-faced Lord, who has one tusk, a massive body, the luster of molten gold, a large belly, and broad eyes that shine like fire. He is seated on a mouse, fanned by attendants, and wears a serpent as a sacred thread.

Invocation: O Lord of Lords, come and remove my distress. Remain present until this Vrat is completed. (Followed by the recitation of the Purusha Sukta).

Offerings: Offer the seat, water for washing feet (Padya), a respectful offering (Arghya), water for sipping (Achamaniya), the five nectars (Panchamrita), and a bath with water from sacred rivers like the Narmada and Ganga. Offer two garments, a sacred thread, sandalwood paste, unbroken rice, flowers, incense, a lamp, food offerings (Naivedya), fruits, and betel leaves. Offer gold as a donation (Dakshina) to ensure success.

Then, with devotion, offer twenty-one blades of Durva grass while chanting the twenty-one names of Ganesha: Ganaadhipataye, Umaputraya, Aghanashanaya, Ekadantaya, Ibhavaktraya, Mushakavahanaya, Vinayakaya, Ishaputraya, Sarvasiddhipradaya, Lambodaraya, Vakratundaya, Modakapriyaya, Vighnavidhvamsakartre, Vishvavandyaya, Amareshaya, Gajakarnaya, Nagayajnopavitine, Bhalachandraya, Parashudharine, Vighnadhipaya, and Vidyapradaya.

Perform the waving of lights (Nirajana) with camphor, offer flowers, and recite the prayer: You alone create, protect, and destroy the universe. You are the embodiment of all. I bow to the Lord of Ganas, the remover of obstacles, the dispeller of devotee's sorrows, the giver of knowledge, and the primeval treasure of the Vedas.

After praying, bow repeatedly and circumambulate twenty-one times. Pray: O Ganesha, those fools who desire success without worshipping You are surely lost. I have now understood Your full power.

Offer the Vayana (gift) to the teacher, saying: O Teacher, Lord of the twice-born, giver of all success, please accept this Vayana and remove my distress. Offer a special Arghya with water, fruits, and flowers.

Then, perform a Homa with one hundred and eight offerings of Modakas (sweet dumplings). Offer the Vayana, consisting of sweets and fruits covered in red cloth, to the teacher. Offer an Arghya to the lunar day (Tithi), the Deity, and the Moon (while chanting the mantra for the Moon, who was born from the ocean of milk and is associated with Rohini).

Finally, ask for forgiveness from the Lord, feed the Brahmins, and then partake of the remaining food yourself. Observe silence while eating seven morsels. Perform this ritual in this manner for four months.

Chapter 70 : The Story of the Chaturthi Vow and the Victory of Kritavirya

The King asked: O Lord, tell me with compassion, who first performed this vow on earth and who made it known? What is the merit and what is the fruit of this vow?

Brahma said: O King, in ancient times, this vow was performed by Goddess Parvati herself, as instructed by Shiva, during all four months. In the fifth month, she was seen by Aparna (Parvati). Previously, it was performed by Sage Agastya when he wished to drink the ocean. Through the grace of the Lord of Obstacles (Ganesha), he accomplished it in three months. In ancient times, Queen Damayanti performed it for six months while searching for Nala, and subsequently, she found him. O King, in the past, Chitralekha brought the son of Pradyumna. Seeing Pradyumna distressed and grieving for his son, wondering where he had gone or who had taken him, Rukmini spoke to him.

Rukmini said: Listen, my son, I will tell you about a vow I performed in my own palace. When you were taken away by Shambara as a six-day-old infant, my heart was breaking with the sorrow of your separation. I wondered when I would see my beautiful, incomparable son. Seeing the children of other women, the thought would arise in my mind that I too should have a son of similar stature. Many years passed while I was consumed by this anxiety. Then, by divine providence, Sage Lomasha arrived. He instructed me in this excellent Sankashta Chaturthi vow, which removes all anxieties, and I performed it four times. By its grace, you returned after killing Shambara in battle. You too should perform this vow, and then you will find your son.

Brahma said: O Lord of the Ganas, Pradyumna performed this vow previously. He had heard from Narada that his son Aniruddha was imprisoned in the city of Banasura. Following the advice of Uddhava, Krishna performed this excellent vow once, according to the prescribed rites, out of fear of the impending war. He went to Shonitapura, defeated Banasura in battle, and in an instant, brought back Aniruddha along with Usha. I myself, desiring to create the universe, performed this vow, O best of kings, and through its influence, I created the diverse world. It has been performed by other gods, demons, and humans for the pacification of obstacles, as well as by sages, Danavas, Yakshas, Kinnaras, serpents, and Rakshasas.

One should perform this vow even in times of distress for the sake of peace. There is no other vow in this world equal to this one for granting all accomplishments. Neither penance, charity, chanting, pilgrimages, mantras, nor knowledge can compare to it. O King, having heard this story, one should eat in silence. Placing one's hands between the knees, one should meditate on Ganesha in the heart. The remnants of the offering should be consumed with one's relatives. Whether the task is great or small, it will be accomplished without a doubt. What more need be said? There is nothing else that grants success so quickly. This should not be taught to the faithless, the atheist, or the deceitful. It should be given to a son, a disciple, or a virtuous person full of devotion. You are dear to me, O King, you are righteous and the best of the Kshatriyas; you are a benefactor of the people, and thus I have instructed you in this vow. Therefore, among all vows, you must perform this one first. Your tasks will attain success; I have not spoken otherwise. Whenever a person, man or woman, sees a task at hand, they should perform this noble vow. Desired goals will be accomplished; what is difficult when the remover of obstacles is pleased?

Suta said: Having heard the procedure in this way, the noble King performed the vow for the pacification of all sorrows. By the power of the vow, he conquered his enemies and enjoyed his kingdom with his sons, free from all thorns.

Thus ends the seventieth chapter of the Upasana Khanda of the Shri Ganesha Purana, titled The Story of the Chaturthi Vow and the Victory of Kritavirya.

Chapter 71 : The Procedure for Concluding the Vow

The King asked, "O wise one, how should the conclusion (udyapana) of this vow be performed? For the benefit of the people, please explain this to me in detail."

Brahma replied, "O King, to ensure the completion of the vow, the conclusion should be performed in the first, fifth, or seventh month. The devotee should perform the worship according to the previously mentioned procedures. After constructing a beautiful pavilion decorated with various cloths, one should create a Sarvatobhadra mandala adorned with various colors. There, one should worship the Lord of Gods upon a water pot as before. With fragrant sandalwood, various flowers, and a coconut, one should offer the arghya with a focused mind, saying, 'O Goddess, beloved of Ganesha, best among the lunar days, take away my distress; please accept this arghya, salutations to you.' Then, 'O Lambodara, salutations to you, who are always fond of modakas, take away my distress, O Lord; please accept this arghya, salutations to you.' Then, 'O Moon, born from the ocean of milk, originating from the lineage of Atri, along with Rohini, accept this arghya offered by me.'

One should offer various types of food—eatable, drinkable, lickable, and suckable—and satisfy the Lord of the Ganas with many fruits. One should appoint an acharya and twenty-one priests. Using the 'Gananam Tva' mantra, one should perform ten thousand oblations, or alternatively, one thousand, five hundred, or one hundred and eight oblations, followed by the sacrifice (bali). Then, one should perform the purnahuti (final offering) and pour the stream of ghee (vasordhara). After completing the remainder of the ritual, one should feed the brahmanas. One should give them pairs of garments, water pots, and dakshina according to one's capacity, without being stingy.

Afterward, one should honor the acharya with clothes, ornaments, and the like. Once he has eaten, one should offer him a gift (bayana) along with fruits. One should offer a winnowing basket filled with sweet pudding, wrapped in red cloth, along with a golden idol of the Lord of the Ganas and a donation. To ensure the completion of the vow, one should give a measure of sesame seeds, and then a cow with its calf, adorned with ornaments. Then, one should ask for forgiveness from the brahmanas, praying that the Lord of Obstacles may be pleased. By performing the conclusion of this vow, one attains the merit of the Ashvamedha sacrifice."

Kartavirya’s father said, "Thus, this vow was instructed to me by Brahma for the benefit of the world. I have told you this today, O son, with affection for the sake of progeny."

Indra said, "Just as the great vow was instructed by his father, the wise Kartavirya performed it exactly in that manner. The learned ones explained the excellent vow properly. Having installed a golden idol of the Lord accompanied by Siddhi and Buddhi in the center of the great pavilion, it was read by the brahmanas in some places, while in others, the Puranas were recited, and in others, there was singing and dancing. The sounds of various musical instruments reached the heavens. Some people debated, while others acted as mediators.

The wise King chanted the supreme mantra. Having chanted, offered oblations, worshipped, and fed many brahmanas, he gave ten thousand cows to them, and he himself was adorned. He gave food to the poor, the blind, and the destitute, and offered many gifts. He received the blessings of the satisfied, truth-telling, and ascetic brahmanas for a son. Due to their blessings, the Queen soon conceived and, at an auspicious time, gave birth to a son endowed with all good qualities. Overjoyed at the birth of his son, the King gave many gifts. He performed the sacred thread ceremony and the marriage of his son at the appropriate times. After crowning his son, who was endowed with knowledge and wisdom, as the king, he, being a virtuous son and enjoying all pleasures, eventually attained the abode of the Lord. Along with the priests, the learned ones, and all the spectators, they all attained the abode of Ganesha through his merit.

Thus ends the seventy-first chapter of the Upasana Khanda of the Sri Ganesha Purana, describing the procedure for the conclusion of the vow.

Chapter 72 : The Birth of Kartavirya

Shurasena asked, O Lord of the Hundred Sacrifices, how was the son born to them after the observance of the vow? Please tell me everything in detail as I am asking you. Indra replied, O King, what is it that cannot be achieved when the Elephant-faced Lord is pleased? By His grace, the Queen conceived. In the ninth month, she gave birth and saw a son who was a blessing, but he had two shoulders and a beautiful face, yet he was devoid of arms and hands. Seeing him without a nose, lotus-like eyes, thighs, knees, calves, and feet, the mother wept.

She said, why did such a child happen to me, an unfortunate woman? O Elephant-faced Lord, why was he given to me without hands and feet? I was better off being barren; why do I have such a son? Why was the sin of my previous life not destroyed? How did your grace bear fruit, O Lord with the elephant face? Why did the words of the Brahmins become fruitless for me? As she struck her chest and forehead repeatedly while weeping, all the women present there also began to cry. Hearing their clamor, the King also arrived. Hearing the weeping, the ministers also came. Hearing their cries, the citizens also began to weep.

The King said, O Lord Elephant-faced, why is your compassion for the poor like this? Why have you shown me such mercy today? How do you remove sins just by remembrance, O sinless one? My chanting, penance, meditation, charity, worship, and service to the Brahmins, as well as my rituals and fire sacrifices, have all gone in vain. Therefore, destiny is powerful, and effort is meaningless. The course of karma is unknown; one never knows what will happen. Just as a mouse can split a mountain, my son was born this way despite my lifelong efforts. Then the ministers spoke to the grief-stricken King.

The ministers said, O King, enough of this sorrow. How can what is destined be otherwise? Did Rama not know about the deer? Yet he went after it. Did the King of Dharma (Yudhisthira) not know that gambling was forbidden? Yet he went to play, leaving everything behind to go to the forest. Do not weep excessively; his beauty will not be lost. If destiny is favorable, he will be beautiful in the future. Just as a tree bears flowers and fruits in time, so too will the King's son become perfect in time.

Indra continued, hearing the words of the ministers, the King became composed. He said to the Queen, rise, rise, do not grieve. The King himself, with a calm mind, summoned the best of Brahmins and performed the worship of Ganesha and the Swastivachana (blessing ritual). He performed the Abhyudayika Shraddha and gave many gifts—garlands, ornaments, clothes, cows, and jewels. He gave various garments to friends, relatives, and servants, and rewarded the musicians as appropriate. He gave gifts to the bards, the poor, the blind, and the needy. In every house, he distributed betel leaves and sugar. On the eleventh day, he named him Kartavirya. He fed the city with great enthusiasm.

Then twelve years passed for the son. Dattatreya came to his house of his own accord. Kritavirya placed his head at his feet and bowed. The sage rose and embraced the excellent King. After seating him on a beautiful seat, he worshipped him with respect, offering a seat, water for the feet, an offering of respect, a cow, clothes, and a sacred thread. He gave incense, lamps, various fruits, ointments, betel leaves, and gold as a donation. After the King had served the great sage Dattatreya, who was seated comfortably, he spoke to him.

The King said, the merit of my past lives has borne fruit today, as I have seen you with my own eyes, O sage. By your grace, O virtuous one, the future will be even better, for people like me do not usually get to see those who are holy. Hearing the words of Kritavirya, Dattatreya said, I have come today because I wished to see your unique son. Then the King, filled with joy, spoke to the sage again.

The King said, my rituals, penance, charity, and vows have gone in vain. This son given by the Lord of the World is a thorn in my heart. Because of this unsightly son, I have become unsightly myself.

Indra said, the King brought the boy and showed him to the sage. Seeing the son, the great sage, covering his eyes, understood the karma through meditation and said to him, O King, this son will be the one who conquers everyone. You have not performed the Sankashti Vrata properly. This king of vows, which destroys all troubles, has been neglected. The son was born without limbs, but through the proper means, he will become whole.

The King said, you have spoken the truth, O Lord. Please show me mercy and tell me the way by which, through your grace, my son may become whole.

Indra said, the sage, filled with compassion, spoke to the son. He taught him the single-syllable mantra and told him to worship Ganesha with devotion using this mantra. Follow the rules of fasting and eating only once a day, O virtuous one. After twelve years, He will grant you a vision. By the mere glance of His eyes, you will attain a divine body. Having said this and bidding farewell to the King, the great sage disappeared. After the sage left, the son, who was without feet but of great mind, said to Kritavirya, take me to the forest. I will perform the ritual to please the Elephant-faced Lord. Hearing his words, his parents wept, but the father sent him to the forest in a carriage. The servants placed him in a leaf hut and returned to the city. He stayed there, determined to perform penance.

Chapter 73 : The Glory of the Chaturthi Vrat

Indra said: He chanted the mantra instructed by his guru with strict discipline, meditating upon the elephant-headed deity with unwavering devotion. Consuming only air and remaining without food, he stood as still as a stone or a rock. In this manner, twelve years passed as he performed his penance. Seeing the Mahatma, a boy devoid of hands and feet, having attained liberation after twelve years, Gajanana (Ganesha) emerged from the middle of the pond, appearing in a form made of coral. Pleased by his devotion and steadfastness, He spoke, facing him.

Ganesha said: Since you have stayed for twelve years in this desolate forest, which is frequented by lions and tigers and filled with dense thickets, I shall grant you a boon. Tell me what is in your mind. Hearing these words and regaining consciousness of his body, the son of Kritavirya bowed to the elephant-faced Lord while many sages in their celestial chariots listened.

The son said: O Lord, may I always have unwavering devotion to Your lotus feet; I have no desire to ask for anything else. Nevertheless, O Lord of Gods, for the sake of my parents' happiness, grant my body beauty that brings joy to all.

Indra said: Hearing his words, the illusory Gajanana, assuming a subtle form, entered into his body with affection. As He entered, the prince attained a divine body and instantly became thousand-armed. He stood with straight legs, appearing like a mountain. The gods and divine sages showered flowers upon him and praised the Lord with songs and musical instruments. Kartavirya, adorned with a thousand arms, roared. Hearing his voice, which resembled the rumbling of clouds, the Lord of Death (Yama) became terrified, let alone others. All the kings on earth trembled in fear, thinking that he could release five hundred arrows at once in battle.

Brahma and the other gods descended from their chariots and said to him: By remembering you, the gods shall regain what was lost. You will remove the afflictions of all people in their hearts. Since you are the direct manifestation of Vishnu, you will be known as Sahasrarjuna and will be famous in the three worlds for as long as the Kalpa lasts. You will be devoted to protecting the virtuous, a guardian of those who seek refuge, a conqueror of all enemies, and the sovereign of the earth. Having granted these various boons, the host of gods disappeared. All the kings brought elephants, horses, human-drawn carriages, umbrellas, whisks, lamps, chariots, and other gifts.

Then, the prince, led by Brahmins, installed the coral idol of the Lord of Ganas in the center of a grand palace. The Brahmins named it Pravala-Ganapa (the Coral Ganesha). He gave villages to the Brahmins for the worship of the deity he had installed. This place became famous on earth as the Pravala-Kshetra, the granter of success. There, Shesha performed penance to support the earth. He received many boons from Ganesha, including the strength to hold the earth and omniscience. He also received the distinction of being the chief among the nine serpent families, with a thousand heads. Because of what he had installed long ago with a very satisfied mind, he became known by a second name, Dharanidhara (the Supporter of the Earth), which grants all desires to those who remember or hear of it.

After worshipping all the eminent Brahmins and taking leave of all the kings, the thousand-armed prince went to his city to see his parents. They were overjoyed to see him and the city in such a state, and they gave various gifts to the leading Brahmins.

Indra said: Thus, the wonderful glory of the Sankashti Chaturthi Vrat, which originated from the son of Kritavirya, has been described. It has been experienced by the gods and kings like Chandrasena. It is most holy and grants success even by mere remembrance. By its grace, Ravana attained universal knowledge, and in the past, the Pandavas regained their kingdom. If the merit of this Vrat is given into my hand, only then will this chariot move toward Amaravati.

Kuta said: Thus, the glory of this Vrat was heard from the mouth of Shakra (Indra) by King Shurasena, who was immersed in the ocean of his own bliss. Having worshipped the lotus feet of Indra, the excellent King Shurasena, feeling satisfied, spoke after hearing the divine story arising from the Chaturthi Vrat.

He said: My past merits have borne fruit, for I have heard of such a Vrat. There is nothing more meritorious than this in the three worlds. Having said this, Shurasena himself began to observe the Vrat of Shakra.

Chapter 74 : The Story of the Chandala Who Taught the Sankashta Chaturthi Vrata

Vyasa said: O Brahma, how did King Shurasena perform the vow? Having heard this excellent history from the mouth of Indra, tell me.

Brahma said: Then Indra commanded his messengers, "Go to the city and bring the one who has performed the Sankashta Chaturthi vow." The messengers went in haste and searched from house to house. While wandering, they saw a beautiful and auspicious celestial chariot. Inside was a wicked Chandali woman, suffering from leprosy, her face oozing, covered in flies, worms, and a foul stench. She had a shriveled belly, long hair, and dried-up teeth, eyes, and nose. She was extremely filthy, with long ear holes and a harsh voice.

The king’s messengers, seeing her being taken by the attendants of Ganesha, were astonished. They bowed and asked the divine messengers, the servants of the Lord of Ganas, "She is extremely vile by birth; how is she going to heaven? Who was she in the past, and how did she become like this? By what merit are you taking her to heaven? Tell us all this, if it is possible."

The divine messengers said: In the region of Bengal, there was a Kshatriya named Sharangadhara. This woman was his daughter, Sundari by name. She had a voice like a cuckoo, a moon-like face, and beauty that surpassed even Rati. She was so beautiful that even the famous celestial nymphs were not worthy to serve her, and she could bewilder the minds of sages with a mere glance. Young men would lose their self-control just by seeing her beauty. She became a world-enchanting prostitute, addicted to illicit affairs, wearing expensive clothes and ornaments, and shameless in the city of Bengal. She always deceived her husband, Chitra, squandering the vast wealth earned by her father.

Once, leaving her husband in bed while she was dressed beautifully, she went out at midnight. Her husband, Chitra, caught her hand in anger and scolded her, "Shame on you, you sinful woman, always engaged in illicit affairs!" Hearing those words, her anger subsided, but being intoxicated and emboldened by consuming forbidden food, she grabbed a knife in the dark and, with her right hand, pierced the belly of her husband, Chitra. She then went to meet the lover who was in her mind. While she was enjoying herself, the neighbors, who were awake, learned of her deeds and reported them to the king. The king’s messengers waited in the dark until she returned home. They seized her, brought her before the king, and by his order, they took her outside and executed her.

She was taken to a terrible hell by Yama’s messengers, where she remained face down, bitten severely by worms. She suffered immense pain, remembering her past deeds. At the end of the Kalpa, she was born in the mortal world as a wretched Chandali. Once, having drunk liquor, she was sleeping during the day, and in the first watch of the night, she woke up feeling extremely hungry. She went to the house of a person who was observing the vow to beg for food. The food given to her by him was eaten by her at the break of dawn. By divine coincidence, while eating, she muttered "Ganesha," and at that very moment, an auspicious chariot was sent by the Lord of Ganas.

Brahma said: Hearing the words of the divine messengers, the king’s messengers bowed and said: We have seen this wonderful event. Listen to the message the king gave us. Indra, seated in his excellent chariot, went to see Gritsamada and Bhrishundi. Having seen, bowed to, and worshipped them, he returned to his city. As he was returning, his chariot fell near the city of Shurasena due to the gaze of a leper, the son of a Vaishya. Shurasena went there, bowed, and worshipped him, and asked for the means to make the chariot move again. Indra said, "It will move due to the merit of the Sankashta Chaturthi vow; make an effort for that." We, the messengers, came to search for that person by his order. If this Chandali has performed this vow, then take her to King Shurasena. The Chandali will bestow the merit of the Sankashta vow upon him. With double the merit, she will return to the chariot, and the chariot will return to Indra’s city. If you agree, let this be done for the sake of the King of Gods.

Hearing this, the servants of the gods said: We do not have the command of Ganesha to give her to anyone else. Saying this, when they lifted her and placed her in the chariot, she immediately gained divine radiance, divine clothes, and ornaments. The divine messengers took her away amidst the sound of celestial instruments. As the king’s messengers returned, they told the story to Shurasena. While they were speaking, the chariot arrived with the Chandali. They saw it shining and illuminating all directions. Her gaze fell upon that chariot of the King of Gods. By the touch of the wind from the chariot, it ascended upwards while all the worlds and sages watched in amazement. Everyone went to their respective places, and Indra returned to Amaravati. She also reached the abode of Ganesha, possessing a divine body.

He who listens to this story properly or makes others listen to it with effort, having abandoned sin through the merit of the Sankashta Chaturthi vow, shall attain all his desires and be freed from all distress.

Chapter 75 : The Glory of the Sankashta Chaturthi Vrat

Brahma said: Having heard and seen the greatness and the origin of the Vrat, Shurasena, desiring to perform it himself, spoke to the best of sages, Vashistha. The King asked, "O Sage, please tell me the auspicious time for the Sankashta Chaturthi Vrat. I wish to perform this Vrat, which yields immediate results." Vashistha replied, "O best of kings, perform this excellent Vrat on the Tuesday that falls on the Krishna Chaturthi of the month of Magha. It grants all accomplishments and fulfills all desires."

Brahma continued: The King, having gathered all the necessary materials and accompanied by his wife, was filled with great devotion. When the auspicious day mentioned by Vashistha arrived, he prepared to perform the excellent Sankashta Chaturthi Vrat. After completing his daily ablutions and rituals, he worshipped Lord Ganesha, sought the blessings of the learned Brahmins, and obtained permission from Vashistha. Fixing his mind on Ganesha and chanting His name, he stood on one leg until the sun set.

He then bathed again, performed the evening prayers, and began the worship with Vashistha’s guidance. He prepared a grand pavilion decorated with banana stalks, adorned with various garments and ornaments, and beautified with umbrellas and whisks. It was radiant with rows of mirrors, flower garlands, various gemstones, and rows of lamps. In the center, he placed a golden image of the elephant-headed Lord upon a golden vessel, an image beautiful in every limb and adorned with jewels. As the leading Brahmins chanted, singers sang, musicians played, and dancers performed, he worshipped the idol with Vedic and Puranic hymns.

He offered sixteen types of services, beginning with the Panchamrita. He placed before the Lord offerings of modaks, cakes, laddus, sweet rice, and various delicious dishes. The King offered fragrant water and various fruits, betel leaves, and gifts of gold and gems to satisfy the Lord. He offered durva grass, performed the waving of lights (arathi), and offered flowers with mantras. Finally, he offered the arghya to the Moon for the sake of the Lord of the Ganas. He worshipped the Brahmins with respect, fed them, and gave them ten thousand cows along with clothes, ornaments, and donations.

Then, the King ate with his friends and relatives and spent the night in vigil, accompanied by music, songs, and stories. At dawn, he bathed, worshipped the Lord again as before, and gifted the idol of Ganesha along with donations to Vashistha. Pleased, Lord Ganesha sent a celestial chariot adorned with all riches. The King, appearing in the form of Ganesha, was placed in the chariot by the Lord’s messengers, while all the people watched, their hearts filled with joy.

As the King ascended toward the divine realm due to his merits, the messengers—who themselves appeared in the form of Ganesha, adorned with crowns and jewels—said to him, "Lord Ganesha, pleased with you, has sent this chariot. We have come because He was eager to see you." Hearing this, the King shed tears of joy. With his body trembling in ecstasy, Shurasena smiled and said to them, "I am not worthy of being seen by the Unmanifest, the Immeasurable, the Eternal Lord of the Universe, who is beyond speech and mind. Even the Vedas and scriptures cannot fully describe Him, and He is the one whom the gods, headed by Shiva, constantly meditate upon. If He has remembered me, then my fortune is truly great."

Hearing this, the messengers replied, "O King, we do not know the extent of the glory of His devotees. We only know that through Him, the formless and attributeless Lord assumes form." The King then said, "I have one great desire. If the Lord and you are pleased, how can I leave my city and go to Ganesha? I have never enjoyed any happiness without my people; how can I enjoy supreme bliss without them?" The messengers replied, "May your wish be fulfilled, otherwise Ganesha will be angry with us."

Brahma said: Then, in an instant, they placed all the people into the chariot. All living beings, including the four types of creatures, were taken aboard. Everyone was adorned with divine garments and ornaments. They wondered, "What is this great miracle? We have not performed any merit, so how did we get such a chariot?" Others said, "This is due to the power of the King’s merit. Just as base metal turns to gold by the power of an alchemical substance, or a great sinner attains perfection through the words of a saint, we have all been uplifted by the King’s merit."

Then the messengers of Ganesha tried to move the chariot upward, but it remained stuck and would not rise from the ground. Everyone became doubtful, wondering how they would reach heaven. They said to each other, "How can one who has no merit possess such a treasure? How can a beggar’s bowl reach the highest shelf?" They noticed a leper among them. Someone said to the King, "Abandon this leper. If he is removed, the chariot will rise." Shurasena, not wanting to abandon him, said to the messengers, "I am the one who has committed sins; I should be the one abandoned, not these people. Or, tell me his past sins and his previous birth, and out of compassion and my knowledge of all things, I shall find a way."

The messengers replied, "O King, do not worry. We shall tell you of the leper’s past sins, his birth, and the remedy."

Thus ends the seventy-fifth chapter of the Upasana Khanda of the Shri Ganesha Purana, describing the fruits of the Chaturthi Vrat performed by Shurasena.

Chapter 76 : The Story of Durvaputrashuddha and the Greatness of the Chaturthi Vrata and the Name Durva

The Messengers said: In the region of Gauda, in the city of Gauda, there once lived a Brahmin who was ascetic, knowledgeable, wise, and a worshipper of gods and Brahmins. A son was born to him, and his mother was named Shakini. His wife, Savitri, was as chaste as the goddess Savitri herself. Because he was their only son, they adorned him with jewels and loved him dearly. He was extremely handsome and shone like the husband of Rati (Kamadeva). His parents could not bear to be separated from him even for a moment.

However, when he reached youth, he abandoned his wife and became addicted to other women, constantly slandering others. He became sinful and disregarded his father’s words. Once, a captivating prostitute arrived in that city. The son, Budha, became obsessed with her. Listen to what he did: he forcibly took his parents' ornaments from their box and, after stealing them right in front of them, gave them to the prostitute and played with her intensely. Abandoning all sense and duty, he became solely devoted to her, like a yogi devoted to Brahman. He was consumed by the fire of lust, intoxicated as if by liquor.

His father, filled with grief and neglecting his own hunger and thirst, searched for his son in every house in the city. Not finding him, he became breathless with anxiety. At midnight, he said to his wife, "Where has our son gone? Without him, the house is empty, just as the night is empty without the moon. Without water, a pond is useless; without a child, a woman is incomplete. When will I see him, the protector of my life?"

Shakini replied, "I am exhausted by hunger and thirst, filled with worry and grief. I do not know where our dear son has gone. If I see him, I shall live; otherwise, I cannot." The father then set out again, holding a staff in his hand. He asked everyone he met on the road about his son. When he became exhausted and hungry, he grew delirious. He met a very old and fearsome man of the lowest caste named Bhima. When asked about his son, Bhima replied clearly, "Your son Budha is at the prostitute's house, playing happily, lost in lust. Why do you grieve in vain, O best of Brahmins? Whose son, mother, or father is he?"

Durva (the father) asked, "How can my son be happy, addicted to a prostitute?" He went to her house immediately and saw his son there, intoxicated, his eyes red from wine. The father grabbed him by the hand and scolded him in anger, "A thorn tree or a stone is better than you. Why do you not end your life? What is the point of living like this?"

Brahma said: Hearing his father's words, the angry Budha struck his father in the face. He said, "O lowest of men, why do you disturb me during my time of pleasure?" Suddenly, a crow's excrement fell upon him, and blaming his father for it, he kicked him so hard that he took his life. As his father died, he uttered "Hari-Ishwara," and the son, tied him by the feet and threw him far away. He then drank wine and enjoyed himself with the prostitute as he pleased.

In the morning, he went home and saw his mother. She embraced him with joy, her breasts flowing with milk from affection, and asked, "Where have you been? What have you done? Tell me in detail, my son. Your father is deeply grieved, and I have been awake all night without food or water. He went to look for you; go and see your father." Because she kept insisting, the son, in a fit of rage, struck her on the head with a dry piece of wood, and she fell to the ground. Realizing she was unconscious, he tied her up and threw her outside, then went back to the prostitute's house and played, feeling great joy. The virtuous people of the city burned the couple on their own funeral pyres. The king did not punish him because he was a Brahmin.

When he returned home, his wife spoke to him gently. Savitri said, "O Lord of my life, listen to me. You were born into a noble and pure lineage; such wicked conduct should be abandoned through wisdom. One should perform actions in this life that bring happiness in the next. One should act in youth so that there is peace in old age. Because of your father's love and your Brahmin status, the king forgives you. Why do you abandon me, your lawful, beautiful, and devoted wife, to be addicted to a prostitute? The world slanders you everywhere, though they do not say it to your face out of fear. People ask me, 'What kind of husband do you have, O beautiful one?' and I feel like dying of shame. If you would enjoy yourself with me, no one would say anything, and there would be no great sin. If you consider my words, abandon her through your own wisdom. A wise man should abandon actions done out of ignorance or mistake."

Stung by his wife's words, the cruel and angry Budha replied, "O shameless, harsh, and wicked woman, you shall meet the same fate as my parents." Savitri replied, "How can he who has no respect for his parents or teachers be my protector? For a virtuous woman, death at the hands of her husband is better in this world and the next."

Brahma said: As his wife spoke, he grabbed her hair and beat her with wood, stones, and fists. Remembering Rama as her husband, she died instantly from the injuries. After going to heaven, she enjoyed supreme happiness. He dragged her by the feet and threw her far away. Unrestrained and joyful, he continued to revel with the prostitute.

After a long time, Budha went to the house of Kalabhairava. While he was bathing, the wicked man grabbed the wife of Kalabhairava by her hair. He kissed and embraced the beautiful woman and took her to the bed. She cursed him, "O wicked-minded, lowest of men, because you have forced yourself upon me while my husband, the great sage Kalabhairava, was away, you shall become a leper. In your next life, no one shall even take your name."

Brahma said: Terrified, Budha returned to the prostitute's house and drank wine, thinking of nothing. How can I describe such wicked deeds? One who speaks of others' sins loses their own merit. Eventually, he died and was taken by the messengers to the abode of Yama. Yama ordered, "Why have you brought him here? Take him to the hells." The messengers threw him into hell until the end of the cosmic cycle.

Having suffered his karma, he was born in the house of a Vaisya. Due to the sage's wife's curse, he was a leper. He was a killer of his father, mother, and wife, a drunkard, and a violator of his teacher's bed. One should bathe with clothes on just by touching him. His name should not be taken, as it brings great sin. When such a wicked soul is finally abandoned by his body, he will surely go to the higher realms; there is no doubt about this.

Brahma said: Hearing these words from the messengers, Shurasena was trembling. He asked the messengers, "I did not know of these sins." The king rose and bowed down in great distress, asking them to show mercy and tell him the way to remove all the sins of this wicked man.

The messengers said, "Rise, O King. Listen with a focused mind to the way to remove these sins. There is a four-syllable name of Ganesha that is famous. Chant it into his ear, and all his sins will be destroyed. Just as the sun destroys all darkness at dawn, there is no other way for him; let the name be chanted."

Brahma said: Following the messengers' words, Shurasena chanted the four-syllable name into the Vaisya's ear three times. As soon as he heard "Gajanana," he attained a divine body, illuminating all directions like the rising sun. With all his sins destroyed by the mere hearing of the name, he boarded a celestial chariot. By the command of Ganesha, the messengers took him to the divine abode of the Lord of Obstacles.

Everything you asked, O sinless one, has been told. This Sankashti Chaturthi vow is highly meritorious, righteous, fame-giving, life-extending, and grants all accomplishments. It pacifies all suffering and destroys all obstacles. What else do you wish to hear?

Chapter 77 : The Description of Jamadagni's Greatness

Vyas said: Tell me, who else performed this great wonder?

Brahma said: Long ago, Jamadagni’s son, Rama, performed this vow. He attained fame, victory, knowledge, and a long life.

Vyas said: O Grandfather, how was Rama born, from whom, and of whom? Tell me all this in detail as I ask.

Brahma said: In Shvetadvipa lived the renowned great sage Jamadagni. He was a knower of the three times, who could create, destroy, punish, or bless through his mere will, causing even the gods to tremble. He had a wife named Renuka, whose beauty was such that even the goddess of love, Rati, could not compare. She was known throughout the worlds as Rati herself, and who could describe the one by whom all are enchanted? For the sake of her eyes’ beauty, the chakora birds and the deer in the forest performed penance, and even the moon serves the Lord to attain the beauty of her face. She was the primordial nature, the eternal goddess.

From this highly virtuous Jamadagni, who was the embodiment of the Lord, and his wife, the divine Rama was born, just as Vishnu, the master of yoga, manifests.
