He was extremely handsome, the charmer of the god of love himself, obedient to his parents, and famous for his strength and valor. He was devoted to the worship of gods, Brahmins, gurus, the wise, and cows. He was a scholar of the Vedas, Vedangas, Shastras, and Smritis. In speech, he was like Brihaspati; in patience, like the Earth; and in depth, like the ocean. He was devoted to his parents' words.

Having taken his parents' permission, he went to Naimisharanya to focus his mind on the study of various sciences. While he was away, the mighty King Kartavirya arrived. His power held the entire world in submission, and he was a partial incarnation of Vishnu, bestowing all wealth. In battle, he could release five hundred arrows at once to destroy his enemies. He was served by the hosts of Indra and was famous for his strength. His elephants, chariots, horses, and infantry were countless. When he marched, his armies covered the earth like clouds covering the sky during the rainy season. Hearing the sound of his conch, his enemies fled in all ten directions, just as herds of wild elephants flee upon hearing a lion’s roar. When the king struck his five hundred palms together, the entire universe trembled.

Once, this king, accompanied by his fourfold army, dressed in blue, under a blue canopy, wandered through forests, rivers, fortresses, and mountains, hunting various animals. On the peak of the Sahya mountain, he saw a hermitage as excellent as the abode of Shiva on Mount Kailash. The king asked his attendants whose place it was.

The attendant said: A great sage named Jamadagni lives here, who is like another sun. He is a man of great fortune, capable of bestowing curses and blessings. The mere sight of him destroys millions of sins. If you wish, we shall go there, and you will have his audience. Your grace will bring benefit to everyone.

Brahma said: Hearing this, the king prepared to go. He left his entire army behind, taking only his chief men, and went to the sage Jamadagni, the treasure of penance. He saw him sitting on kusha grass, shining like fire, and bowed down with his entire body, as did all who accompanied him. The soldiers sat in the spacious halls and groves, while the king stood before the sage. Directed by the sage to a seat, the son of Kritavirya sat down, surrounded by his four companions. The sage honored them all with water for the feet, offerings, and seats. The sage then gave them cows and honored all his disciples.

The soldiers, exhausted from hunting, bathed in the clear, cool waters of the various lakes. Hearing the sounds of the Vedas, the recitation of scriptures, and the music played by the disciples, the lotus-eyed king said to the great sage: Today my parents are blessed, and my birth, knowledge, and penance have borne fruit. The tree of merit has blossomed today because I have seen you. Today my wealth, my lineage, and my fame are blessed. You are undoubtedly the Supreme Brahman. Seeing your hospitality, my mind is completely satisfied.

Hearing these words, the sage, with a pleasant mind and a gentle smile, asked him, even though he already knew: Who are you, whose son are you, what is your name, and what is your purpose?

The King said: For a king who performs his duties, there is no greater purpose than seeing someone like you. I am the son of Kritavirya, known as Kartavirya. With your permission, I shall return to my city.

The Sage said: O best of kings, I have heard of your great fame. My desire to see you has been fulfilled today. My body, soul, penance, knowledge, and hermitage have all become meaningful today because of your arrival. How can you leave without eating anything? What would be lacking in me, and how would I be praised in the world? Please eat something and make me feel fulfilled, O Lord.

The King said: It is true that it is time to eat, and I should eat at your command. Even a Brahmin would drink water if food were unavailable. However, I cannot eat while leaving behind my countless soldiers. I know, O Brahman, that there is no lack of power here to feed everyone. Having seen you, I am satisfied and shall depart now.

The Sage said: Do not worry, O royal sage. I will feed you and your entire army. What is impossible for those who practice penance? If there is an army at your home, summon them as well. Rest for a moment on the beautiful bank of the river. You will see a wonder once the food is ready.

Brahma said: Hearing the sage’s words, the son of Kritavirya, filled with wonder, went to the beautiful riverbank. The sage called his wife and told her the situation. They summoned the Kamadhenu (wish-fulfilling cow) and worshipped her, praying: O Cow, protect our honor. The king has come with an countless army and has been invited to eat. Provide food according to the taste of the king and his army. Do this instantly, or else our truth will be lost, and we will face disgrace in the world. Do as you wish.

Thus prayed to, the Kamadhenu, by her own power, instantly created a great city, beautiful with various palaces, adorned with pillars of jewels, and filled with assembly halls. It was decorated with gardens of various flowers and vines, flags and banners, and echoed with the sound of various musical instruments. It was filled with rows of golden vessels and four types of food. It was adorned with great arches and surrounded by moats. It was bustling with many servants and maids.

People stood here and there, preventing others from entering, saying there was no permission from the sage Jamadagni. When the countless vessels were served, shining with the light of lamps, filled with various dishes, sweets, milk-preparations, and five types of nectar, along with foods to be chewed, licked, sucked, and drunk, and seasoned with sea salt, lime, mango, nectar, wood-apple, camphor, cardamom, asafoetida, and pepper, the sage then called his disciples to invite the son of Kritavirya and his army to eat, due to the complete satisfaction of the divine Kamadhenu.

Chapter 78 : The Tale of Kartavirya

Brahma said: Then the sage told his disciples to go quickly and invite the king, who was residing on the riverbank. The disciples went to the king, offered their respects, conveyed the sage’s blessings, and delivered the invitation. They said, "O King, please come with your army to dine; countless dishes prepared with the six tastes have been laid out for you."

Brahma said: Upon hearing this, King Kartavirya rose, summoned his soldiers, bathed, and set out with his entire army. When they arrived at the sage’s hermitage, they beheld a sight never before seen or heard of in the three worlds—the grandeur of the entrance was beyond compare. Though the king was initially stopped by the gatekeepers, the disciples intervened, and he was led into the dining hall. As the king stood in the center, he marveled at the sage's wealth, thinking that such splendor was not even found in the realms of Vishnu or Shiva, nor in the abode of Brahma, the creator and protector. The sage then came forward and directed everyone to their respective seats. Following the king's permission, the disciples served the soldiers and those dining outside.

Once everyone was seated, the king was also given his place. As the music began to play, they all started to eat. The food, consisting of delicious roots and fruits, was of a kind never seen before, and the guests whispered to one another, wondering what it was. They were amazed at how such a feast could be prepared in just an hour. Having eaten to their heart's content, they were satisfied and finished their meal. They returned their plates, and the disciples cleared away the remnants and the utensils. Afterward, in another spacious hall prepared with comfortable bedding, they enjoyed sugarcane, grapes, mangoes, jackfruit, and pomegranates. They were also offered betel leaves, cloves, camphor, and cardamom, which the sage provided. Finally, the sage joyfully distributed clothes and ornaments to all, giving the most precious gifts to the king.

The sage said: "What can a forest-dwelling sage offer a king like you, who already possesses everything and is like Vishnu himself? By accepting my invitation, you have brought great fame to me in the three worlds. It is a virtuous act for a powerful king to honor the words of a humble sage. When a great person fulfills the request of someone lowly, it is considered an act of righteousness, bringing immense glory in all worlds. By honoring my request, you have also warded off great misfortune."

Brahma said: Hearing these words from Jamadagni, the king asked, filled with astonishment, "I saw nothing at first. Was this done through magic, or is it the power of your penance? Tell me, O virtuous one."

The sage replied: "I have never spoken a lie, even in jest. I tell you the truth: all of this was accomplished by the Kamadhenu (the wish-fulfilling cow)."

Brahma said: A person whose destiny is unfavorable often develops a wicked mind. Influenced by evil planetary alignments, the king, despite being fully satisfied after the meal, began to covet the Kamadhenu. He said, "O Sage, what use is a cow to sages like you, who are peaceful, eat only roots and fruits, have conquered your senses, and are intent on liberation? You live in the forest, practice yoga, and study the scriptures—what need have you for such wealth? A great object belongs to a great purpose. It is not fitting for a forest-dweller like you to possess such a great treasure. Therefore, O Brahmin, you should joyfully give me the Kamadhenu. Consider it as if it were still with you, residing in your heart. Respect the boundaries of kings, for what is there in these worlds that a powerful king cannot take, whether in his own kingdom or another?"

Brahma said: Hearing these words from the wicked-minded Kartavirya, the great sage Jamadagni flared up with anger, like a fire fueled by abundant ghee. With reddened eyes, he spoke to the king as if teaching him a lesson.

The sage said: "I invited a virtuous and noble king to dine, but I did not realize the crookedness hidden in your heart, like that of a heron. Just as a cuckoo raises a crow’s chick through deception, only for it to reveal its true nature in the end, I was the one who was deluded. I have never seen or heard of such a betrayal of friendship in this world."

Thus ends the seventy-eighth chapter of the Upasana Khanda in the Shri Ganesha Purana, titled "The Tale of Kartavirya."

Chapter 79 : The Tale of Kartavirya

The Sage said: You have initiated a deed that contradicts your righteous conduct; by inviting your own destruction, you desire the Kamadhenu. You are deluded, O King, for you seek what is unattainable. The sin born from the destruction of the three worlds will fall upon your head.

Brahma said: Pierced by these words as if by arrows, the King became like the fire of universal dissolution. Burning with rage, he vomited fire from his mouth and spoke in great fury to the sage Jamadagni. The King said: O wicked one, I have never heard such insolent words from anyone. Because you are a Brahmin, I have tolerated this harsh speech.

The Sage said: Then the King arose and quickly commanded his messengers to release the Kamadhenu from her tether and bring her to him immediately. When the messengers surrounded the Kamadhenu at his command, she merely let out a snort, and they lost their lives and ascended to heaven. The King then burned other messengers with the fire of his rage. By the force of her breath, the warriors were blown into the sky, covering the sun so that nothing could be seen. The directions became shrouded in darkness, the sky vanished from sight, the earth trembled, and the trees fell, shaken to their roots.

The terrified soldiers fled in all ten directions. The cow, struck by a whip from someone, flew through the air and charged at the army, just as a lion attacks a herd of elephants or Garuda attacks serpents. A great cry of distress arose from the fleeing warriors. Kartavirya, the mighty one, said to them: Do not be afraid. When I blow my conch with joy, the Kamadhenu will fearfully return to her stall. Behold my prowess and see what the Kamadhenu truly is.

He then blew his great conch, filling the three worlds with its sound. But the Kamadhenu did not fear; instead, the King's servants struck her with iron rods and clubs. Wherever she was struck on her body, various warriors fully armed with weapons emerged. Shakas and Barbaras emerged from her hair, and various other Yavana warriors and fighters emerged from her feet. There were also hosts of horses, elephants, and powerful charioteers. They fought there against the soldiers of Kartavirya.

The soldiers of Kartavirya fell, struck by these warriors. Others gathered there at night like moths falling into a fire. Hundreds of men fell, killed by each other's blows, breaking weapons against weapons and engaging in hand-to-hand combat. In such a tumultuous battle, with weapons falling everywhere, it could not be distinguished who was one's own and who was the enemy. With the sun obscured by dust, they slaughtered each other, and a great clamor arose, crying, "Kill! Kill!" The air was thick with the neighing of horses, the trumpeting of elephants, the shouts of warriors, the clatter of chariot wheels, and the sounds of drums, cymbals, and flutes.

Thus, a great battle occurred between the forces of the cow and those of Kartavirya, terrifying to all beings and demons, pleasing to birds and jackals, and bringing fear to the wives of the warriors. Some had their knees broken, others their heads. There was no counting the broken swords, shields, spears, arrows, bows, warriors, and charioteers. The remaining soldiers of Kartavirya fled, and the forces of the cow followed them, laughing but not killing them. They reproached the King, asking what evil the sage had done to him, for it was the King's own past sins that had corrupted his mind.

When his army was thus shattered, the son of Kritavirya arose. He took five hundred bows and arrows in his hands. Placing his left knee on the ground and drawing his bows with speed, the mighty-armed King released a volley of arrows at the cow's army. But the King's volley of arrows was fruitless, like the actions of the wicked or the intimacy of a barren woman. Again and again, the noble King released arrows, yet not a single warrior of the Kamadhenu was harmed.

When his volley of arrows proved useless, the King became distressed, worrying about where his strength had gone. Thinking that one should not strike those who are confused, the warriors of the cow ascended to heaven. The Kamadhenu, filled with pride, thought, "What is the use of fighting with a weakling?"

When the Kamadhenu had departed, Kartavirya went to the sage and said in anger, "O Brahmin, I have understood your deceit. A Brahmin who harbors deceit in his heart is not to be respected." Saying this, he took one arrow and pierced the noble sage. As the great arrow pierced his heart, the sage gave up his life. Renuka said to the King, "You have committed the sin of Brahmin-slaughter in vain."

With bloodshot eyes and filled with rage, the King said to her, "Stay silent, or I will kill you too, O wife of the sage." The wicked King struck her in anger with twenty-one arrows. Even then, she remembered the sage Jamadagni in her mind. Afterwards, she said to the King, "O wicked outcast, what have you done? Why have you killed us without any offense? Your arms will surely be destroyed; there is no doubt about this."

Hearing her words, the King departed. With his remaining few soldiers, he went to his city, filled with anxiety, lamenting his own actions and grieving for his dead soldiers. Devoid of enthusiasm and effort, he entered his own palace.

Chapter 80 : Renuka's Abandonment of Her Body

Renuka, deeply distressed after he had left, lamented, wondering where her sons had gone now that this destruction had arrived. She pondered what she should do with her husband dead and her body pierced by a multitude of arrows, asking where her beloved son Rama, who possessed such great wrath, had gone. She felt that her life force would depart to the realm of the gods the moment she saw him. Rama, summoned by her mere thought, arrived before his mother. He saw her covered in arrows and his father lying dead, struck in the heart by the wicked Kartavirya’s sharp shafts. Rama fell to the ground in a faint, like a tree broken by the wind, and then wept bitterly in great sorrow for his parents.

Rama said, Today there is darkness everywhere, and the ten directions are void. Just as the earth is without Mount Meru or Amaravati is without Indra, this hermitage does not shine, being bereft of my father today. Just as the three worlds are incomplete without the Ganges, this circle of the hermitage does not shine without Renuka. The gods have lost their fear, and the sages are now without a protector; what will become of the merit he earned through his penance? Rama lamented in many such ways, struggling intensely like a fish out of water. Then, weeping, he approached his mother and removed the arrows while holding her head in his lap.

Rama wept again, pained by his mother’s suffering, and asked how she, who was capable of reducing the three worlds to ashes, had fallen to the ground, tormented by the wicked one’s arrows. He asked why she was preparing to leave him, reminding her that she never forgot him even for a moment when he was playing in the past. He asked how she could leave him now, noting that she had always provided him with abundant milk, clothes, food, and beautiful fruits and roots. He cursed his life, saying that existence was meaningless for one bereft of his parents.

Brahma said, Hearing these words of her son, Renuka, deeply sorrowful, wiped away her son’s tears and spoke in great agitation. She told him not to grieve and that she would remain near him, then recounted the events that had transpired. She explained that the son of Kritavirya had arrived at the hermitage at midday with his army, and though her husband had honored and fed them, the king had demanded the wish-fulfilling cow. When the sage remained silent, the king took the cow by force in his anger. The moment the cow was touched, she manifested a mighty force, and a battle ensued with the king’s soldiers. The soldiers were defeated and fled, but the king fought on, repeatedly discharging five hundred arrows. Eventually, he was defeated and went home, and the cow ascended to heaven. The wicked king returned and, in his rage, struck her husband in the chest with a single arrow, and then struck her twenty-one times without any offense before departing.

She urged Rama to destroy the wicked one quickly and to rid the earth of the warrior caste twenty-one times, as the king had inflicted twenty-one arrows upon her body. She asked him to fulfill one more request: to perform their funeral rites in a place where no one else had been cremated. She instructed him to summon the great sage Dattatreya, who knew all things, and to perform the rites for thirteen days, promising that only then would they attain the highest state, as there was no one else as capable. Having said this, Renuka abandoned her body and attained the inaccessible abode. The high-minded Rama performed everything exactly as she had instructed.

Chapter 81 : The Story of Rama

Brahma said: Rama, having performed the shaving ritual and bathed properly according to the prescribed rites, performed the funeral rites (Shraddha) for his parents as instructed by the Brahmins. Starting from the place of rest, he offered oblations into the sacred fire for both of them. He then went to the sage Dattatreya. Seeing the sage, who appeared disheveled, accompanied by a disciple, holding a dog, and looking dirty and frail, Rama recognized him through meditation and bowed to the great sage. He stood before him with folded hands for half a watch. Dattatreya, the all-knowing sage, understood his intent and, addressing Rama, said, "I know your purpose, yet I ask you, why have you come here?"

Rama then narrated the entire story clearly from the beginning. He said, "The son of Kritavirya (Kartaviryarjuna) came to my father’s hermitage. He was fed with excellent food and various delicacies. After eating with his army, he demanded the Kamadhenu (wish-fulfilling cow). When my father refused, he tried to take her by force. The cow, being struck, produced many hosts of soldiers. Defeated by them, the king returned to his palace, and the Kamadhenu went to heaven. Enraged, the king struck my father, Jamadagni, in the heart with a powerful arrow and struck my mother twenty-one times with arrows in his fury. I was not there, O Brahmin; I returned later and saw them. Having cremated them with the sacred fire, I have come here following my mother's instructions. Except for Dattatreya, there is no one else to advise me on this matter. After performing all the rites for thirteen days, the king, the son of Kritavirya, must be killed, and the earth must be rid of the Kshatriyas twenty-one times. My mother, Renuka, commanded me thus; therefore, I have come here. Please have mercy on me."

Brahma said: Hearing Rama’s words, the sage, who was a friend of Renuka, felt his heart heavy with grief and spoke to Rama. Dattatreya said, "One who has been fed in a house should not harbor enmity toward the host. If a wicked person does so, he will surely see the consequences from the Lord of Death. For now, perform the funeral rites for your parents properly."

Brahma said: Then, accompanied by Dattatreya, Rama returned to the hermitage and performed the final rites for his parents with devotion. He followed the mantras given by Dattatreya until the second day. When the rites were concluded, the sage intended to go to Kolhapur. Rama asked, "When will you return?" The sage replied, "When you remember me, saying 'Come, Dattatreya,' you will see me, O sinless one."

After performing the rites, Rama went out to beg for food daily, thinking that one should not eat food offered by those in a state of impurity. On the fifth day, after completing the rites, as the great sage was leaving after receiving permission, a tiger appeared. Out of fear, Rama cried out, "Mother, Mother, where shall I go today?" At Rama’s call, his mother Renuka appeared. Because she was called by the Bhargava (Rama) before the twelfth night, she appeared with an incomplete head, though she was filled with maternal love. She said, "O child, tell me why you have called me." She embraced Rama with affection, her breasts flowing with milk. On the sixth day, the sage Dattatreya returned and saw Renuka in that state. He said to Rama, "Why did you call her in the middle? She has come with an incomplete body. If you had called her after the Sapindikarana rite, this Renuka would have come whole and beautiful in all her limbs, O best of Brahmins."

Rama replied, "O Brahmin, due to my childish nature and fear, and by my nature, I called out 'Mother,' and that is how the great sage saw her." On the eleventh day, Rama performed the Vrishotsarga, and on the twelfth, he performed the Sapindikarana for both. On the following day, he performed the Patheya Shraddha, the Punyahavachana, and gave many gifts to the Brahmins as appropriate. Jamadagni, with a divine body, went to Brahmaloka. Renuka remained on earth, appearing in various places in that same form. She fulfills all the desires of those who serve her with devotion. Her greatness is described in detail in the Skanda Purana. Fearing excessive length, it is not described here, O best of sages.

Chapter 82 : The Granting of the Boon to Rama

Vyasa said: How did Rama, while still a boy, defeat the thousand-armed Kartavirya Arjuna, who possessed a vast army, all by himself? Tell me this in detail.

Suta said: One day, Rama asked his mother, "O Mother, tell me the means by which I can defeat him whom even Indra and the other gods fear, and who possesses an infinite army consisting of four divisions. Tell me how I can rid the earth of the Kshatriyas twenty-one times. By your grace, may I achieve victory and may my incomparable fame be renowned throughout all the worlds."

The Mother said: Son, you shall have victory if you worship Shankara. If the Great God is pleased, all your desires will be fulfilled.

Hearing this, Rama went to Kailasa. After bowing to his mother’s feet and receiving her blessings, he saw the Great God seated on a jeweled throne. With folded hands, Rama bowed and praised him.

Rama said: Salutations to you, O Lord of gods, Lord of Gauri, Shambhu. Salutations to the Creator, Sustainer, and Destroyer of the universe. Salutations to the form of the universe, the abode of the universe, and the moon-crested one. Salutations to you, who are beyond qualities, the cause of pure knowledge, formless yet with form, and eternal. Salutations to you, who are beyond the Vedas, Vedanta, and scriptures; you are the manifest and the unmanifest, the essence of truth. Salutations to you, who are the awakener of the three qualities and yet beyond them. Salutations to you, who know the world and are yet free from it.

Suta said: Hearing this hymn, the Great Lord was pleased. He addressed Rama, saying, "I am satisfied with your nectar-like words. Ask for a boon that you desire in your heart. I know you, O son of Jamadagni, O Brahmana."

Rama said: The wicked Kartavirya, desiring the wish-fulfilling cow, killed my father Jamadagni in anger without any fault. My mother, Renuka, was struck by arrows. My mother has commanded me to kill that wicked king, who is accompanied by an army of twenty-one thousand. I have taken refuge in you; tell me the means for his destruction. By your grace, I shall rid the earth of the Kshatriyas twenty-one times.

Suta said: Knowing the truth, the Great God meditated and revealed to Rama the means of victory. He taught Rama the six-syllable mantra that pleases the elephant-faced Lord Ganesha. He instructed him to chant it with effort, perform a homa (fire sacrifice) with one-tenth of the count, and similarly perform tarpana (libations) and feed the Brahmanas with one-tenth of the count. "If you do this with great devotion, the elephant-faced Lord of gods will be pleased and will accomplish all your tasks."

Hearing this, Rama bowed to Shiva with respect. Taking his leave, Rama wandered until he found an excellent place north of the Krishna River. It was beautiful with various trees and creepers and was a place of great spiritual power. There, he performed the rituals as instructed by Shambhu. Standing on one toe, the Brahmana controlled his senses and mind, focused on the elephant-faced Lord, and chanted the great mantra. He performed the homa, tarpana, and feeding of the Brahmanas in the prescribed proportions.

Then, the Lord Ganesha appeared, pleased. He was four-armed, large-bodied, full of great illusion, and very beautiful. He wore a serpent as a sacred thread and was adorned with various ornaments. He wore a crown and earrings, and his face shone with beautiful cheeks. His chest was radiant with garlands of pearls and coral. He held a battle-axe, a lotus, a tusk, and sweets in his hands. He held a lotus in his trunk, twirling it at will, and his radiance illuminated all directions.

Rama saw him and suddenly closed his eyes, blinded by his brilliance. Then the Brahmana praised him.

Rama said: Salutations to you, O Lord of the world, who shines like a thousand suns. Salutations to the Lord of all knowledge, the giver of all success. Salutations to you, the Lord of obstacles, the remover of obstacles. Salutations to you, the indweller of all, the beloved of all. Salutations to the God who loves his devotees, the embodiment of knowledge. Salutations to the Creator and Sustainer of the universe. Please remove the great obstacle that hinders my penance.

Suta said: Hearing this praise, the gentle-radiant Ganesha spoke to Rama, who was overwhelmed by his intense brilliance.

Ganesha said: O Rama, I have come to grant you the boon for which you have been chanting the six-syllable mantra day and night. Ask for whatever you desire in your heart. I am the Creator, Sustainer, and Destroyer of countless universes. Even Brahma and the sages do not know my true form. I have come to grant you my vision.

Rama said: You are the immeasurable foundation of all, the cause of creation and destruction. You are not visible to people through the Vedas, penance, sacrifices, vows, charity, or yoga. Yet, by your grace, O Lord of obstacles, I have seen you. What else should I ask for? Grant me firm devotion to you.

Ganesha said: Your devotion to me shall be firm, O best of Brahmanas. Even when tempted by boons, your intellect did not waver. Take this battle-axe of mine, which will destroy all your enemies. Your name shall be famous in the three worlds as Parashurama.

Suta said: Having granted him the boon and the battle-axe, the elephant-faced Lord disappeared while everyone watched. Rama then installed the Great Ganesha with the help of Brahmanas learned in the Vedas and scriptures. He fed the Brahmanas and gave many gifts. He built a sturdy temple with jeweled pillars. After worshipping, circumambulating, and bowing to Ganesha, Rama returned to his abode with a pleased mind.

Then, Rama loudly challenged the king Kartavirya. In battle, he severed the thousand arms of the king. He rid the earth of the Kshatriyas twenty-one times. After performing a sacrifice with generous offerings, he gave the earth to the Brahmanas. Recognizing him as Lord Vishnu, the people worshipped him. Seeing his prowess, which surpassed all the gods, the people were amazed.

Thus, O Brahmana, I have described to you in brief the glory of the elephant-faced God. It is not possible for anyone to describe it in its entirety. Whoever on earth listens to this section on worship will attain all their desires and reach the abode of Ganesha, where they will rejoice until the end of time.

Chapter 83 : The Origin of Tarakasura

The Sage said: In which place did Rama perform the most wonderful penance? Tell me that, O Lord of the worlds, for I am not satisfied with hearing it.

K said: The region known in all four yugas by the name Mayureshwar is where the Lord of gods descended, riding upon a peacock. Because he mounted a peacock to defeat the great and arrogant demon named Kamalasura, he is clearly known as Mayureshwar. Praised by gods and sages, he attained great fame in the world. There, he performed his rituals and subsequently obtained the axe. He himself became known in the heavens as Parashurama. I will tell you the history of this, O best of sages, listen.

There was a demon named Taraka who possessed great strength and valor. He performed severe penance for a thousand divine years. Pleased by this, Brahma granted him fearlessness from all beings—gods, sages, Yakshas, Gandharvas, serpents, and demons. He was told that death would not come to him from any weapon, but that when Skanda (Kartikeya) was born, his death would occur. Hearing this, and filled with the pride of his strength, Taraka oppressed the worlds. He imprisoned the Brahmins who were devoted to Vedic study, penance, and the Agnihotra fire. Having brought all kings and Nagas under his control, he went to heaven.

Indra and the other gods then fled to the caves of the Himalayas. Because of the fear of Taraka, no sacrifices or worship could take place anywhere. Taraka declared, "I alone am the Lord, the god, and the family deity of the Brahmins. I alone am to be worshipped and bowed to; there is no other in this world. Whoever bows to or worships another shall be punished or sent to the abode of Yama." He had this announced by his messengers in all the worlds. Consequently, all people became devoid of virtue, lacking in study, sacrifice, and charity. Their family duties were destroyed, and they became wicked, devoid of good conduct. The sages and saints took refuge in mountain caves.

They prayed to the Lord, asking how the demon had grown so powerful. "O Shambhu, without you, to whom shall we go for refuge, O Lord of the world? You are the Creator, the Protector, the Sustainer, and the Destroyer of the worlds. He burns us with his arrogance like a forest fire. If you wish to destroy, then destroy the world yourself; otherwise, destroy the demon Taraka who torments everyone." Having prayed thus, they performed difficult and excellent penance, some eating leaves, some air, some fasting, and some living only on water.

While the sages were thus engaged, the demon king, having occupied the throne of Indra, struck Brahma. Vishnu then went to the ocean of milk to sleep, and Shiva left Kailasa for a mountain cave. The guardians of the directions and the elephants of the quarters also took refuge in various caves. In their places, the demon king installed other demons. He ruled the subjects, and his roar caused the heavens to tremble.

Then, Indra and the other gods, hiding in a mountain cave, praised Girisha, the Lord of Girija, with deep and joyful voices.

The gods said: O Lord, you are the earth, the sky, and the moon; you are the wind, the fire, the sacrificer, and the water. You create the moving and the stationary, and you destroy all by your own will. It is not appropriate for you, the remover of others' sorrows, to let your own glory be eclipsed. Therefore, destroy him, or save the sages and the gods who are devoted to you. To whom shall we go for refuge, O Girisha, if not you? Whom shall we worship, O Maheshwara? To whom shall we speak, O destroyer of distress, O Lord of Parvati? Who else is capable of protecting us, O Lord of all?

K said: As they were praising him, they heard a voice from the sky saying, "When the son of Hara is born, his destruction will come; make efforts toward that." Hearing this voice, all the gods were filled with joy. They went to Kailasa, the abode of the gods. Not seeing Shankara there, the gods saw Uma, the form of the primordial nature, before them. They prayed to her, "O Mother, you who give the knowledge of the savior, save us from Taraka. O Mother, we bow to you, the mother of the gods, the protector of the three worlds, the one praised by Brahma and others. You are the one whom the Vedas cannot fully describe; bring welfare to the world, O beloved of the Lord. You have taken a form by your own will; destroy the demon, O sinless one."

K said: Thus prayed to, the Mother of the world said to them, "I have heard the voice from the sky; Shankara will bring welfare. Let all of you come with me to where Shankara is performing supreme penance, observing strict rules." Saying this, she assumed the form of a huntress (Bhilli), seeing whom even the greatest yogi would be struck by the arrows of desire. Even some of the gods became agitated by passion. Urvashi, Menaka, Rambha, Purvachitti, and Rati were ashamed to see her, as she was beautiful in every limb. The gods and Girija reached the presence of Shiva. They saw the Lord, motionless as a pillar, his eyes fixed in meditation, contemplating the Brahman in his mind, chanting, and free from all attachments.

Then, the Mother of the world suggested a pleasant plan to all the gods. "Shiva is sitting beyond the body, devoted to penance. Make the God of Love (Kama) manifest his desire for the sake of the body. When Shiva, who is focused on one, is struck by his arrow, he will return to his bodily state, and the task will be accomplished." Then all the gods remembered the powerful Kama. They told him their purpose. They prayed to him, "You are the lord of all beings, moving and stationary. Creation happens through you, and this world is pervaded by you. Even the weak become strong through you. Without you, the world is in vain. Therefore, you must accomplish this urgent task for everyone."

Kama said: "Although I am but a spark of fire, through your grace, I will perform this task until the fall of my body. With a bow of flowers, a string of bees, and arrows of glances, I will conquer all, even Shankara." Having obtained the support of Spring, all the gods watched.

K said: Having said this, Kama went to where the eternal Shiva was, to enchant the Lord for the success of the gods' mission.

Thus ends the eighty-third chapter of the Upasana Khanda of the Shri Ganesha Purana, titled "The Tale of Taraka."

Chapter 84 : The Burning of Kama

Kama said: Having spoken thus, Kama departed to accomplish the task of the gods. He saw the abode of Shankara, which was filled with trees and creepers. It was inhabited by lions and tigers, and abounded in birds and wild animals. At that very moment, he created a magical garden. There were numerous lakes with nectar-like water, and trees with many flowers that spread fragrance for miles. There were jambu, mango, and jujube trees laden with ripe fruits, as well as plantain, jackfruit, coconut, and date palms. There were also cardamom, clove, and black pepper trees, along with many others. The fragrance of these trees reached the nostrils of Hara. At dawn, Hara saw the wonderful garden of Kama, which was enchanting with its moonlight-like luster and filled with various fruits and flowers.

At that moment, it disturbed the mind of the trident-wielder. Shiva mentally cursed his own Ashoka grove. Becoming conscious of his physical form, the Lord pondered the cause, wondering who had created this beautiful forest and caused an obstacle to his penance. He wondered which wicked person, whose life was nearing its end, had suddenly created this. Furrowing his brows in anger, his eyes became red. Kama, terrified, hid himself and could not be found. He remembered Indra and the other gods, who, upon being remembered, arrived there. The gods, desiring the success of their mission, arrived mounted on their celestial chariots. Just then, Shiva saw the very small and slender Kama. To burn the mind-born one, he opened his third eye. The entire earth, heaven, and the netherworld trembled.

The gods cried out, "Do not kill him!" but before they could finish, the fire from his eye reduced Kama to ashes, leaving only a heap of dust. Then, the Bhilli (Parvati in her tribal form) respectfully prayed to Maheshana, bowing with folded hands for the welfare of the three worlds. She said, "O Shankara, withdraw this fire that is burning the three worlds. The demon Taraka, who is very powerful, has obtained a boon from Brahma. He has conquered the three worlds, which are now devoid of Vedic study and sacrificial offerings. All the gods, having been displaced from their abodes, saw you engaged in penance. They hurriedly invited Kama to induce a physical desire in you, O sinless one. He has been reduced to ashes due to this great offense. Now, O Lord, protect us who have come to you for refuge. You are renowned in the three worlds as the protector of those who seek shelter. O Mahadeva, O compassionate Shankara, forgive the offense of these helpless gods who seek your protection."

Kama said: Hearing these words from her, as she lay with her head at his feet, O Sage, Shankara, with a smiling face, withdrew the fire and said, "Rise, rise! Today, I have accomplished the protection of the gods because of your words and your devotion as you fell at my feet." Then, he suddenly embraced the Bhilli, took her onto his lap, and mounting his bull, returned to Kailasa along with her.

Thus ends the eighty-fourth chapter of the Upasana Khanda in the Shri Ganesha Purana, titled "The Burning of Kama."

Chapter 85 : The Story of Skanda

The Lord said: Scorched by the fire of desire due to Shiva’s embrace, the Goddess, like a fish out of water, found no comfort anywhere. Even when she fell into the water, which was cool and fragrant with ushira, she found no relief, nor could she sleep on land. Camphor and sandalwood only increased her heat; no cooling substance brought her any satisfaction. After a long time had passed in this state, she was reduced to mere skin and bones. Then, approaching Girisha (Shiva), Parvati spoke: "O Lord, do you not perceive the state I have reached? Though Kama (the God of Love) was burnt, he still torments me greatly. I have tried many remedies for his pacification, but none have worked. O Lord, please do whatever is necessary to bring me peace."

Hearing these words from his beloved, Hara, who always fulfills her desires, took her hand in private and led her to the bed. Overcome by desire, he enjoyed her as he wished. Even though Kama was dead, he accomplished a great task urged by the gods. Shiva earned the fame of being the greatest archer, second to none. Sixty thousand years passed while they played together.

The sages and gods, having lost their positions and hearing of Kama’s success, returned to Kailasa and found Hara still absorbed in play. Realizing this, they stood silently, their minds troubled by anxiety. Terrified of the demon Taraka, they retreated to a cave. They wondered when the demon would be killed, when they would return to their homes, and when Shambhu would end their suffering. While the gods, led by Brahma, were drowned in this ocean of anxiety, the preceptor of the gods (Brihaspati) said: "Listen to me, O sinless ones. Send Agni to Hara, disguised in another form. He will quickly awaken Hara, and your task will be accomplished."

Then, summoning Agni, they praised him with various hymns. The gods said: "O Brahman, all sacrificial rites and rituals originate from you. You are the cause of water and the mouth of the gods. You are the initiator of the Agnihotra and are known by names like Garhapatya. You daily consume the vast waters of the ocean, and you digest the six tastes in the stomachs of all beings. You act in every union of all creatures; that which is abandoned by you receives the name of a corpse and is consumed by you. You are the cause of sustaining the life of all beings, O Lord of gods. Without you and water, nothing can be cooked anywhere. You are Brahma, Rudra, and the Sun, assuming many forms. You are the root of anger, O Lord of the world. Wherever there is brilliance, that form is yours alone. Therefore, we pray to you today, O benefactor of the three worlds. The demon Taraka has conquered the three worlds. You know the divine voice and the fate of Kama, as well as the state of Parvati and Hara, who are immersed in long-lasting play. Go there in another form and beg for alms. By doing this, the work of the world and our own work will be accomplished."

Brahma said: Hearing the words of the gods, Agni assumed the form of a Brahmana wearing saffron robes and went to where Parvati and Shiva were. Standing outside while they were absorbed in play, he called out, "Give alms!" three times in a loud voice. Both heard the sound. Astonished, they looked at each other and asked, "Who has come here?" They wondered what should be given to him. Parvati, unable to hold the seed of Shiva in her womb, and knowing the future, gave it to the mendicant. If it were dropped on the earth, it would burn the three worlds, both moving and stationary. Fearing their curse, Agni drank the seed of Hara. Agni became pregnant, feeling both ashamed and scorched.

Wherever Agni went, he found no peace. At dawn, when the sun was in the sign of Libra, he went to the Ganga to bathe. While he was performing his ablutions, six wives of sages arrived there to bathe in the month of Kartika. Agni thought, "This Ganga, the beloved of Hara, is cool and water-like; if she is capable, she will hold what I have released." He deposited the seed, and the six women consumed it in six parts. At that very moment, Agni disappeared. The heat vanished as Agni went to a distant land. The women put on their clothes and returned home. Their husbands noticed their faces were exceptionally radiant. Realizing through their divine vision that they were pregnant, the sages cast them out of their homes, saying their faces should not be seen. They returned to the banks of the Ganga, which were adorned with reeds, and released their wombs. Having bathed and become pure, they returned home.

After they had departed, having released their wombs, a child with six faces and twelve arms was born there. By his mere roar, the mountains fell to the earth, the entire world trembled, and even Shesha in the netherworld shook. Trees were uprooted, and the sun was covered by mist. Meanwhile, Narada, of divine vision, was on his way to Kailasa to see Girisha and saw the child on the path. He was extremely brilliant, powerful, and difficult to behold. Realizing who he was through meditation, he silently arrived at Kailasa. He told Parvati and Shiva the entire story. The whole earth rejoiced upon learning that he was the son of Girisha. Divine drums sounded, and the Gandharvas sang wonderfully.

Narada said: "O Gauri, while coming here, I saw your son on the path. He has six faces, twelve arms, and the radiance of ten million suns. He has fallen on the banks of the Ganga. Have you abandoned him? He is endowed with the beauty of ten million Cupids, and his roar has agitated everything. O Gauri, how could you be so cruel to such a beautiful child?"

The Lord said: Having said this, as Narada disappeared, Gauri went toward the child.

Chapter 86 : Shankara Narrates the Ganesha Vrata to Skanda

Seeing the child, the virtuous Goddess, her breasts dripping with affection, embraced him with joy, holding him in her arms. Ganga, with her deep resonant voice, said, "This is my child." Agni said to the daughter of the mountain, "He is mine." The Krittikas also cried out in distress, saying, "This child was born from us; he is ours alone." Arguing in this manner, they, led by Agni, went to Kailasa, the abode of the gods, to see Girisha, the moon-crested Lord.

Gauri entered first, carrying the child on her hip. Shiva took him onto his lap, smelled his head, and with great joy and divine mantras, delighted the child. Ganga, Agni, and the six women then returned to their respective abodes. Shiva summoned Brahma and Brihaspati to name the child and asked them to perform the naming ceremony.

Brahma and Brihaspati said, "Since he was born in the month of Kartika, his first name is clearly Kartikeya. He is also the joy of Parvati. Because he was born in the reed forest, he is Sharajanma. Because he was born from the Krittikas, he is remembered as Kartikeya. Since he has six mothers, he is also known as Shanmatura. This son is the conqueror of Taraka and will surely defeat him. He will be the commander of the divine army, hence he is called Senani. Because of his great army, he is Mahasena, and because he has six faces, he is Sadanana. Since the seed was spilled three times, he is called Skanda."

While they were speaking, Indra and the other gods arrived. They rejoiced, bowed to him, worshipped him, praised him, and sang. The sound of divine instruments filled the earth and the netherworlds. Then, bowing to the commander, they prayed, "O Lord, destroy Taraka, the thorn of the three worlds." They anointed him as the commander-in-chief with various offerings, using Vedic and Tantric mantras recited by many sages. Having received his permission, the gods returned to their places, and the sages, freed from anxiety, performed their penance as before. They thought, "With the commander present, we have no fear anywhere."

The child grew rapidly, like the moon in the waxing fortnight. Once, out of childish playfulness, he flew up to catch the moon, but Brahma stopped him, saying, "Do not commit such a reckless act." By his intellect, he surpassed Brihaspati, and by his strength, he surpassed Indra. Once, seeing Shiva sitting comfortably with Parvati, he bowed and asked for the fulfillment of all his desires.

Skanda said, "Father, I have heard many auspicious and great stories from you. Tell me of a vow that grants all accomplishments, increases the wealth of sons, destroys all sins, and grants righteousness, prosperity, pleasure, and liberation. Although I am satisfied with you, the teacher of the three worlds, tell me of an auspicious vow that grants victory over all enemies."

Ishwara said, "You have asked well, Skanda. I will tell you of a vow that is beneficial to all people and grants all accomplishments to men. It destroys all sins, grants the four goals of life, destroys all enemies, increases the wealth of sons, removes the distress of poverty, and pleases the Lord of the Ganas. A person who performs it with devotion is worshipped even by the gods. He becomes free to act according to his will and becomes a creator, sustainer, and destroyer. By merely seeing such a person, the great sins of others are dissolved. There is no vow equal to the Skanda Varada Chaturthi vow."

Hearing these words spoken by Shankara, the commander again asked his father about the glory of the vow. Skanda said, "O Hara, if you are pleased, tell me in detail about the glory of this vow. In which month and on which day should it be started? What is the procedure, what is the fruit, and who has experienced its efficacy? Tell me all this for the benefit of all people and the fame of this vow."

Thus ends the eighty-sixth chapter of the Skandopakhyana in the Upasana Khanda of the Sri Ganesha Purana, titled "The Dialogue between Shiva and Skanda."

Chapter 87 : The Slaying of Taraka

Shankara said: I shall now tell you the supreme procedure for this vow. One should begin this vow on the fourth day of the bright fortnight of the month of Shravana. After performing a morning bath with a paste of sesame and amlaki, and having completed all daily obligatory rituals, one should remain free from anger. In a pure place, one should construct a pavilion decorated with banana stalks, sugarcane, fly-whisks, and flowers, and adorned with rows of mirrors. In the center, place a water pot covered with a pair of cloths, and upon it, draw an eight-petaled lotus with sandalwood paste. Having obtained the guru's permission and sanctified the ritual items, one should worship the Lord of the Ganas (Ganesha) with the sixteen offerings.

According to one's capacity, one should prepare an image of gold or silver. One should offer twenty-one types of cooked food, twenty-one in number, to the elephant-faced deity. Offer twenty-one coins as dakshina (offering), whether gold or silver, without any stinginess. Offer twenty-one blades of durva grass, either white or green, to the Lord of Gods, followed by the offering of mantra-flowers. Worship twenty-one Brahmins who are learned in the Vedas, feed them with similar food, and give them gifts. Then, bow down, seek forgiveness, and recite the "achidra" (completeness) mantras.

The procedure for the earthen Ganesha has been described previously; the entire method of worship is the same here. One should listen to the story along with relatives, meditate on Ganesha while eating, and one may observe silence or fast. Perform this monthly vow until the fourth day of the bright fortnight of Bhadrapada. On that day, a great festival should be celebrated with devotion according to one's means. Worship the Lord of Ganas according to the aforementioned procedure. At night, keep a vigil with songs, musical instruments, the recitation of Puranas, and various excellent stories. Praise the elephant-faced deity with the thousand-name mantra. In the morning, bathe purely and worship Him again.

Feed one hundred and twenty-one Brahmins with devotion. Give them cows, land, gold, clothes, ornaments, and wealth according to your capacity; if you are unable, feed at least twenty-one Brahmins. If the image is made of metal, it should be given to a Brahmin; otherwise, it should be immersed in water with great enthusiasm, accompanied by divine musical instruments, carried on a chariot, with umbrellas, flags, banners, and the sounds of Vedic chants. After the children have performed mock battles, immerse the image and return home.

O Skanda, whoever performs this vow even once will attain all desires and reach the abode of Ganesha. Brahma performed this while chanting the single-syllable mantra for the sake of creation, and the Lord appeared before him, granting him various powers. Vishnu, while performing the task of preservation, observed this vow and chanted the wonderful six-syllable mantra, thereby attaining the power to incarnate at will. I (Shiva) also performed this, my son, by chanting the eight-syllable mantra. Indra and Manmatha, desiring to slay the demons Vritra and Shambara, performed this vow and were able to defeat them. I possess various powers and can destroy the three worlds. Yakshas, Gandharvas, sages, Kinnaras, serpents, demons, Siddhas, Charanas, and humans have all performed this for the fulfillment of their desires. Therefore, O Skanda, perform this Chaturthi vow; you will become invincible in battle and famous in the three worlds. I give you the six-syllable mantra of the Giver of Boons.

Brahma said: At an auspicious moment, Shiva gave him the six-syllable initiation. O best of Brahmins, Skanda immediately went to a very pure place, filled with trees and creepers, abounding in fruits and flowers, and adorned with lakes and ponds, to perform penance. Standing on one leg, Skanda performed severe penance. He performed the vow according to the procedure told by Shambhu. Due to the power of the mantra and the vow, the Supreme Lord Gajanana became pleased and revealed His supreme form, which is meditated upon by yogis, to the commander of the army.

The six-faced Skanda saw the Lord of great brilliance, adorned with four arms, a magnificent crown, earrings, and armlets. He had one tusk, the moon on his forehead, a trunk, and held a noose, a goad, a garland, and a tusk in his hands. He was adorned with pearls and gems, had a serpent as a belt, wore divine garments, and was anointed with divine fragrances. He shone like many suns, illuminated by flames of brilliance. Skanda’s eyes blossomed with wonder, and his mind became agitated, thinking, "What is this? I cannot comprehend what I am seeing." He then asked silently, "Who are you, and what is your name?"

Gajanana said: I am He whom you have been thinking of day and night with a focused mind. I have come to grant you a boon; tell me what is in your heart. I, the elephant-faced one, am pleased with your penance.

Skanda said: Neither the gods, nor the authors of the scriptures, nor Brahma, nor the serpents headed by Shesha truly know your form. O Lord of the world, O Elephant-faced one, I have seen you today. By this, my desires are fulfilled. Yet, according to your divine words, I ask: may I never face defeat, and may you remain manifest to me whenever I think of you. May I never forget your lotus feet, and may I be the foremost among all gods. Since you have become visible, be known here by the name "Laksha-Vinayaka" (The Ganesha of a Lakh), and be the wish-fulfilling tree for your devotees.

The Lord said: Everything you have requested today will come to pass, O Skanda. You will never forget me, I will be present when you think of me, your enemies will be defeated, and you will be the foremost among gods.

Brahma said: Having granted these boons, the Lord, being pleased, gave Skanda his own vehicle, the peacock, due to his humility and the power of his penance. From then on, he became known as Mayuradhvaja (the one with the peacock banner). Gajanana said: The great demons headed by Taraka will meet their death at your hands. Because of your request, O sinless one, I will remain here for a long time by the name Laksha-Vinayaka, being affectionate to my devotees.

Brahma said: Having said this, the Lord disappeared. Skanda then had a great image made, installed it with the help of Brahmins, and gave it the auspicious name "Laksha-Vinayaka." He worshipped that image with a lakh of modakas, flowers, and durva grass, along with other items, and fed a lakh of Brahmins. After praising and bowing to the Lord, Skanda went to Shiva, the benefactor of the world, mounted his peacock, and told him everything. He mentioned the name "Mayuradhvaja" given by the Lord. By the command of Shiva, Skanda went to slay the demon Taraka. Remembering Gajanana and receiving Shiva's blessings, he was anointed as the commander of the divine army by the sages. The mighty commander, seeing Taraka, waged war and, after a lakh of years, killed him with his power. Even Shesha would not have the power to describe that battle. When the demon Taraka was slain, all the gods rejoiced. Sages, guardians of the world, serpents, and humans showered flowers upon Skanda in delight. All the gods and worlds returned to their respective abodes, and the rituals of Svaha, Svadha, and Vashatkara were performed as before.

Brahma said: Thus, the power of this deity and the efficacy of the vow have been described to you. That great demon, who was invincible to the thirty-three crore gods, was slain by Skanda in battle due to the power of this vow. He became worthy of worship by Indra and the other gods.

Vyasa asked: O Prajapati, tell me in which place he performed this penance with concentration.

Brahma said: He performed the penance where the Ghushneshvara (Jyotirlinga) resides. It is famous by the name Laksha-Vinayaka. Later, a king named Ela became famous in that city, and the city became known by his name, O sage.

Chapter 88 : The Request for Boons by Kama

The Sage said: O Brahman, I have heard the story concerning the vow of Gajanana. If Kama was burnt by the fire of Shiva’s anger, how is it that he is still seen in all the worlds? O Four-faced One (Brahma), please explain this to me in detail.

Brahma said: When Shiva opened his third eye in anger and realized the offense committed by Kama, Rati, seeing her husband dead, approached Shiva. Prostrating herself with her whole body, she praised him according to her understanding.

Rati said: I bow to the Lord, the companion of Girija, the one with the bull-emblem, and the one with an eye on his forehead. You protect the worlds with the quality of goodness (Sattva) and create them with the quality of passion (Rajas). You, the Lord of all, dissolve the world at will, assuming the form of darkness (Tamas). You, who carry the skull of beings and live on alms, fulfill all desires. O compassionate Lord Maheshwara, be a refuge for me, who has lost her beloved. You are capable of doing, undoing, and doing otherwise. May you grant me the good fortune of my husband’s life, as I have sought your refuge. If not, I shall end my own life to uphold my honor, O Lord.

Brahma said: Thus praised by her, Shambhu was pleased and said to her: O fortunate one, wife of Kama, O beautiful-faced one, choose a boon. Pleased by your hymn, I grant you all the desires in your heart. Hearing the words of Shiva, Rati was delighted and bowed to him. Desiring good fortune and being deeply distressed, she said to the Lord:

Rati said: O Lord, if you are pleased, listen to my supreme request. Whether on earth, in heaven, or in the netherworld, women possess many charming qualities, but not even a fraction of my beauty exists in them. Seeing me, even Indra and others have lost their composure and dignity. I feel great shame because of this; please remove it. O Shankara, beauty is useless without Kama (desire). The lack of my husband burns me, and I am like a widow. O Ocean of Mercy, restore my husband to life.

Thus requested by her, Shankara, who brings happiness to the world, spoke with gentle words, gladdening the wife of Kama.

Shiva said: Do not worry, O auspicious one; you should not feel ashamed. O girl, Kama will be visible to you as soon as you remember him. Even when thought of in the mind, he will be known as Manobhava (born of the mind). He will fulfill your desires, and you will be honored. When he takes birth from Vishnu in the womb of Rama (Lakshmi), your husband will be known in the world by the name Pradyumna. O fortunate one, go now to your home.

She went to her beautiful palace as per Shiva’s instruction. She remembered her husband, and he appeared before her. By the will of the Lord, he became manifest to her. Filled with wonder, she rejoiced with her husband.

Then, Kama went to Shambhu, bowed, and said: O Lord of gods, why was I made bodiless (Ananga) without having committed an offense? The gods, led by Indra, were troubled by Tarakasura, and the sages, knowing of your union with the daughter of the mountain, requested me to break your meditation for the benefit of all. I performed that act for the sake of others. There is no merit equal to helping others in the three worlds, yet by my fate, the result was the opposite. I was the most beautiful among the thirty-three crore gods, and I was the standard of beauty for all. How can I exist, O Lord, without my body, like a ghost? Therefore, O Mahadeva, please show me your grace.

Brahma said: Then Shiva gave the one-syllable mantra of Ganesha to Kama and instructed him on how to perform the ritual. Kama went to a beautiful place that grants success and performed the penance as ordered by Shankara. For one hundred years, he performed great penance, chanting the one-syllable mantra and meditating on Ganesha, living only on air, accompanied by Rati.

Then, the Lord of gods, Gajanana, became pleased and appeared. He had ten arms, was adorned with a great crown, and was decorated with glowing jewels, earrings, and armlets. He shone like a crore of suns, adorned with pearl necklaces, divine garlands, and garments, and anointed with divine fragrances. His face was like the red of vermilion, his ten hands held various weapons, his navel was adorned by Shesha, and he was resplendent with many ornaments. His shoulders were like those of a lion, and his roar terrified all.

When Gajanana appeared, Indra and other gods, along with Apsaras, Yakshas, Gandharvas, and Kinnaras, arrived. With the sound of divine instruments, they worshipped Gajanana with sixteen offerings. Kama also rose, bowed to all the gods, and then bowed to the feet of the Lord. He praised the formidable and compassionate Lord.

Kama said: You are blessed among the gods, the embodiment of the Supreme Brahman. Though formless, you have assumed a form for the sake of your devotees. Today my birth is blessed, and my penance is even more blessed, for I have seen your pair of feet, which liberate one from all sorrows. You are the cause of all success, the giver of Dharma, wealth, desire, and liberation. My eyes are blessed today for having seen the Supreme Being. The Vedas, the Sankhya, and the followers of Patanjali do not know you; even the Vedas, saying "Neti, Neti" (not this, not this), fall silent before you. You are the Lord of all, in whose pores exist infinite crores of universes. Blessed is the mantra through which you were seen.

Ganesha said: You have spoken well, O Lord of Rati. Brahma and others do not know me, but when I assume a form, they know me. It is by my grace that you have seen me, as I am pleased with your penance and the mantra. Choose whatever desires you have, O Kama; I grant them to you.

Brahma said: Hearing the words of the Lord, the mind-born one (Kama) spoke again. He told him the entire story—how he was made bodiless by Shiva, his wife's lamentation, the mantra, his long penance, and the boon given by Shiva. He then requested the following boons from the pleased Gajanana:

Kama said: If you are pleased, O Lord, grant me my body back. May I be honored among all the gods, may I regain my former beauty, may I have firm devotion to your feet, and may I be victorious in the three worlds.

Chapter 89 : The Story of Shesha

Ganesha said: Whatever you have prayed for, Kama, shall all be yours. Born from the womb of Rati, you shall be complete and beautiful in every limb. You will be honored by all and victorious in the three worlds. Flowers, fruits, tender leaves, the beauty of women, the breeze, moonlight, sandalwood, lotus, and the sound of swans shall be your stimulants; with these, you shall conquer even Hara and others. By merely seeing or remembering these, you shall exist in the minds of others. Thus, your name shall be Manobhu (born of the mind) and Smritibhu (born of memory) among people. You shall have unwavering devotion and constant remembrance of my feet. Whenever you are in a great crisis, I shall appear before you if you remember me.

Brahma said: Having granted these boons to Kama, the elephant-headed Lord disappeared while the gods and sages watched. Kama then created a magnificent image of Ganesha, installed it, and worshipped it. He offered delicacies prepared by Rati, such as modakas and laddus, and named the deity Mahotkata due to His brilliance. He built a beautiful palace adorned with jewel-studded pillars.

Born from the womb of Rukmini, he was abandoned by a demon into the ocean. He was swallowed by a fish, which was later caught by fishermen and given to Shambara, who presented it to Mayavati. When the fish was cut open, he emerged and was raised by her. Narada told her that this was Kama, whom she had raised. Instructed in many illusions, he eventually killed Shambara. By the grace of Ganesha, he conquered many. Taught by Mayavati in various arts of illusion, he became known as Pradyumna and went to the city of Saraswati. Honored by all gods and a conqueror of the three worlds, he became blissful through the grace of Ganesha. When seen by Rukmini and other women, he appeared like another Krishna. They rose and approached him, feeling shy. Realizing he was her son through Narada’s words, they rejoiced and embraced him, and Rati also bowed to them. The entire city was filled with joy upon knowing this.

Kavi said: O great sage, thus the glory of Ganesha in Janasthana, where Rama cut off Shurpanakha’s nose—which is why the place is known as Nashik—was told. Even today, stones resembling modakas are seen there. Thus, Kama worshipped Ganesha with the six-syllable mantra, just as Shesha had worshipped Him. Both Rati and Madana rejoiced.

Vyasa said: O Brahma, how did Shesha worship Ganesha? For what purpose, and what did he obtain from the pleased Ganesha? Tell me this in detail, O four-faced one, for the merit of the questioner, the listener, and the speaker increases.

Brahma said: You have asked well, O Brahmin. I shall tell you the nectar-like story; listen attentively, O son of Satyavati. Once, O sage, Shiva was sitting happily with Parvati on a beautiful mountain peak, adorned with various trees and creepers, echoing with the sound of waterfalls, where bees hummed in golden lotuses. The breeze carrying the scent of Champaka, Ashoka, Bakula, and Malati flowers delighted the hearts of those residing on its summit. Gandharvas, Apsaras, Yakshas, Kinnaras, gods, sages, and Nagas came there to see the Lord of Girija. Some bowed with full prostration, Gandharvas sang loudly, and Apsaras danced. They worshipped the ten-armed Hara, who wore a tiger skin, was surrounded by Nandi and the Bhringi hosts, had the moon on his forehead, held a trident, had his body smeared with ash, bore Shesha on his head, and had the bull as his emblem. Others worshipped the Lord with mental offerings. Some remained in meditation with closed eyes. Sages like Vashistha, Vamadeva, Jamadagni, Trita, Atri, Kanva, Bharadwaja, and Gautama praised the Lord of Parvati with various hymns.

Among those sages, Shesha became filled with immense pride. He thought, "I am the greatest in the three worlds; there is no other. Since Shiva is the greatest, I reside on His head. The power to support the earth lies in me, nowhere else. Through Vasuki, the gods obtained nectar, and thus immortality; therefore, there is none greater than me." Knowing this pride in his heart, the all-seeing Shiva suddenly stood up. He shook the earth's surface, and Shesha, being so arrogant, had each of his heads split into ten. He fainted and remained lifeless for half a prahara. From then on, he became adorned with a thousand hoods. Shesha, the ornament of the Lord of the three worlds, lamented, "I who was an ornament am now in this state. I do not know by what karma this happened. I cannot move, like a bird without wings. What shall I do? Where shall I go? Who will be my savior now? Who will tell me a virtuous way to regain my position? Who will remove my suffering?" While he was thus lost in thought, he saw the sage Narada passing by that path. The king of serpents rejoiced slightly, as if a beggar had found a dream. Narada saw his miserable state—motionless, breathless, and deep in meditation. Though knowing all things, Narada asked Shesha.

Chapter 90 : Kama's Rebirth and the Story of Shesha

Narada said: Why have you become so lusterless and deeply sorrowful? How did your head get broken, and whose displeasure did you incur, O Sage? Or were you agitated by Lord Shiva, or did you act out of pride? Tell me the reason, O Shesha, so that I may suggest a remedy. Who else but you can support the earth along with all its moving and non-moving beings? Before he could speak, the sage himself suggested a remedy to the King of Serpents to reach his own abode.

Narada said: Listen to my words, O Shesha, the repository of all arts. By this, even the gods and others will become like your servants, and you will carry the earth on your head like a flower garland, O child.

Shesha said: If I have any past merit, then your appearance before me is surely not without reason. Otherwise, how could those without merit have your vision? Due to my extremely shattered body, I am unable to support the earth. Tell me the effort, O Sage, by which I may become as I was before.

Narada said: I shall tell you the great mantra of that God today, O King of Serpents, by whose grace Indra and others attained their respective positions. By whose command the creators, preservers, and destroyers of the entire universe act, and by whose grace, O Lord of all, you will regain your former state. Seeing your plight, my heart is deeply moved. Therefore, I give you the six-syllable mantra of Ganesha. By merely practicing this, the Elephant-faced Lord will appear before you and fulfill all the desires you seek.

The narrator said: After instructing Shesha, Narada disappeared. Shesha, having made a firm and auspicious resolve for penance, controlled all his senses and meditated upon Lord Ganesha. He chanted the supreme mantra for a thousand years. At the end of that period, he saw the God of Gods, Ganesha, before him. He appeared mounted on a lion, three-eyed, ten-armed, wearing serpent earrings and armlets, adorned with a pearl necklace, a beautiful crown, and holding a gem, a goad, and a rosary. Followed constantly by groups of gods and sages, with a curved trunk and elephant face, one should meditate upon the single-tusked Lord who assumes a form for the sake of his devotees and grants boons to gods and men.

Thus, Ganesha arrived to give him vision, accompanied by Siddhi and Buddhi, just as the serpent had meditated upon him earlier. He was as brilliant as a thousand suns, his radiance filling the directions. Shesha was overwhelmed by his brilliance and became as if blind. He trembled, filled with fear, his mind agitated and distressed. After a moment, he regained his composure and thought: What is this brilliance that has arrived, resembling the fire of dissolution? If it can burn all worlds, it will surely burn me too. How can something auspicious happen while performing an auspicious act? Or perhaps, as Narada said, I am seeing the Lord of the Ganas.

As he was thus anxious, the Elephant-faced Lord said: Do not fear, O one skilled in logic, I have come as a boon-giver. Ask for what you desire, which you have been meditating upon day and night. I am the creator, preserver, and destroyer of the world, the Lord of all. By my radiance, the moon, fire, sun, and stars shine. Though I am the form of the Supreme Brahman, I have been pleased by your penance. I have appeared to grant you a boon and for the welfare of the world. Ask me for all the boons you desire.

Shesha said: O Lord, I am overwhelmed by your brilliance; I am unable to see or speak. Your grace upon me is complete, O gentle and sinless one.

The narrator said: Being thus prayed to, the ocean of compassion, Ganesha, became as gentle as a crore of moons, O Lord of gods. Then Shesha, having bowed and praised the Lord of all, asked for boons. I bow to the beginningless and endless Lord, the Leader of the Ganas. I bow to the all-pervading Lord, the cause of the causes of the world, the form of all, the Lord of the universe, who is worshipped by the world. I bow to the Elephant-faced Lord, the leader of the Ganas, the Lord of Garuda, the master of qualities, who is beyond qualities. I bow to the Lord of knowledge, the God of gods, the beloved of the gods, who is dear to Siddhi and Buddhi, the giver of all accomplishments, and the provider of worldly enjoyment and liberation. I bow to the Lord who removes all obstacles, the Leader of the Ganas.

The narrator said: Having praised the Lord of gods, the boon-giver, in this way, listen, O Great Sage, to the boons he asked for.

Shesha said: Today my penance, knowledge, father, mother, and birth are blessed. Blessed is my tongue that has started to praise you, and blessed are my body, eyes, and many heads. My lineage, character, and wealth are blessed by the sight of your lotus feet. Grant me uninterrupted devotion to you, O Lord of infinite power. What sorrow should I speak of, O knower of all, O Lord of obstacles? My heads were shattered due to my excessive pride. I was struck to the ground by the great wrath of Maheshwara. By the grace of Narada, I have seen your lotus feet. Now, O Lord, grant me supremacy in the three worlds. Grant me skill in all heads and the strength to support the earth. Grant me an immovable position and constant vision of you. Grant me the proximity of Shankara and devotion to Shiva.

Ganesha said: If your head has become ten-fold, O King of Serpents, then you shall be known as the thousand-headed one, adorned with a thousand hoods, as long as the moon, sun, and stars exist. Your strength to support the earth will be firm. By my grace, you will attain a stable position on the five-headed one, O best of serpents, and you will have my constant proximity. Whatever else you desire, O Shesha, shall all be fulfilled.

The narrator said: Having granted these boons, he bound him to his own belly. Thus, Ganesha received the name "Vyalabaddhodara" (the one who has a serpent bound to his belly). The Lord placed his hand on Shesha's head for protection and joyfully showed his own cosmic form to Shesha. He filled the sky, the earth, the directions, and the intermediate directions with his sound. The earth was at his feet, the directions were his ears, and the sun was his eyes. The herbs were his hair, and the mountains were his nails. The clouds were his sweat, and the four-faced Brahma was his creation. The entire universe, including the four oceans, was in his belly. He was the one with infinite faces, infinite eyes, the self-ruler, with infinite forms, infinite power, and extreme brilliance. In his pores, thousands of universes shone. Seeing him in such a form, Shesha became as if bewildered by fear. He prayed to the Lord of obstacles to become gentle again.

Then the ten-armed God became the one mounted on a lion. The boon-giving Lord said: Such a form has not been seen even by the gods. By my grace, it has been seen by you today, O Shesha, through good fortune. I have granted you an immovable position in Patala and in Shiva. Today, I have given you the earth to carry like a flower.

Shesha said: By carrying the earth on my head, the name "Dharanidhara" (Earth-supporter) will become famous in the world, even for me. Remain stable in this region and fulfill the desires of your devotees.

Brahma said: Saying "So be it," the Lord of obstacles disappeared. Shesha also established that form with devotion. He built a beautiful golden palace adorned with many gems. He established the name "Dharanidhara" for this place. He became the bed for Hari and carried the earth like a flower. He became stable on the lotus of the navel and became the ornament of the Lord of obstacles. Thus, O Vyasa, the wonderful glory of the Lord of obstacles has been told to you. Ganesha is famous in Pravala Nagara as Dharanidhara. It was for this reason that Shesha performed the worship of the Lord.

Chapter 91 : The Creation by Kashyapa

Vyasa said: O Lord, please tell me another story of Ganesha. My mind becomes extremely eager as I listen to you, O Lord.

Brahma said: Once, during the time of dissolution, Ganesha commanded me, saying, "O Brahma, create various kinds of beings by my order." I then created seven sons from my heart. I will tell you their names: Kashyapa, Gautama, Jamadagni, Vashistha, Bharadwaja, Atri, and Vishwamitra—these seven are well-versed in all knowledge. They all asked me, "O Lord of gods, give us instructions." I instructed Kashyapa, as he was the most intelligent among them, to create the diverse world. He agreed, said "Om," and went to the forest to perform penance.

He chanted the single-syllable mantra for a thousand divine years. Pleased by this, the Lord Ganesha, the elephant-faced deity, appeared before Kashyapa. He was four-armed, lotus-eyed, adorned with a great crown, holding a noose, a goad, a garland, and a tusk in his hands. He wore beautiful armlets, was adorned with gold, gems, and pearls, and had a serpent for a belly. His radiance was like that of ten million suns, and his face was beautiful with a trunk and shining eyes. Seeing the Lord, Kashyapa danced with joy, bowed down, and worshipped him with various auspicious items. With folded hands, he praised Ganesha, saying, "My birth, my mother, my father, my penance, my knowledge, my body, and my vision are all blessed. This earth, the trees, and the fruits are blessed. Blessed is the single-syllable mantra through which I have seen the Lord of all."

Ganesha, the Supreme Soul, who is beyond the reach of the Vedas and the mind, and from whom all gods like Hara, Isha, and Agni emerge, was pleased. He said to the humble Kashyapa, "O Sage, I am pleased with your devotion and prayers. Ask for any boon you desire." Kashyapa replied, "O Lord, give me the power to create various beings, and grant me unwavering devotion to your lotus feet. Also, grant me a son who will be known as Kashyapa-nandana, and whenever I remember you, may you appear before me." Ganesha replied, "O great sage, all your desires will be fulfilled. You will have my constant devotion, and I will be near you in times of trouble. By my grace, you will create a wonderful world." Having said this, the Lord disappeared.

Kashyapa then returned to his abode. Once, suddenly, he was overcome by worldly desires and could not find peace anywhere. He forgot his daily duties, meditations, and prayers. Seeing him in such a state, his fourteen wives, including Diti, Aditi, Danu, Kadru, and Vinata, approached him. Kashyapa then fathered children through them in various homes. In due time, Diti gave birth to many demons, Aditi to gods and Gandharvas, and Danu to Danavas. Gradually, Kinnaras, Yakshas, Siddhas, Charanas, Guhyakas, wild and domestic animals, the earth, mountains, trees, oceans, rivers, creepers, grains, minerals, gems, pearls, insects, ants, snakes, and birds were all born.

Seeing this diverse creation, the wise Kashyapa rejoiced. He initiated his progeny into various mantras, such as the sixteen-syllable, eighteen-syllable, single-syllable, six-syllable, five-syllable, eight-syllable, and twelve-syllable mantras. He instructed them to perform penance until they could see Ganesha, the foundation of all and the giver of all accomplishments.
